#166. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#164. EPA reports ‘zero’ airborne lead following controlled Army detonation in Iowa

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Hours after detonating 4.4 tons of lead azide in Iowa, officials reported early test results from the surrounding areas.

Hours after detonating 4.4 tons of lead azide in Iowa, officials reported early test results from the surrounding areas.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Saying it was necessary to prevent the possible spontaneous explosion of a stockpile of material used in igniters and blasting caps, the US Army conducted a controlled detonation of over 9,700 pounds of lead azide today at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant (IAAAP) in Middletown, Iowa. Videos of the detonation showed plumes of smoke several hundred feet high drifting south before quickly dissipating.

Following the explosion, the Army confirmed that all of the barrels at the plant had been destroyed without any secondary explosions or infrastructure damage. “I believe, absolutely, that we reduced, as close to zero as it’s absolutely humanly possible, the risk to the health and safety of the people of Middletown and the extended communities throughout Iowa,” said Maj. Gen. Eric Shirley during a press conference held two hours after the blast, according to the Associated Press.

The US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) has been monitoring the area around the ammunition plant leading up to today’s event. According to a statement from the EPA following the detonation, “real-time air monitors are reporting zero detection of airborne lead throughout the region” an hour after the detonation. The data collected by EPA employees, contractors, and monitoring equipment is shared here.

The Army has used lead azide in munitions for nearly a century but began transitioning away from the material around 15 years ago because it can be difficult to transport and store. Over time, the material can degrade and crystallize and eventually become unstable enough to detonate at ambient temperatures. The Iowa ammunition plant housed 68 barrels containing about 9,700 pounds of lead azide, representing the country’s remaining stockpile. For safety reasons, the material was stored in liquid form embedded in sawdust and burlap bags while water and ethanol were added to prevent freezing. However, the decision was made to detonate the stockpile after an inspection by the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms (ATF) discovered “significant crystallization on the exterior of the barrels” last March.

Ahead of today’s detonation, the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) supported a recommended, but voluntary, temporary relocation of all the residents of Middletown and immediate surrounding areas that began yesterday. A cone-shaped evacuation zone extends about five miles southwest of the ammunition plant to account for the post-explosion dispersion and deposition path of dust. Displaced residents will be lodged until Friday, November 27th, and can return home once the all-clear has been given.

To reduce the amount of lead dust released into surrounding areas and the environment, the Army said it took several additional precautions as part of today’s detonation. Walls made of four 16,000-gallon water tanks were installed to capture lead particulate, while the aging earth-covered magazines holding the barrels were covered with tarps and lead containment screens and flooded with foam.

#162. Ukraine: Poland to set up no-fly zone near Ukraine border

Poland urged NATO allies to be vigilant at their borders after repeated Russian airspace incursions. Allies have also offered different opinions on Kyiv attacking Russian ships in Libya.

Below, you can review news from  Russia's war in Ukraine from Wednesday, November 25:

US President Donald Trump and NATO chief Mark Rutte called on Russia not to "expand the war" in Ukraine.

Rutte traveled to Florida for the meeting with Trump.

"We also discussed the importance of Russia avoiding any actions that intentionally, or unintentionally, expand the war, and the importance of obtaining a peaceful resolution to the war as rapidly as possible," they said in a joint statement sent by the White House.

US envoy Jared Kushner says Russian President Vladimir Putin has given Washington his word that he will not use nuclear weapons in the war in Ukraine, the dpa news agency reported, citing a podcast interview.

Kushner, who is US President Donald Trump's son-in-law, made the comments on the podcast "MD Meets," hosted by Axel Springer chief executive Mathias Döpfner.

"He's agreed not to use nuclear weapons, which obviously is a big thing, and he's reassured us that that's not his intention," Kushner said, adding that Putin had shown restraint in the conflict.

Western countries have repeatedly worried that Russia could turn to tactical nuclear weapons since launching the full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Meanwhile, Kushner denied that Washington is too close to Moscow, saying the US sees itself as a mediator.

"We have not put any pressure on President [Volodymyr] Zelenskyy or the Ukrainians," he said. He added that no country has given Kyiv more money than the US, and that information from US intelligence agencies still flows to Ukraine.

However, he blamed Ukraine for setting back peace efforts.

"There was the attack that the Ukrainians did on Putin's house at the end of December, which I think pushed him back," he said.

Russia claimed last December that Ukraine tried to hit Putin's Valdai residence with drones. The US Central Intelligence Agency says the residence was not the intended target.

Russia launched more ballistic missiles at the Ukrainian capital, Kyiv, late on Wednesday.

Explosions were heard in the city, and a building in an industrial area was hit, Mayor Vitali Klitschko wrote on Telegram. He said residents should remain in shelters.

The Ukrainian air force confirmed the Russian attack. Telegram channels linked to the military reported that up to eight missiles had been fired from Russia's Bryansk region near the Ukrainian border. The missiles reportedly targeted districts of Kyiv east of the Dnipro River.

The full extent of the damage was not immediately clear.

Russia has stepped up attacks on infrastructure in Kyiv and other major Ukrainian cities in recent months, seeking to disrupt electricity, heating and water supplies.

Ukraine has struggled to defend itself against ballistic missiles amid shortages of interceptor missiles for its US-made Patriot air defense systems.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In an interview with the German MD Meets Podcast, US envoy Jared Kushner warned against a downward spiral in the conflict in Ukraine, which he described as "a proxy war where Europe is funding and arming Ukraine against Russia."

Kushner, US President Donald Trump's son-in-law and chosen intermediary in negotiations with Russia's Vladimir Putin, also warned that "it won't take a lot of miscalculation for this thing to escalate massively."

After several failed rounds of talks involving his fellow envoy, Steve Witkoff, Kushner admitted that the issue of territoriy in eastern Ukraine remained a sticking point.

"What Steve and I have deciphered from having many, many hours with President Putin and from the Russian team, is that you know the territorial issue is right now a little bit of a deadlock," he said.

However, Kushner also appeared to accuse the Europeans of hypocrisy for supporting Ukraine while continuing to spend vast sums of money on Russian energy.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A German-Ukrainian woman accused of spying for Russia goes on trial in Berlin on Thursday.

She is charged with providing an employee and secret agent at the Russian Embassy in Berlin with sensitive information.

The alleged spy is accused of tipping the man off about high intelligence-value events over a period of years and helping him to secure entry.

The dpa news agency reports that the Russian agent in question was the country's deputy military attache, who has since been expelled from Germany.

The woman had long been known as a pro-Russian networker in the Berlin area, having lived in the country for several decades.

The lawsuit against her from the federal prosecutor's office based in Karslruhe also alleges that she tried to "win over individuals from the Federal Defense Ministry and the weapons industry for her purposes."

She is accused of acting out of personal conviction, rather than, say, fear or blackmail. She was arrested in January this year.

The woman is accused of making contact with the Russian espionage asset as of October 2023 at the latest. Her most pressing task was to seek out information pertaining to Germany defense policy and the weapons industry, particularly in light of and where relevant to Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine.

The Ukrainian General Staff announced that Ukrainian forces had attacked a major Russian military air base, damaging five aircraft used to launch missiles and bombs at Ukraine.

According to the General Staff, three MiG-31 jets, which are used to fire hypersonic missiles, and two Su-34 fighter-bombers were knocked out of operation "for some time."

The report stated that the Savasleyka air base, located east of Moscow in the Nizhny Novgorod region, had been hit in the latest long-range attacks aimed at crippling Moscow's war effort.

Russia did not immediately comment on the announcement.

Numerous sources inside Russian foreign policy circles on Wednesday underscored the Kremlin's desire for European diplomats to travel to Moscow and explain their intentions before they be allowed to participate in any Russia-Ukraine peace negotiations.

Russian President Vladimir Putin originally extended the invitation last month. On Wednesday, Foreign Policy Advisor Yury Ushakov said the invitation stands but that no Europeans have so far agreed to embark on the diplomatic sojourn.

Russia's Ambassador to Germany Sergei Nechayev on Wednesday told France's AFP news agency that reaction to Putin's invitation has been "restrained."

Nechayev claimed that Moscow remains, "ready to continue the dialogue and carry on with negotiations."

He also repeated the Kremlin claim that it is Europe that is the impediment to stopping the war, and that Russia — which launched and continues to execute its unprovoked years-long war of aggression in Ukraine — is the only party truly interested in peace.

Nechayev said the Kremlin doesn't see the same "honesty" from the Europeans that it does from US President Donald Trump's team — made up of his son-in-law Jared Kushner and his friend Steve Witkoff — and wants to "know precisely what their intentions are."

Russia has been accused of foot dragging as a way to prolong attacks on Ukraine. On Wednesday, Nechayev continued in that vein, saying: "Our request to Europe is this: Decide what it is you want, determine whom you will appoint as negotiators, and, most importantly, clarify what you aim to achieve."

Poland's Deputy Defense Minister Cezary Tomczyk told reporters that Warsaw will set up a no-fly zone inside its border with Ukraine and suggested other regional allies do the same in the wake of repeated Russian incursions into NATO airspace as part of Moscow's war of aggression.

"An ordinance is already being prepared by the government and should enter into force at the end of December or the beginning of January," said Tomczyk.

"We will also urge our allies — the ⁠entire ​NATO alliance — to establish such a ​principle along the eastern flank," he continued, "Poland was a pioneer ​in this regard, ‌and the Baltic states have since followed our lead."

Poland strictly limits air traffic at its border already.

On Wednesday, Tomczyk said the proposed no-fly zone will cover several dozen kilometers of territory from the border and provide added support for Poland's armed forces.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz defended his government's expenditures on Ukraine aid during a budget debate in the Bundestag on Wednesday.

Merz tackled criticism from opposition parties on the left and right head on, saying "Russia knows exactly ​what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

"Russia is escalating in the Baltic region, in Poland, on the Black Sea. Russia ​is escalating ‌in space. And Russia is now escalating massively here, in our own country, too, even at the risk of loss of life," he added.

Merz said, "anyone who doesn't see that, doesn't understand the world around them, or they are pursuing entirely different aims."

The Left Party and Alternative for Germany (AfD) have both called for an end to Ukraine aid and better relations with Moscow.

Among other things, Germany blames Russia for an explosive drone incident at Leipzig airport this summer as well as cyberattacks, sabotage and airspace incursions across the EU and NATO.

Merz said on Wednesday that by supporting Ukraine, Germany is protecting itself.   "We are learning from Ukraine," said Merz, "technologically for civilian use but of course also in defense ⁠technology."

Kyiv and Moscow both assessed damage to energy infrastructure on Wednesday morning as both sides continued to inflict damage on one another amid Russia's over four-and-a-half-year attempted invasion of neighboring Ukraine.

Russia again focused its attacks on Ukraine's energy structure in an attempt to break the will of the country's civilian population as the bitter cold of winter sets in.

Moscow's troops attacked a thermal energy power plant, shutting it down; as well as targeting a gas production facility in Poltawa, where ensuing fires also shut down operations.

Ukraine's acting energy minister said that power outages were reported in five regions as a result of the Russian attacks.

In Russia's Samara region, Ukrainian drones hit at least two oil refineries, killing one person, sparking large fires and and causing energy blackouts.

Kyiv's attacks on Russian ships in and off the coast of Libya over the past couple of days have ruffled feathers among Ukraine's allies, with France and Italy, for instance, coming down on entirely different sides of the issue.

French Foreign Minister Jean-Noel Barrot said Kyiv was justified in its actions because it has an "absolute right to self-defense." Libya on Wednesday announced that two of its soldiers had died from injuries sustained during a weekend drone attack that sank a Russian warship.

Speaking with French broadcaster TF1, Barrot said, "For nearly five years now, [Russian President] Vladimir Putin has not only been bombing Ukraine, but has also been constantly engaging in acts of aggression and harassing Europeans in an attempt to undermine our unity."

Barrot said Russia alone is to blame for the war and its expansion, and then asked, "What would we do if we were being bombarded day and night by an enemy — an invader?"

Italy's Foreign Minister Antonio Tajani on Wednesday criticized Kyiv's actions, saying, "It was a mistake to violate Libya's sovereignty" and warned against further similar activities in other regions.

Speaking with Italy's Corriere della Sera newspaper ‌on ⁠Wednesday, Tajani said, "Italy was absolutely unaware of Kyiv's intentions to strike in ​Libyan territorial waters, even inside a port."

Tajani said he had spoken with Ukraine Foreign Minister Andrii Sybihato and conveyed Italy's concern over the matter, adding that Rome would continue its staunch support for Ukraine and its sovereignty while at the same time seeking to keep the conflict from spreading.

As Russia's invasion of Ukraine creeps toward its fifth year, both sides continue to blast each other's infrastructure.

Overnight attacks targeted gas, oil and thermal energy facilities on both sides of the conflict's front lines.

While Moscow chided former UK Prime Minister Boris Johnson's calls for a contingent of Western soldiers to be stationed in Ukraine, France's foreign minister defended Kyiv's recent attacks on Russian maritime vessels in far away Libya — something Italy's foreign minister criticized.

In Berlin, Chancellor Friedrich Merz defended German support for Ukraine as opposition parties with sympathies for Moscow complain it's too expensive.

Merz called out those wanting to curtail support as either "failing to grasp" the fact that "Russia wants more than Ukraine" or "pursuing entirely different aims with regard to our country's freedom, openness and liberalism."

Lastly, Poland's defense minister on Wednesday expressed astonishment over the fact that the US has not yet condemned or even reacted to repeated Russian drone and missile incursions into Polish airspace in recent days.

#161. WhatsApp’s new scheduled messages let you choose the right time to send

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

You can schedule WhatsApp texts you might forget to send up to two weeks ahead of time.﻿

You can schedule WhatsApp texts you might forget to send up to two weeks ahead of time.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

WhatsApp’s 3 billion users are getting an option that Meta says is one of the most requested features, with the ability to schedule messages in advance for both private conversations and group chats. Most messaging platforms have had this for a very long time, but now it’s on WhatsApp too.

If you think of a text at an inconvenient time or don’t want to forget to fire off a birthday message at 4AM, you can draft it and set it to send anywhere from 10 minutes to two weeks later. The feature works with photos and videos, too.

To schedule a message, press and hold the send button and pick the date and time you want it to arrive. It will look like any other message to the person receiving it. You can see all future messages you have lined up, and delete them beforehand if you change your mind.

Even if your phone is off or out of battery, your scheduled message will still show up at the time you chose, and it remains end-to-end encrypted like all your personal messages, so no one else can read it, not even us.

Scheduled messages work with text, photos and videos, and are rolling out globally over the coming weeks. It’s one more way to show up for the people who matter to you, now at exactly the right time.

#159. The Return of Jensen Huang

#158. Jensen Huang still thinks a succession plan creates unnecessary hierarchy

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang kicked off his return to the chipmaker he co-founded by holding an all-hands meeting that covered remorse, therapy, and even the company’s code of conduct, according to accounts from multiple insiders who spoke to the media.

The topic of succession planning also came up — a particularly sticky issue seen as a key risk at Nvidia. Huang reportedly told staff that Nvidia doesn’t require succession planning because of its unique business model, CNBC reported, citing an unnamed source who was present. Surprisingly, given the events of the last two weeks, Huang argued that a succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy.

Investors and analysts saw the issue differently earlier this month, when Huang resigned as CEO following his admission that he had extramarital affairs with at least two people, including one whom the U.S. government suspects of being a Chinese spy.

Nvidia is a flat organization, unlike other large corporations that have a swath of C-suite executives and multiple layers of management. Huang sits at the top and says he has about 60 direct reports. Nvidia has just five executive officers running the show, including Huang. The other four are CFO Colette Kress, who was interim CEO for 12 days; Ajay Puri, who heads worldwide field operations; Debora Shoquist, executive vice president of operations; and Timothy Teter, executive vice president and general counsel, according to the company’s regulatory filings.

Huang has previously defended the company’s structure in numerous interviews, including one in March with podcaster Lex Fridman.

“When you’re designing a company, you should first think about what is it that you want the company to produce,” Huang said in the interview. “You know, I see a lot of companies’ organization charts, and they all look the same. Hamburger [company] organization charts, software organization charts, and car company organization charts. They all look the same. And it doesn’t make any sense to me. You know, the goal of company is to be the machinery, the mechanism, the system that produces the output. And that output is the product that we like to create.”

Huang’s comments to staff, and his previous remarks on the topic of succession, may not be enough to assuage the board or shareholders over the long term, however.

#157. The Verge’s 2026 holiday gift guide for travelers

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Globe-trotting can be a real slog, but with the right tools, hitting the open road (or air) doesn’t have to be a struggle.

Globe-trotting can be a real slog, but with the right tools, hitting the open road (or air) doesn’t have to be a struggle.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Everyone has that traveler in their life. That person who feels more at home on a 14-hour train ride than a quiet Sunday on the couch. They speak multiple languages, have a gut like a goat, and make friends wherever they go. Whether they’re your kindred spirit or the person you live vicariously through, one thing never changes: They always need new gear.

That’s what makes travelers so fun to buy for. But you don’t want to get them just anything. The key is to find that clever gift even they didn’t know they needed, and that’s where we come in. From streaming gadgets for the hotel to portable espresso makers and high-capacity chargers, these are our favorite gifts to help travelers take their next adventure to the next level.

Give Yourself A Gift With Holiday Travel Deals. Make the most of the season with limited-time holiday offers on travel and more. However you choose to celebrate, explore more ways to save and make your next journey even more rewarding. Terms apply. Ends December 15, 2026.

Every traveler needs a go-to backpack, and the Uniqlo Utility stands out for all the little things. Its minimalist, water-resistant design hides plenty of pockets on the front and sides, plus a roomy laptop sleeve and extras, including a handy key holder in the front pocket.

Few things are more frustrating on a long flight than trying to keep your phone stable between your seasoned pretzels and fellow passengers. Thankfully, MiiKare’s adjustable, inexpensive clamp can make things less stressful for your favorite frequent flyer, so they can finally watch Olympus Has Fallen in peace.

We all tend to skimp on simple items for ourselves, which is why this fancy-pants sleep mask makes such a thoughtful present. Its 3D contoured fit is designed to block all light while providing plush memory foam comfort so your loved one can get the rest they need between adventures.

They say once you go bidet, you never go back. That’s certainly harder to achieve when traveling outside Japan, though Tushy offers an effective solution with its handy, collapsible bidet. The manual contraption isn’t heated, but one portable bathroom luxury at a time.

Mechanism’s clever, durable creations are a favorite here at The Verge. The company’s foldable tray table mount is particularly brilliant, offering travelers an ergonomic way to mount devices — including larger gaming handhelds like the Steam Deck — for easy access on long flights. Just don’t forget to pick up the proper grip.

Built for jet setters, Anker’s 100W travel adapter features a durable design, a handy LCD display to track charge percentage, and compatibility in over 200 countries. It doesn’t support AC plugs, but it does have four USB inputs for all your portables, including a trio of USB-C ports.

What if you could give the gift of stress-free Wi-Fi on the go? That’s the promise of TP-Link’s dual-band Roam 7, a portable Wi-Fi 7 router that simplifies connecting to public portals so travelers can connect everything to a single device and be done with it.

For the fastidious person on your list who can’t stop polishing their camera, tablet, or other delicate piece of equipment, there’s the lightweight Magic Wrap. The foldable, plush fabric sticks to itself, with no zippers, edges, or even magnets to scuff or scratch your most precious gear.

You can’t project anything anywhere from your phone or watch (yet), but the Aurzen Zip makes that moment feel closer. Bright and durable, the foldable projector can mirror content from your phone or tablet on most flat surfaces, but fair warning: It doesn’t work with streaming services like Netflix without a $129.99 CastPlay HDMI dongle.

Whether your favorite traveler is a packing nerd or a slob who’s always losing everything (we’re not here to judge), everyone can use a little extra organization. Cocoon’s Grid-It accessories store it all, from chargers and gadgets to makeup and passports, with flexible straps to lock it all in.

Losing important documents on a trip can be disastrous. Bagsmart’s Blast Travel Pouch is a great way to keep them all safe, with multiple pockets, an easily adjustable strap, and even RFID blocking to deflect electronic thieves.

It’s time for a smarter robovac. Thankfully, there’s the Matic, which looks less like the rambling discs that pervade the space and more like an actual robot that, well, vacuums. It doesn’t get under beds, but it also doesn’t get stuck under them, and it does a great job providing truly no-fuss cleaning thanks to its superb mapping.

One charging port, two phones. That’s not a problem with Anker’s dual-headed USB-C cable, which offers either 4 or 6 feet of length to provide game-changing versatility when you need it most.

Who says you can’t travel with a big screen? The Xreal One Pro feature dual micro-OLED displays to project a virtual 171-inch screen from your device of choice. A 57-degree field of view, max 120Hz refresh rate, and built-in speakers round things out, delivering portable cinematic entertainment for one.

Apple’s AirPods Pro are the epitome of wireless earbud refinement, with fantastic noise-canceling and transparency modes, a light and airy fit, and clear sound thanks to ear tips that seal your ears without plugging them up. The latest pair continues the quest for perfection with faster processing, more features, and better ergonomics than ever.

Oura didn’t reinvent the wheel with the Ring 5, but it did reshape it into a slimmer, thinner, more wearable design that could make all the difference. Inside are the same extensive health and fitness-tracking features for charting everything from sleep to heart health, backed by long battery life.

Whether you’re white-water rafting or trekking through a damp jungle, keeping your gear dry is essential. The Sea to Summit dry bag is a slick, affordable solution with clever features, including a reflective interior and a D-ring buckle to secure it to boats and bikes. The solid warranty is just a plus.

SwitchBot’s powerful, battery-powered fan is a big upgrade over your average plug-in. As a 3D circulator, it can tilt to push air in all directions. You can also control it via an app, and it even detaches from its stand to sit on a desk or tabletop. Quiet and versatile, it’s equipped for nearly any adventure on or off the grid.

Espresso in the woods? Yes, please. The GR2’s magical mechanics make it possible, letting you turn 12 ounces of grounds into espresso without power. Its carrying case houses all the accessories needed to get that sweet shot of bean, making real coffee available virtually anywhere.

Theragun’s latest therapeutic cure for sore muscles is as packable as it is luxurious. The third-gen Mini includes clever features like a travel lock to keep it off during transport, guided routines, and USB-C charging. There’s even a Y2K edition for the ’90s kid in all of us.

For travelers hitting the road when the thermometer is rising, cooling off is essential. Nitecore’s long-lasting izzCool 10 Pro offers heat relief with a powerful fan and fillable reservoir that mists you for a refreshing respite from the sun. It even works with larger containers to double as a nighttime humidifier.

Every traveler needs quality noise-canceling for long flights, but flagship headphones are becoming increasingly spendy. Sony’s former flagship WH-1000XM4 were so good, they’ve been bestsellers through two new generations. Now the company has revamped them in the XM4C, adding minor improvements to a stone-cold classic at a nice price.

Packing light is essential for all travelers, but it’s particularly vital for those who trek long distances. This high-capacity Nitecore charger is a killer addition to any pack, with water resistance, fast charging, and a slim, lightweight design that can power your devices without weighing them down.

The Switch 2 leverages the same ideas as its predecessor, only with a larger, 7.9-inch display, a faster chip, and a bevy of new social features. However, its most important trait is that it’s the newest Nintendo console, which means access to a host of weird, beautiful, family-friendly games that only Nintendo could dream up, now on a better screen.

Every traveler’s been there. You get to the hotel or rental place, go to plug in your gear, and there’s only one outlet available. Fortunately, Tessan’s pack-friendly power strip offers all the ports, with multiple USB-C, USB-A, and AC inputs to charge everything in one place.

If you have an avid road tripper on your list, Ugreen’s four-port car charger blends slick design with serious utility. More importantly, it has a built-in retractable USB-C cable that delivers up to 60W of power and keeps things tidy.

For on-the-go creators, it’s all about capturing the moment in the highest video quality possible. Insta360’s Luna Pro steadycam offers plenty of features to get the job done right, including 4K vertical video, swift file transfers, four hours of battery life, and a detachable touchscreen that doubles as a remote.

Buying your loved one a pair of shoes is tougher than you might think, but a gift card to The Walking Company is a step in the right direction. The store offers a wide variety of ultra-comfortable kicks, boots, sandals, and even orthotics, along with a shoe finder to help them find the right fit.

Retroid has had its stumbles, but the Pocket 5 is a hit, making it a great gift for the globe-trotting gamer. With a 5.5-inch OLED screen, a light yet durable design, and tons of titles available from the Google Play Store, the portable deck provides plenty of reasons to keep playing through the runway taxi.

If you have a friend who constantly complains about their partner’s snoring habits, Soundcore’s Sleep A30 could be a gift they’ll never forget. With a comfy design, even for side sleepers, and advanced noise-canceling tech that actively adjusts to mask unwanted sounds, they’re a recipe for sweet, silent relief.

New parents need everything, all at once, and traveling always raises the stakes. The Hatch Go is the sound machine they’ll be thanking you for well into toddlerhood. Offering 10 soothing sounds from a crazy-compact frame with up to 15 hours of juice, it’s simple, effective, and essential.

How is your giftee supposed to bring you back something special when they’re already overpacked? The Matador Packable Duffel, of course. The durable, 30-liter carrier breaks down to micro size, then easily expands to hold loads of overseas paraphernalia. It’s a win-win!

#155. Trump reportedly says pardoning Jensen Huang is ‘an option’

During an interview with the New York Post on Tuesday, President Trump said that pardoning Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, who has been ensnared in a scandal involving an alleged Chinese spy, is “an option,” albeit one that he doesn’t think he will have to exercise.

“It’s always an option, I guess, but I don’t think it’ll be necessary,” the president reportedly told the outlet.

Huang, who was ousted from his own company less than two weeks ago, was reinstated Monday. He faces ongoing questions about his relationship with Alice Huang, an executive with alleged ties to Chinese intelligence who previously worked for a company that has been accused of illegally diverting Nvidia chips to China.

Huang is scheduled to testify at a public Senate Banking Committee hearing next week.

When asked about the hearing, Trump reportedly said: “I think I want him to just tell the truth, and I mean, his lawyers are going to have to advise him, but I’ve always found him to be 100% aboveboard, and you know a very smart person.”

At another point in the interview, when asked what he knew about Huang’s other extramarital affairs, Trump reportedly said: “I really didn’t get into that. I got into the business of chips, much more than the business of sex.”

TechCrunch has reached out to the White House for comment.

#154. Nigeria's upcoming election marred by rising AI disinformation

As Nigeria inches closer to the 2027 polls, the use of artificial intelligence in the electoral process has prompted questions about integrity and institutional readiness. Can Nigerians trust their democratic processes?

Nigeria has just over two months to go until its general election, with incumbent Bola Tinubu running on a ticket focused on combating raging insecurity and resolving the nation's economic crisis. He faces off against former Vice President Atiku Abubakar and senior opposition figure Peter Obi.

The country's electoral body, the Independent National Electoral Commission (INEC), recently released its preliminary voter's register, which reportedly is riddled with images believed to have been generated using artificial intelligence (AI).

Among the telltale signs of AI usage were exaggerated facial features in images and inconsistencies on the electoral registers, such as duplicates of names and even identities of people who since the previous election had died.

The development raises concerns about the body's overall preparedness ahead of the country's upcoming 2027 general election. Instead of making the upcoming vote more transparent by allowing access to its procedures, INEC has come under fire amid broader controversies about the expanding influence of AI in elections across the continent.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Joash Amupitan, the chairman of the electoral commission, said the body is now investigating the development, focusing on discrepancies potentially created by AI use.

To this end, he announced the establishment of a dedicated unit to monitor AI-related threats, detect deepfakes and strengthen the integrity of the electoral process, warning that the credibility of the upcoming election could otherwise be under attack.

However, even prior to the proliferation of AI across Africa and the globe, this would not be the first time that Nigeria has found itself in the unfortunate position of having to deal with the circulation of fabricated images, doctored audio and fake videos online. In the run-up to the 2023 election, deepfakes falsely linking candidates to terror-related activities filled social media platforms.

These are often designed to promote certain candidates or to discredit opponents in a bid to influence public perception, which now with the use of AI could create disinformation campaigns of new proportions.

Lois Ugbede, the assistant editor at the factchecking platform DUBAWA, told DW that "based on our monitoring so far, AI-driven disinformation is serious and is becoming commonplace."

"This could influence political discourse and voter behavior ahead of 2027 because we are already seeing parties using inflated crowds to show that they have a large followership or support," she added.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

At the same time, rather than influencing the vote in favor of any particular candidate, "the deeper danger is that people may begin to doubt authentic information simply because they know convincing AI-generated fakes are possible," said Akintunde Babatunde, executive director of the Abuja-based Centre for Journalism, Innovation and Development.

"We often focus on the risk of people believing an AI-generated falsehood, but there is another problem where genuine evidence can be dismissed as AI-generated," he noted, citing instances of a lawmaker who recently made inflammatory remarks during a campaign rally — but then claimed the clips were manipulated.

Chibuike Mgbeahuruike, an election consultant at the Centre for Democracy and Development, told DW that apart from the spread of synthetic content online, AI intrusion into the electoral commission's portal could also "undermine its institutional credibility, especially with the low level of confidence that we are already witnessing."

He added, however, that AI itself is not inherently a threat to electoral integrity. The real threat lies in "the absence of effective accountability for its deployment," as innovations in tech increasingly appear to infiltrate governments and their democratic processes.

"Voters must have confidence … that the technology is also transparent, accountable and independently scrutinized, and ultimately subject to human and institutional oversight," said Mgbeahuruike.

Mallam Jibrin Baba Ndace, the director-general of state-owned broadcaster Voice of Nigeria, agrees, urging journalists at a recent conference also to not fall into the trap of believing reports, which later could turn out to be false due to AI manipulation.

He stressed the importance of protection measures and due diligence in news media, with the growing inability of public institutions like INEC to provide impartial information due to the use and influence of AI and other election-related tech.

Beyond AI, Nigeria's electoral processes have undergone various technological shifts over the years in hopes of improving transparency while increasing efficiency.

Electoral authorities first deployed electronic assistance in 2011, when Direct Data Capture Machines were introduced; the devices collect voters' biometrics by scanning fingerprints or capturing facial recognition data with webcams to stop voters from submitting multiple ballots.

Ten years later, a system called the Bimodal Voter Accreditation System (BVAS) was introduced as the new standard, designed to combine fingerprints with facial recognition. However, during the 2023 general election, this voting process was marred by system failures, as poor cell signals delayed these voter accreditation procedures.

However, with INEC tallying more than 103 million Nigerians registered to participate in the coming poll, disinformation, fake news and technological challenges amount to only a few of several concerns pertaining to Nigeria's democracy.

Voter apathy has become a particular concern, especially among the country's youth. The 2023 election recorded the lowest voter turnout since the country's return to democratic rule in 1999, with merely 27% of registered voters going to the polls.

#153. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#151. Google’s patent for custom AI video feeds paints a bleak picture of entertainment’s future

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Imagine an endless feed of AI slop in which you are the main character of every story.﻿

Imagine an endless feed of AI slop in which you are the main character of every story.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Last year, Google filed a patent for a system called My Channel that’s designed to create feeds filled with personalized, AI-generated videos and advertisements based on information the company knows about its users. The patent has been rejected, but unfortunately, Google might appeal or resubmit an amended version, and we could see this monstrosity come to market.

First spotted by Patentlyze, the patent — which was filed last May and published last week — describes how My Channel would use a person’s demographic data and viewing history to create its own prompts that would then be fed into an LLM to help generate AI videos tailored to that person’s specific interests. In addition to factoring in what kinds of videos a user tends to watch, the system would capture contextual data from their device like IP addresses, GPS locations, and specific details about the user’s age. My Channel could also base its prompts on current news like sports scores and things happening on social media, and all of the video generation would take place on Google’s servers.

According to the patent, My Channel would be able to churn out entire movies of various genres. If a user watches a lot of videos about Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey, the system might serve up something like Ash Koosha’s Odysseus: The Fall. The patent details how My Channel might be able to connect to other streaming services — which would give it even more information about users and, presumably, access to useful training data for video generation. It all seems like a convoluted way to whip up AI knockoffs of professionally produced art, but what’s most unsettling about the patent are its mentions of My Channel creating digital avatars of users and turning them into “the protagonist or lead character” in the content it pumps out.

Setting aside the fact that My Channel would use photos, videos, and audio clips of people to create these avatars, the system sounds like it is designed to generate a form of “entertainment” that is deeply at odds with what makes engaging with art a worthwhile, enjoyable experience. It can be fun to metaphorically project one’s self into fictional narratives to understand how characters feel, but what Google is proposing is giving people access to an endless supply of bespoke content that doesn’t at all encourage them to use their imaginations to empathize with or relate to the characters. The patent makes it seem like Google thinks that viewers would be entertained enough just by seeing themselves onscreen. That’s bleak in and of itself because My Channel sounds very much like an unholy cross between JibJab and the panoptic elements of Google.

My Channel feels like an even more depressing idea when you consider how the service would make it that much easier for its users to avoid broadening their horizons by being exposed to new things that exist outside their comfort zones. If Google were able to strike the right licensing deals with other streaming services, My Channel users could presumably gorge themselves on artless AI regurgitations of preexisting content. That might keep some people glued to the service — a goal that every streaming platform strives for — but it would also discourage them from engaging with entertainment that isn’t expressly crafted to appeal to their assumed tastes.

When people refer to AI-generated videos as “slop,” they’re talking about the fact that generative models are incapable of generating art with the same kind of creative intention that makes human-made stories compelling. Video generation models can mimic visual elements of the data (read: movies and series) they’re trained on, but they cannot truly think or feel in the way that directors, writers, and actors can. This reality is why it has been so baffling to see AI-boosting entertainment companies, streamers, and studios insisting that the technology will fundamentally revolutionize the industry for the better. It feels much more likely that gen AI is going to keep flooding the internet with garbage no one wants to watch.

#149. Yale’s Sonnenfeld: Nvidia is a ‘detriment’ to US industrial might — not a champion

Investors cheered Jensen Huang’s return as Nvidia’s CEO on Monday, but renowned business academic Jeffrey Sonnenfeld of Yale thinks they’re celebrating a mistake. He sees a company that is largely responsible for putting sophisticated technology in the hands of the country’s adversaries, at a moment when the risks couldn’t be higher. Far from a champion of American industry, he says, Nvidia is “a detriment to U.S. global industrial might.”

As evidence, Sonnenfeld points to research suggesting Nvidia’s most advanced chips have reached China despite U.S. export controls and to sinking shares of Anthropic, which he links to the realization that China’s seemingly miraculous open source AI models are, in a sense, copies of top U.S. labs’ work, even as Nvidia has insisted that smuggling is a “non-factor.”

Indeed, Huang’s return was greeted with just as much enthusiasm in Asia, The Washington Post reported. “The king is back!” read one headline from China’s state-affiliated Star Market Daily.

The reinstatement itself followed heavy pressure from Washington. Nvidia’s chairman had effectively ruled out Huang’s return just last week. Then, in a torrent of private weekend calls led by Vice President JD Vance, top administration officials leaned on the board to welcome Huang back, with President Trump calling the board directly to vouch for him. “I made the deal,” Trump told CNBC on Monday.

Sonnenfeld says the directors who went along, and who returned Huang to the board even as it investigates whether foreign intelligence efforts targeted him, now face a “historic level of individual liability.”

The legal deluge has already begun. As we noted a couple of weeks ago, a potential class-action lawsuit was filed almost immediately. Days later, a shareholder derivative suit followed. Filed on Nvidia’s behalf against the directors themselves, the suit alleges the board ignored red flags about Huang’s relationship with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang.

The class action is very much aimed at making Nvidia and Huang compensate investors for their losses, while the derivative suit puts the directors’ own behavior on trial, with any recovery going to Nvidia. Three more suits followed on November 18, the day after Nvidia’s most recent earnings call; all five were filed in Northern California, where Nvidia is based.

Huang and Nvidia are also juggling a Justice Department antitrust investigation, a reported SEC probe into Nvidia’s accounting, a possible FBI review, a California subpoena, an export-control case that landed partner Megaspeed on the U.S. trade blacklist on Monday, and congressional inquiries, including a Senate Banking hearing next week at which Huang has agreed to testify.

Here’s more of what Sonnenfeld had to say, in a Q&A that’s been edited for length and clarity.

So much has happened since we last talked. Huang was ousted, he’s come back. Do you think the board’s reliance on government assurances here is defensible or an abdication of its own duties?

They failed to rely on experts. That’s one of the reasons this board is going to be sued and lose, and these directors are likely to face a historic level of individual liability. They have a very inadequate directors and officers liability policy. They have also just failed in their diligence. They hired the Wachtell [law] firm. The Wachtell firm barely got off the blocks to do an investigation. And then they have already reversed themselves, so they didn’t use the expertise needed.

It’s wild that they also put Huang right back on the board of directors.

The board showed, in addition to incompetence and collaboration, just cowardice in their capitulation because Trump has nothing to wave over them. If this was SpaceX, where 46% of its business comes from the government [you could maybe see the case for Trump’s involvement]. For Nvidia, it’s not even 1% of its business that comes from the U.S. government, so [the board] didn’t have that threat. [Trump] can call off [federal] investigations, but he can’t call off the shareholder litigation. Some are already filed, and the estimates are there’ll probably be 30 of them, and they’re going to be extremely costly.

And this is not, by the way, just a blue or red state issue. We already have 14 GOP senators who’ve already come out against this, and very prominent conservatives, like Pete Ricketts and Josh Hawley and Tom Cotton and John Cornyn. Those are some issues that may help explain why Nvidia’s stock is tumbling today.Might the board say, “We saw the classified intelligence findings, and they assure us Jensen will be absolved of wrongdoing”?

They haven’t seen those findings, and we also haven’t seen what will be the result of the Senate Intelligence Committee, the Senate Special Committee on Investigations, the Senate Banking Committee, and all their House counterparts. The board hasn’t seen any of that, and even if they saw it, which they have not yet, that still wouldn’t matter.

Do you think the Commerce Department will continue to tighten its chip export controls to keep China from accessing so much compute or walk back some of those recent moves?

We already see that Megaspeed has finally been put on the sanctions list, as it should have been before. And we have documentation now that Nvidia, rather than a champion of U.S. global industrial might, is actually a detriment to U.S. global industrial might, because their chips have now turned out to be in the hands and in the systems of not just the Chinese military, as they got channeled to Northwestern Polytechnical University, which is a military institution — this is going into weapons that will, of course, be funneled to Iran and Russia, and they didn’t have access to that a year ago. [Editor’s note: China has long denied supplying weapons to either country. Nvidia chips have been found in Russian drones, however, and the U.S. ambassador to China said in September that President Trump had discussed selling arms to China, an account Trump has denied.]

The open source models that people have celebrated and marveled at — that China somehow has this ingenuity to be creating their own chips, so that Kimi and DeepSeek and these other celebrated open source models were producing a miracle of Anthropic-like results — turns out we know why. They are pirating it. They are “distilling it” from Anthropic using high-end Nvidia chips.

Nvidia has denied reports that its most advanced chips have been smuggled into China.

There’s an Israeli company, Lava, that just three weeks ago showed [dozens of] Blackwell chips are in China. We’re seeing, with the return of Jensen Huang, Anthropic plummet. Why would that be? Of course, this is because it’s hurting OpenAI. It’s hurting Grok. It’s hurting Perplexity. It’s hurting Gemini. And you’ve seen none of these tech titans celebrate this. We have the misogynist tech bros holding locker-room-type rallies about Jensen Huang’s return, but nobody else has spoken out in favor of this. [You’re not hearing from] the CEOs of Microsoft or IBM or J.P. Morgan or Walmart or General Motors or Caterpillar. Nobody thinks that this is a good move.

You don’t think Nvidia needed Huang back?

He was not the guy designing the chips. It’s important to point out that that’s a guy named Chris Malachowsky, a co-founder who’s still there who designed these GPUs and has a whole team of next-generation people like Jonah Alben doing the chip design. As for the great software that has leveraged these chips, that’s also not Jensen Huang. That’s a guy named Ian Buck, who’s been very much celebrated in the last two weeks. He’s the one who designed the CUDA software that leverages the chips. Those people weren’t going anywhere, and wherever Jensen Huang is has nothing to do with them.

Some are trying to make Huang’s forced resignation about the extramarital affairs to which he has admitted, calling it HR policy gone wild.

A CEO who doesn’t stand by his own code of conduct makes it completely unenforceable.

Wired quoted an Nvidia employee who said they’d never take an HR training course again if Huang was reinstated.

This thing is a sexual affair with a subordinate — whether it’s coercion, or even if it truly was somehow consensual, it’s not fair. Every decision on resource allocation, on staffing, on product decisions, is not objective. It’s either favoritism, or, if the relationship fails, retribution. All the other people on that team rise and fall with a sexual relationship; it’s something they have no control over.

#148. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#146. Amazon loses its latest attempt to get out of handling more recall notices

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

A court ruled in favor of the US CPSC’s argument that Amazon is responsible for recalled products that other companies sell on its platforms.

A court ruled in favor of the US CPSC’s argument that Amazon is responsible for recalled products that other companies sell on its platforms.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Amazon is on the hook to recall more than 400,000 hazardous products after losing its legal battle against the US Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC). The US District Court for the District of Maryland ruled in favor of the CPSC, which argued that Amazon is a distributor of goods, not a logistics provider, making it responsible for carrying out recalls of third-party products.

Amazon sued the CPSC in March 2025 after the agency ordered Amazon to recall over 400,000 “Fulfilled by Amazon” products that pose a “substantial product hazard,” including faulty carbon monoxide detectors, hair dryers without electrocution protection, and children’s sleepwear that doesn’t meet federal flammability standards.

As a result, Amazon will be required to implement the following measures, initially outlined in the CPSC’s January 2025 order:

On the day CPSC publishes the recall releases, Amazon must notify the public by posting the recall releases to Amazon.com; notify original purchasers directly via email and by providing information regarding the recalls on each purchaser’s “Your Orders” page.

Amazon must provide one round of notice of the recalls to Fulfilled by Amazon participants that sold the subject products.

Incentive to Remove Products from Commerce:

Amazon must issue a full refund in the amount of the purchase price to any consumer who submits proof of destruction or disposal of a subject product.

Amazon must maintain the recall releases on the “Recalls and Product Safety Alerts” webpage on amazon.com for a minimum of five years.

Amazon must submit monthly progress reports for five years and maintain records of its actions to comply with the Order for the same period.

“We have a longstanding commitment to removing unsafe and recalled products from our store and notifying customers, backed by an industry-leading recalls process,” Amazon spokesperson Sammy Brill says in an emailed statement to The Verge. “We’re reviewing our options following the ruling and look forward to continuing to work with the CPSC to put customer safety first.”

Update, November 25th: Added Amazon’s statement.
