#144. The questions Jensen Huang and Nvidia will still have to answer about Megaspeed

Jensen Huang’s return as CEO to Nvidia hasn’t eased the scrutiny the company faces. The chipmaker still needs to address multiple inquiries into allegations that its advanced GPUs were smuggled into China by a customer, the Singapore-based data center operator Megaspeed, in violation of U.S. export rules designed to slow China’s development of frontier AI models.

The central question for investigators is what Nvidia knew about where those chips ended up and whether Huang’s affair with a Megaspeed executive gave the company reason to doubt Megaspeed’s assurances. Huang’s return doesn’t settle that. U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche has suggested Nvidia isn’t a target of an investigation, but California’s attorney general and Congress are pressing ahead, and the Securities and Exchange Commission is reportedly investigating, too.

The U.S. government opened an investigation into Megaspeed in 2024 and requested information from Nvidia about its billions of dollars in sales to the company, concerned that some of the chips Megaspeed bought for data center projects in Malaysia were in fact being diverted to China. Singapore also began investigating similar allegations in 2025. Megaspeed has denied any wrongdoing.

Diversion of this kind would be a fairly routine issue for a company like Nvidia, whose in-demand chips are the targets of smugglers and resellers. When it comes to compliance and liability for export-control violations, Nvidia and other chip sellers need assurances that the end users of their chips are obeying the rules. However, they also cannot ignore other information that might call the reliability of those assurances into question, export-control attorneys told TechCrunch.

And that’s why Jensen Huang’s affair with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang was such a problem for Nvidia, likely even more so than her alleged role as a source for Chinese intelligence agencies. If Nvidia personnel, from lower-level employees up to the CEO, had reason to believe that statements from Megaspeed weren’t reliable, the attorneys said, that would increase the company’s legal risk.

“Government investigators likely would want to know, notwithstanding whatever formal assurances were exchanged between the parties, what anyone at Nvidia actually knew about the end use and end users of the chips,” Dan Fisher-Owens, an attorney at the firm Berliner Corcoran & Rowe, told TechCrunch last week.

The concern Nvidia’s board faced when it decided to oust Huang is that he knew more about what Megaspeed was doing with Nvidia’s products than was reflected in any of the compliance statements. The Wall Street Journal reported that Alice Huang and her husband, Kang Wei, were part of a network of businesses that funneled chips to a company in Shanghai. Kang also reportedly introduced his wife to Jensen Huang, whom he had known for a decade.

On Monday, the U.S. government added Megaspeed and its affiliates to a list of companies restricted from U.S. trade due to national security concerns. The U.S. also issued new rules last week barring large data centers that purchase U.S. chips from serving customers in China and other nations seen as problematic by the U.S. government. Nvidia, meanwhile, said it would stop selling licensed chips to China and laid out plans to hire its first compliance officer and create a new board committee focused on export controls.

Nvidia’s board said it reinstated Huang on Monday after receiving “input from senior levels of the United States government” that left it confident his return posed no national security risk. President Donald Trump later said he called into the meeting to give them that assurance.

In a news conference Tuesday, Blanche said that Nvidia was “a good corporate citizen” and suggested it is not the target of an investigation, but Blanche declined to answer questions about the government’s investigation into Megaspeed, which the Justice Department said was ongoing as recently as last week.

“When you say the export investigation is still ongoing, I don’t know what you’re talking about,” Blanche said.

Other authorities may be more demanding. Nvidia faces investigations by California Attorney General Rob Bonta and, reportedly, the SEC, both tied to its corporate disclosures. While many details of the investigation still aren’t known, short sellers complained about the company’s decision to stop breaking out its exports to Singapore in financial filings after journalists raised concerns about chip diversion.

Members of Congress from both parties say they want answers from Nvidia, with many citing alarm about China and some questioning the White House’s role in Huang’s return. Three committees in the House of Representatives will privately interview Huang and Nvidia’s top lawyer, Tim Teter, next week. Teter reportedly intervened in the Department of Commerce’s investigation of Megaspeed last year.

“We remain committed to getting answers on the mass smuggling of Nvidia products, their diversion for Chinese military purposes, and their key role in advancing our foremost adversary’s AI capabilities,” the Republican chairs of House committees focused on foreign affairs, intelligence, and China said in a joint statement.

Rep. Brian Mast, the chair of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, has also demanded that the Department of Commerce share extensive data regarding its investigation into chip smuggling, including by Megaspeed and its affiliates.

The Senate Banking Committee will also question Huang in a public hearing on December 3.

“Mr. Huang, Nvidia executives, and the company’s board still owe Congress answers about the company’s compliance with U.S. export controls,” Senator Elizabeth Warren, the ranking Democratic member on the committee, said in a statement. “The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what ‘input’ was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement.”

#143. EU must set out a new timeline for cage-free farming, court rules

European General Court ruled that the European Commission has failed to set out a timeline after pledging to end caged farming by the end of 2023.

A European Union (EU) court told the bloc on Wednesday to draw a new timeline for plans to ban cages for farm animals.

Judges at the European General Court ruled that the EU has failed to follow through on a pledge to ban the practice.

In 2021, the European Commission set out the goal to pass a legislation by the end of 2023 to stop farmers from cooping hens, rabbits, sows and other animals in cages. The move came after 1,4 million people signed a petition.

But it failed to follow up on the pledge. This resulted in the campaigners requesting a new timeline and eventually led to a court case when that was also not happening.

The Luxembourg-based General Court, the European Union's second-highest, on Wednesday ruled in favor of the animal rights campaigners.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"The Commission is under an obligation — which is not limited in time — to set out a timeline for all the measures which it intends to take in response to such an initiative," the court said.

According to the "European citizens' initiative" system, the Commission has to receive the petitions that gather over one million signatures across at least seven bloc countries. However, it is not obligated to meet the demands.

The court noted that if the Commission decides to take action, it has to keep the public and the European Parliament posted on a timeline in accordance with the principle of "good administration."

The Commission said it was analyzing the ruling before making a decision. It can still appeal the ruling.

It has previously stated an intention to put forward a proposal for the phased elimination of cage systems for chickens by the end of this year, as well as a separate proposal to transition from farrowing crates to pen-based systems for pigs by 2027.

According to the Albert Schweitzer Foundation, no timetable has been set for calves, rabbits, ducks, geese and quail.

In the EU, about 143 million egg-laying chickens are kept in cages while the majority of female pigs spend at least part of their lives caged.

The use of cage systems in agricultural production accredited as "organic" is already prohibited in the EU.

#140. Flipkart CEO Kalyan Krishnamurthy to step down next week amid IPO uncertainty

Kalyan Krishnamurthy (pictured above), who steered Flipkart through Walmart’s acquisition in 2018 and helped build it into one of India’s biggest e-commerce companies, is stepping down as chief executive next week after more than 11 years with the company.

On Wednesday, Flipkart said it would announce Krishnamurthy’s successor in the coming weeks, and Chief Product and Technology Officer Balaji Thiagarajan will lead the company until a replacement is found.

A Walmart spokesperson told TechCrunch that Krishnamurthy’s last day at Flipkart will be December 4.

Krishnamurthy joined Flipkart in 2015 from Tiger Global, one of the company’s early investors, and became its CEO in 2017. He remained at the helm after Walmart acquired a controlling stake in the company for $16 billion in 2018.

Under Krishnamurthy, Flipkart said its business grew more than five-fold. The company has more than 500 million registered users and over 1.4 million sellers, including those on Shopsy, its low-cost shopping platform. The company’s marketplace offers over 150 million products across more than 80 categories.

Flipkart has of late moved into newer segments, including quick-commerce through Flipkart Minutes and, more recently, food delivery through a venture called Eat-in by Minutes. The expansion is targeted at Indian consumers who are increasingly turning to quick-delivery services for everything from groceries to electronics.

Thiagarajan joined Flipkart in September last year to lead its product and technology teams. Before that, he had founded Kasu.ai, a Bay Area startup working on AI assistants for corporate finance. He also previously worked at Google, Microsoft, and Uber in senior engineering positions, per his LinkedIn profile.

Krishnamurthy’s exit comes amid uncertainty around Flipkart’s IPO. In March, the company moved its headquarters from Singapore to India as part of its preparations to go public, but in May, Walmart reportedly told Flipkart to delay its IPO and focus on becoming profitable first.

Walmart declined to comment beyond its announcement when asked what prompted Krishnamurthy’s departure, and whether the leadership change would affect Flipkart’s IPO plans. Flipkart did not respond to a request for comment.

Founded in 2007 by Sachin and Binny Bansal as an online bookstore, much like its bigger rival Amazon, Flipkart eventually expanded into other categories. Under Krishnamurthy, it has grown into India’s largest online retailer by gross merchandise value, ahead of Amazon.

“Both Flipkart and Walmart are better because of Kalyan. We are grateful for his leadership, the governance he instilled and his many contributions to the company,” Chris Nicholas, president and CEO, Walmart International, said in a statement.

#137. US Army detonates unstable lead-based explosives in Iowa

The US Army says a controlled blast is the safest way to deal with explosive chemicals that began crystallizing in storage. US President Donald Trump said "mitigation efforts have far exceeded expectations."

The United States Army detonated nearly 5 tons of aging lead azide at an Iowa ammunition plant on Wednesday, saying the explosive chemical has become too unstable to store.

The controlled blast occurred a little after 9 a.m. local time (1500 GMT) at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown, and will destroy the country's entire stockpile of the chemical. Plumes of smoke were seen on camera in video footage after the controlled blast in Middletown.

The US Army has said residents of a small town and other nearby areas should leave "out of an abundance of caution."

"We don't believe that the public should be in a panic," said acting Secretary of the Army Adam Telle. "Our goal is to keep as much of it as we can here on our Iowa Army Ammunition plant to prevent it from traveling."

"Early reports from Iowa are that the Army's mitigation efforts have far exceeded expectations," US President Donald Trump said on his Truth Social platform.

The material was moved to Iowa for storage from bases around the country in 2009.

But a March inspection found that it had partly crystallized, making it sensitive to friction and cold and potentially capable of triggering a spontaneous explosion.

"Inaction is no longer an option," said Major General Eric Shirley, who leads the operation.

Rob Cekada, director of the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives, told reporters: "It's not a matter of if, it's a matter of when, and that would not be a great place for us to be in."

To limit toxic lead dust, the Army has built barriers around the bunkers holding the material and will use foam and water to trap particles.

Reuters reported that Army projections show dust could settle up to 40 miles (64 km) southwest, reaching northern Missouri.

Officials expect the wind to carry the plume away from Burlington, a city of nearly 25,000 people.

While residents within 5 miles (8 kilometers) were encouraged to evacuate, the Federal Emergency Management Agency has also advised people 5 to 20 miles away to stay indoors with windows closed.

The date of the detonation changed three times after public backlash over its impact on the environment and public health. A federal appeals court cleared the plan on Monday after environmental groups sued.

Farmer Jill Holler, who organized a protest, told Reuters: "I'm not opposed if this is the only safe option."

"What I'm saying is that it's been so mishandled that people are confused and don't trust anything they're saying," she added.

#135. Amazon and Meta reportedly in talks to lift blockade on AI agent Muse

Amazon may be having a change of heart about Meta’s AI agent, Muse. The two tech giants are reportedly discussing a potential deal that would allow Meta’s popular AI agent to connect directly to Amazon’s store.

The talks would mark a significant reversal from just two months ago, when Muse users discovered they were unable to purchase products on Amazon and instead received an error message when attempting to check out.

According to a Wednesday report by Fortune, citing people familiar with the discussions, Amazon and Meta have held multiple rounds of talks about the matter. Two key issues remain under negotiation: how search results would be presented and how advertising would be handled under a potential agreement.

One of Amazon’s biggest concerns is how third-party shopping agents like Muse could affect its advertising business. In October, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy argued that few customers were making purchases through outside shopping agents.

Amazon may also have been waiting for Muse to mature before agreeing to integrate it with its marketplace. If Muse places the wrong order or creates a problem with a purchase, Amazon could ultimately be left dealing with frustrated customers and unhappy sellers.

Fortune reported that executives are concerned that third-party shopping agents could weaken Amazon’s direct relationship with consumers, according to people familiar with the matter.

Any potential agreement between Amazon and Meta would reportedly require approval from both companies’ CEOs, Fortune also reported.

Meta and Amazon have not yet responded to TechCrunch’s requests for comment.

#134. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#132. Todoist confirms 6% of customers’ details were stolen in data breach

Task management app Todoist said on Tuesday that a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates. The company said around 6% of its customers were affected and blamed stolen credentials for the breach.

Todoist said the attacker used stolen credentials to access the company’s version control system and source code between November 22 and 23. The hacker then changed employee access controls and reached the app’s cloud infrastructure via an employee’s work account.

The hacker downloaded credentials of early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method, but didn’t change any source code, the company said. Todoist said it has blocked the attack path and reset passwords as a mitigation step, as well as disabled the Todoist for Outlook email add-on.

For a small number of people, about 0.001% of its userbase, the hacker also searched through tasks, comments, and attachment names to look for credentials for other services, like API keys and access tokens.

The hacker also pulled Todoist API tokens and Slack integration tokens, and looked for emails, addresses, and attachment file names through the Todoist for Outlook extension if it was connected to accounts and had permission to read emails.

The company said it has sent a notice to those affected and asked them to reset their credentials and change their login details in other places with the same password. The company is asking users to also update API tokens, reconnect integrations, and revoke or replace any access keys, recovery phrases, or credentials they had stored in Todoist comments.

Todoist said it has also reset its internal access structure and how remote command execution works in its cloud environment to prevent such incidents in the future.

Todoist’s founder and CEO Amir Salihefendić apologized to users about the incident on X, and said the company will take a closer look at why it happened.

The to-do app’s data breach is the latest in a burgeoning list of data breaches in which hackers use stolen credentials to access data and systems at enterprises. So far this year, we’ve seen several companies fall victim to this form of attack, including Uber and Klue.

#131. €561 Billion and Still Not Enough: Germany's 2027 Budget Exposes a Coalition on the Brink

The budget for 2027 has reached new highs, and pension funds, defense spending and a weak economy are increasing the pressure. At the same time, tensions are mounting within the government.

At first glance, everything seems to be business as usual. Lawmakers in the Bundestag, the lower house of parliament, are holding four days of final debates on how much money the federal government may spend in the coming year, and on what. These follow a familiar pattern: The government pats itself on the back, while the opposition voices criticism. But anyone listening more closely will notice that there are doubts even in the ranks of the coalition parties, the conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU), their Bavarian allies, the Christian Social Union (CSU), and the center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD).

"As a country, we are in a difficult situation geopolitically, technologically, and demographically, and next year's budget is an accurate reflection of that," Chancellor Friedrich Merz (CDU) acknowledged on Wednesday in the Bundestag.

"If we don't change course, in a few years we'll find ourselves in a situation where all federal revenue will have to be spent on social programs, interest payments, and defense," he reflected on Wednesday during a four-hour debate focused on government policy as a whole, rather than the individual budgets of the ministries.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

This government has now been in office for about 20 months. Back in early 2025, expectations were enormous. Merz had promised to bring the budget back in line with the debt brake enshrined in the constitution, which stipulates that the government can spend only slightly more than it collects (with exceptions now made for defense and security).

Yet for 2027, spending — at just under €561 billion ($614 billion) — will be higher than ever before, and tax revenues are nowhere near sufficient to cover this, meaning Germany is looking at record borrowing for the year totaling €210 billion.

The largest opposition party, the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) calculated that the government will incur €401,000 in new debt every minute next year. In the Bundestag, AfD co-leader Tino Chrupalla called it a "debt orgy" that will paralyze the country. Merz will "go down in history as the chancellor who led Germany to bankruptcy," he exclaimed.

Germany's budget deficit has only ever been higher in the COVID-19 pandemic. The current pressures are obvious: The economy is struggling, the population aging, and Russia's war against Ukraine is about to enter its sixth year.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The defense budget is now by far the highest in the history of the Federal Republic, with €109 billion allocated for 2027, an increase of €27 billion on last year. Added to this is the multi-year, debt-financed special military fund, intended to modernize the armed forces, bringing the total defense budget to €139 billion.

The AfD and the socialist Left Party have sharply criticized this. Left Party parliamentary leader Heidi Reichinnek accused the government of pursuing "militarization and boundless rearmament on the one hand, and brutal social cuts and an orgy of budget cuts on the other."

The AfD's Chrupalla said that the temporary reduction in the electricity tax, pushed through at the last minute, would save the average family a mere €100 a year, while Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy "walks out of the chancellery here in Berlin with a check for €200 million after just one afternoon."

"Whose chancellor are you, Mr. Merz?" he asked.

The opposition Greens did not agree. While they also criticized the budget as "unserious," Green Party Bundestag member Britta Haßelmann described the AfD's stance on Ukraine as shameful. She rejected the idea that money was being wasted on Ukraine, saying that supporting Ukraine bolstered security for Germany and Europe.

The chancellor shares this view. "You can't say it often enough, either to the far right or the far left: Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than just Ukraine," Merz said.

He added that Russia is escalating tensions in the Baltic region, in Poland, in the Black Sea, and in space; and that Moscow is also seeking escalation in Germany, even at the cost of lives. "That is why we must show Russia its limits," he said.

But it is becoming increasingly difficult for the government to justify the high spending on Ukraine. Germany's pension and health care costs are only getting bigger: By far the largest single budget item is that of the Ministry of Labor and Social Affairs, which is rising to over €200 billion, even with cuts to unemployment benefits.

Work is currently underway on a reform of the pension system — something that many see as urgent, as the workforce born in the 1960s, when more than a million children were born in Germany every year, is on brink of retiring. Birth rates nearly halved by the mid-1970s and declined even further afterward. For now, so-called "boomers" still make up one-third of the current workforce — but soon they will be drawing pensions.

Even now, Germany's pensions cannot be financed solely with contributions on wages and salaries. In 2025, just over €420 billion in pensions were paid out, with the government contributing €127 billion of that amount. By 2027, the government aims to save €3 billion on this figure, which is likely to further increase pension contributions.

The coalition parties are stuck in a row over how to reform the pension system. Chancellor Merz insisted in the Bundestag that the coalition was solving the country's fundamental problems, but he warned that in future, tax revenues "will no longer be enough to afford everything we have become all too accustomed to," he said. "All of us here will have to give up some of the ideas we cherish."

The SPD, on the other hand, is counting on increasing federal revenue. Its parliamentary leader Matthias Miersch emphasized in the Bundestag that large fortunes and inheritances must contribute more to "public costs."

That, he said, must also apply to large companies. Currently, heirs to family-owned businesses often pay significantly less in taxes than heirs to real estate or financial assets, because they can be exempted from inheritance tax if they continue to run the business. This is intended to protect them from having to pay taxes in the event of a breakup or sale of the company.

According to Miersch, the government must now think about taxing the wealthy more. "For us, this issue is by no means off the table; on the contrary, we are putting it on the agenda," he said. The government's revenue situation is "an issue we need to discuss."

All in all, the outlook for 2027 is strained — and not just financially. The political differences between the CDU/CSU and the SPD are likely to make working together even more difficult. Conflict is inevitable, especially with several state elections scheduled for the spring.

The chancellor knows this and tried to appear confident that better collaboration was possible. "We must seek compromise in our joint decision-making, represented by these — admittedly quite different — coalition partners," he said.

This article was originally written in German.

#130. A renovation was meant to improve these Hong Kong homes - it ended in a blaze that killed 168

The fire was one of Hong Kong's deadliest in decades

It was a sunny Wednesday afternoon on 26 November last year, and Mrs Lau was attending a dance class at an activity centre close to her home.

At around 14:45 she heard the faint sounds of fire engine sirens - but didn't think much of it.

It was only a while later, when she started receiving calls from a friend, that she found out that Wang Fuk Court - the eight-block apartment complex she had called home for the past 40 years - was ablaze. And her husband - a wheelchair user - was trapped inside their home.

"I grabbed my things quickly and ran down without even putting my shoes on properly," Mrs Lau, who wanted to be identified only by her surname, told the BBC.

When she got there, the building had been cordoned off by police.

She called her 74-year-old husband but he told her not to worry - he hadn't heard an alarm so it was probably a small fire, he said.

Mrs Lau could only watch helplessly as the blaze tore through Wang Fuk Court at a peak speed of about 10m (33ft) per second - an "unprecedented" rate, a final report released by an independent committee in relation to the deadly blaze that killed 168 people found.

It was a tragedy that was entirely preventable but happened as a result of a series of human errors, fire safety failures, alleged fraud and suspected corruption, said the report.

Lau Man Kwong took care of his grandson before his death (blurred at source)

"It was ferociousâ¦ I saw the fire jump [from one building to another] - then a huge fireball burst. It was truly hell on Earth. How could anyone possibly escape?" said Mrs Lau.

She tried calling her husband again. This time, there was no answer.

The body of Lau Man Kwong was eventually found by rescuers - he had locked himself inside the toilet.

"He was wearing his slippers, sitting on the toilet and clutching his identity card," said his wife, who added that she escaped death "by the narrowest margin".

"I would have held onto my husband and died with him.

"He was such a good person. I just don't understand why he had to die in such a terrible way."

Mr Lau and his wife moved into Wang Fuk Court in 1983 - the year the building was completed.

She recalls an idyllic life - a family with three children who were able to grow up close to the sea and spend weekends playing at a nearby park.

"All three of my children adored my husbandâ¦ He could be very stern, and I was the only woman who could make him laugh."

In 2024, mandated renovations began in Wang Fuk Court.

In Hong Kong, all buildings over 30 years old have to undergo an inspection - and any necessary renovations as a result.

Like many building complexes in Hong Kong, Wang Fuk Court had a management committee that oversaw things like building repairs. As many as 57 construction companies submitted bids. But the company that won, Prestige Construction and Engineering (PC&E), submitted the most expensive bid at HK$336m ($42.8m; Â£32.4m).

Residents, many of whom were over 65, had to bear the cost. The hefty bills triggered protests but eventually, the contract was signed. But once the renovations began in July 2024, residents were unhappy.

Among the complaints was that workers constantly smoked on site, dropping cigarette butts everywhere.

The 1,154 page report suggested the fire was caused by a lit cigarette.

Watch: Christy says she still sends WhatsApp messages to her husband who died in the blaze

There were also complaints about most of Wang Fuk Court's eight buildings having their windows taped over with foam boards to stop debris falling in - blocking daylight or any sort of view at all, for months.

All eight buildings were also covered in a protective mesh netting and bamboo scaffolding.

The report also found that after typhoons damaged the original scaffolding nets in 2025, cheaper non-fire-retardant replacements were chosen - something that was done as a "cost-saving measure at the expense of the safety of the residents".

Residents had filed nearly 140 complaints to six government departments, including the fire services and the buildings departments, in and after 2024, but these were largely ignored, the report went on to say.

It also revealed that a staff member of the estate's property manager, ISS, had turned off the power supply to the fire alarms in all eight buildings - which is why no alarms rang on the day itself.

ISS later told the BBC it had "reviewed and reinforced its procedures [and] staff training" in Hong Kong, adding that it takes the findings of the report "very seriously".

But for those who did manage to flee their homes - the emergency staircase turned out to be another death trap. Windows were removed from some fire escape stairwells to create temporary openings for workers - letting in smoke and cutting off vital escape routes.

Dozens, including Christy's husband, died in such stairwells.

Christy's daughter celebrated her ninth birthday the day before the fire

Christy, who wanted to be identified only by her first name, says her husband was at home alone when the fire broke out. She only found out about it when her friends sent her messages about it. By the time she made it to the apartment complex, it was already cordoned off.

She found out later that he had died of smoke inhalation in the stairwell, something she says that is at least of some consolation to her.

"My husband's body would have been burnt and he would have been in more pain if he was at home. At the very leastâ¦ his suffering was minimal."

Christy says her family had just celebrated their eldest child's ninth birthday the day before - they had saved some leftover cake in the fridge to eat the next day. Their father never got to eat it.

Both children have been deeply affected by the loss of their father.

Her youngest child, a seven-year-old boy, had "covered his ears and yelled" when he was first told what had happened to his father.

He now often comforts his mother, saying he will take care of her as he is the only man in the family.

"He hides his emotions. A seven-year-old child should not behave this way, and I feel heartbroken," says Christy, who has herself been diagnosed with PTSD and depression.

Her daughter has "extreme thoughts" she says, and channels her emotions through art.

She turned 10 on Wednesday, but this year, there was no birthday celebration in sight.

Her birthday, she says, will now instead be a day to commemorate her father.

Wang Fuk Court's problems date back to before the renovations began.

The management company hired to oversee and supervise the project, Will Power, conspired with PC&E to defraud residents in Wang Fuk Court, according to the city's Independent Commission Against Corruption (ICAC).

Although PC&E was found to have breached safety requirements and violated building department rules for multiple previous projects, homeowners at Wang Fuk Court were told that they had a clean record.

The ICAC alleges that this was part of a lucrative scheme, with Will Power's director Wong Hap-yin, along with other individuals, laundering over $5m in suspected proceeds from the tendering process from Wang Fuk Court, among other housing projects.

Once PC&E cinched the contract, Will Power's registered inspector - an individual meant to oversee the renovations - merely rubber-stamped the documents, without having actually conducted any inspections personally, the report added.

Will Power has been since liquidated and PC&E could not be reached for a response.

Both companies - Will Power and PC&E - and seven individuals have been charged with 25 counts of offences including manslaughter, conspiracy to defraud, attempting to pervert the course of public justice and tax evasion.

Four of them are still in custody but none of the cases have gone to trial yet.

The report said both companies involved were the "main culprits responsible".

But it also directed heavy criticism at authorities, saying that "no government department took effective ownership of various fire safety issues and complaints about fire hazards fell into a vacuum".

One expert, Prof John Burns of Hong Kong University, had called the tragedy the "largest governance crisis" that authorities have faced since 2020 - a reference to the controversial National Security Law that was passed in that year.

On the day the report was released, almost one year after the fire, Hong Kong's housing chief Winnie Ho resigned and the city's top leader John Lee apologised for the first time, promising that a "large number" of reforms would be rolled out.

Homeowners at Wang Fuk Court (pictured) were told their construction company had a clean record

Five months after the fire, Mrs Lau was allowed into her flat for the first time to collect any personal belongings that might have remained.

In her first visit, she was given just three hours to retrieve 40 years' worth of belongings.

Pictures taken from the visit show a home that has been utterly burned out - with every surface blackened and soot covering the floor.

"I almost fainted when I saw what had become of my home. I don't understand how the home I had built, such a warm and beautiful home, could end up like this," said Mrs Lau.

The frame of Mr Lau's wheelchair was one of the few things left in their burnt home

Her children, who came with her, described the house as a "war zone". Among the rubble, they found broken pieces of a piggy bank that Mr Lau had dutifully filled with coins every day for the past 20 years.

"Everyone in our family knew it was his favourite possession. The piggy bank died with him," said Mrs Lau.

Mrs Lau and her daughter have since rented a place nearby to live - and she is worried about how she is going to afford a new house.

The government has said it will spend $867m (Â£663m) to acquire affected flats - but Mrs Lau says it is not nearly enough to buy a similarly sized flat in the area.

Still, her family was in a better position than many others, she said.

"I lost my husband but many families lost multiple family members... Babies less than a year old died. "

Stories have emerged of some of the other victims - a six-month-old who died alongside her 68-year-old grandmother when the fire broke out.

The bodies of an Indonesian domestic helper and the elderly person she worked for were found huddled together.

Mrs Lau's son has since bought new piggy banks in memory of his father

As for Christy, she says she is planning to file a lawsuit against those responsible for the fire - but acknowledges that even if she wins, she "can't [get] my husband's life back".

"I share things that happen in our daily life, as if he still existed," she says. "I often write to him, 'I miss you so much. Are you doing well on the other side?'"

It's a similar story for Mrs Lau, who says she can't go anywhere without seeing her husband.

"Every day, something reminds me of him," she said.

"I still find it very hard to accept that my husband is gone, my home is gone, and so many of my neighbours are goneâ¦. This place was my roots. I never imagined it would also mark an ending for me."

'My heart breaks every time I'm called a hero' - Hong Kong fire survivor

Shares have veered wildly for days, pushed and pulled by the drama at Nvidia and the rise and fall of Anthropic’s stock after its blockbuster public offering.

#128. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#126. Allstate suffers multiday tech outage ahead of Thanksgiving

U.S. insurer Allstate has been experiencing a tech outage since Monday morning, leaving customers nationwide unable to access their accounts, review their policy information, file claims, pay their bills, and more, just ahead of the U.S. Thanksgiving holidays.

The outage has been impacting both the company’s website and mobile app, TechCrunch confirmed.

The breakdown follows a report from earlier this month that Allstate was planning to lay off up to 10% of its staff as part of an AI-driven efficiency push. (The company had not confirmed the report, which was attributed to unnamed sources.)

Allstate’s outage is the latest in a series of disruptions to services such as Microsoft Outlook and Teams, the airlines, Apple Pay, and several banks. Trump administration officials have said the outages are not being caused by cyberattacks, but are rather being caused by a recent set of upgrades to systems, many of which run on older technology. Those systems are now in need of more frequent security and bug fixes as AI makes it easier and faster to discover vulnerabilities.

At present, a message on Allstate’s website advises customers trying to log in that its services aren’t available, and that the company is “working to restore access as soon as possible.” On X, Allstate is employing what appear to be automated replies to customer complaints, asking them to DM the Allstate account with more information.

Downdetector, a crowdsourced outage-reporting website, shows users began reporting Allstate’s outage from Monday morning. However, the company hasn’t yet issued a formal statement about the disruption, what’s causing it, or when it may be resolved. In the meantime, local insurance agents have been fielding calls and emails from customers who aren’t able to use the company’s online services.

The timing of the outage is particularly bad given that Thanksgiving is a big travel holiday in the U.S., which means there will be car accidents. Customers will be left without a way to get in touch with their auto insurer to file claims and get roadside assistance through Allstate’s app.

Allstate did not immediately return a request for comment. On Wednesday evening, after publication, the company sent the following statement (below). It did not answer questions about the outage’s cause or planned resolution.

Allstate customers can reach us in many ways, including online, by phone and through local offices. Some access has been restricted over the last 48 hours, but we continue to be available for customers and respond to and settle claims. To the extent service interruptions impact a customer’s ability to make a payment, they won’t lose coverage or be charged late fees.

Updated after publication with Allstate’s comment.

#125. France's political mainstream risks presidential vote debacle with no clear candidate

Protests on the streets of France over students' concerns and the cost of living have been marked by looting and unrest in recent weeks

France's political mainstream faces wipe-out at the presidential election in April-May 2027, unless it acts fast to choose who it wishes to stand.

With every poll confirming the dominance of hard-right and hard-left leaders Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc MÃ©lenchon, candidates of the middle-ground â the old "legacy" parties â are being squeezed out of the game.

In theory any contender from centre-right or centre-left who was chosen in the first multi-candidate round of voting in April should be in a strong position to win in the play-off a fortnight later.

That is because at their separate ends of the spectrum both Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon have more people who oppose than support them.

But personal rivalries, lack of preparation and differing political visions have so far put paid to any hope of a meaningful challenge.

"The game in the centre is totally blocked," says political writer FranÃ§ois Fressoz at the left-leaning Le Monde newspaper. "The partisans of reason have lost their reason."

On the left â the old Socialist Party and its acolytes â there is at least agreement on a candidate: European Parliament MP RaphaÃ«l Glucksmann.

His problem is that allies who he might once have counted on â Greens, Communists and other small left-wing parties, not to mention many Socialist voters â are being pulled inexorably into the MÃ©lenchon orbit.

The protests started as an expression of anger at conditions in high schools

In the last two weeks, Green party leader Marine Tondelier, a majority of Communist parliamentary deputies and one-time Socialist presidential hopeful BenoÃ®t Hamon have all thrown in their lot with the leader of France Unbowed (LFI).

Another prominent figure of the left â FranÃ§ois Ruffin â has also withdrawn from the race, bowing to the unbowed MÃ©lenchon's now almost total command of the field.

So with the soft-left emasculated, the main hope for an alternative to Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon has to be focused on the right. But here the prospects â which until recently seemed bright â are now clouded with gloom.

"A MÃ©lenchonâLe Pen duel in round two is not yet inevitable," says Guillaume Tabard, political analyst at the conservative Le Figaro newspaper. "But it is looking increasingly probable."

In recent weeks the poll ratings of the main centre-right hopeful Edouard Philippe â a former prime minister under President Macron â have fallen sharply, and he is now consistently placed behind MÃ©lenchon in the first round of the vote. Marine Le Pen is way ahead of both.

Other candidates for command of this centre-right "space" are circling. They include another former Macron prime minister, Gabriel Attal; the leader of the right-wing Republicans (and former Macron interior minister), Bruno Retailleau; and now â a surprise new challenger â the mayor of Cannes, David Lisnard.

David Lisnard (centre) has seen membership of his micro-party Nouvelle Energie jump to more than 100,000

Lisnard, who stands on a liberal economic platform of less state and lower taxes, has emerged from nowhere to become the man of the hour. Membership of his micro-party Nouvelle Energie (New Energy) has shot up to more than 100,000, and he is being taken seriously as a possible Philippe replacement.

But this very multiplicity of names â not to mention others like judoka Teddy Riner, journalist Natacha Polony and former prime minister Dominique de Villepin â betrays the essential weakness of the centre-right challenge.

According to veteran commentator Nicolas Baverez, "If you want to win an election, it's quite simple: you need a strategy, a programme, and activists. Right now in the space between Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon we have none of that: there is a total vacuum.

"The most glaring absence is digital. MÃ©lenchon especially, but Le Pen too, are running extremely effective social media campaigns. In the middleâ¦ nothing."

Guillaume Tabard agrees that the lack of a shared platform is proving a serious handicap.

"In recent weeks we have seen Marine Le Pen issue her manifesto proposals for reforming pensions. We have seen Jean-Luc MÃ©lenchon publish his programme in a best-selling book," he says.

"What do we have in between? Arguments about names and polling figures and whether or not there should be a primary. It's not, to put it mildly, an encouraging start."

For Fressoz, "There is no-one in the centre who is taking up the fight. When Le Pen makes her proposals about pensions, there is no-one saying, 'Hang on, that doesn't add up!'. Macron â who did after all take her on and beat her â is said to be furious."

Any mention of Macron, though, is most unwelcome for the camp that nominally is his heir. Philippe, Attal and Retailleau all served under a president who Baverez now calls "as toxic as Trump", so deep is his unpopularity in the country. Lisnard, by contrast, makes much of his refusal to serve under Macron â but he faces another handicap.

According to Fressoz, "Lisnard has shot up in the polls because he is on the right of the right, and there is always an appetite for a figure who is not Le Pen, but who speaks her kind of language on immigration.

"But Lisnard is an economic liberal, and in France that is a minority taste. He'll have his moment but then he will deflate."

Emmanuel Macron is now considered a politically toxic figure in France

With five months to go to until the election, all is not lost for the mainstream. Optimists in its ranks say that come the new year, opinion polls will start settling behind one or other centre-right candidate, and the others will feel obliged to step aside.

Talk of a centre-right primary â as occurred ahead of the 2017 election â is beginning to ebb. The two strongest candidates, Philippe and Lisnard, are opposed. Lisnard says he supports one but his conditions are designed to be unacceptable to the rest. Retailleau and Attal are in favour â but that, says Tabard, is "mainly as a way to keep their hopes alive."

In theory then it is possible a name will emerge in January or February, and start pulling behind him (or less likely, her) the mass of voters who do not want a disruptive radical as France's next head of state.

For Fressoz of Le Monde, "After years of Macron's 'neither left nor right', the left and right are once again shaping French politics. But now it is the extremes who are in control."

Le Pen and MÃ©lenchon have both already run three times for the presidency. Their visions were defined long ago, their strategies laid, their activists trained. Polarisation is their friend, so is the social tension of recent weeks, and both are undisputed masters in their own camps.

The 2027 dÃ©nouement has been in their sights for years. That is their massive advantage.

#123. WhatsApp now lets you schedule messages in group and individual chats

WhatsApp on Wednesday started rolling out a much-sought-after feature: the ability to schedule messages in both individual and group chats.

The feature is rolling out globally to all users across iOS and Android. Once it becomes available, you can type a message and long-press the send button to schedule it. You can choose to send a message anywhere between 10 minutes and 14 days later.

Users will be able to see all their scheduled messages and delete them if needed. It’s not clear if there is a way to edit the schedule of a message.

Notably, scheduled messages will be sent even if your phone is off or outside network coverage. You can schedule messages containing text, photos, and videos.

The feature could prove useful for wishing someone a happy birthday on time or chatting with people across time zones. Businesses may also find it easier to schedule texts and updates to their customers, and people who use the app to coordinate work will likely find themselves using scheduled texts to update their teams.

WhatsApp rivals like Telegram and Signal have had the ability to schedule messages for a while now, so the Meta-owned chat app is late in rolling it out.

#122. Spain: Canary Islands witness 1-day migration record

The arrival of over 2,000 migrants to the islands comes as Spain holds elections this weekend. The president of the Canary Islands said the archipelago is facing "unprecedented migration pressure."

The Spanish Canary Islands witnessed a one-day migration record on Tuesday, with over 2,100 people arriving to the region in over 24 hours.

Most of the migrants landed on the the island of El Hierro, according to Spanish authorities.

"The Canaries can't take it anymore. We're experiencing an unprecedented migration pressure," President of the Canary Islands Fernando Clavijo wrote on X. He requested an urgent meeting with European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen to discuss an immediate EU response.

Around 100 minors arrived on Tuesday, with a baby also born during the trip from Africa to the islands.

The Canaries, a tourist destination whose shortest gap with northwest Africa is around 100 kilometers (around 62 miles), tends to see more migrant arrivals in the fall when the waters are calmer. The waters become rougher in the winter due to storms and swells.

Earlier this month, nearly 1,600 migrants from West Africa were saved and taken to the Canary Islands in a previous record. Over 26,000 migrants came to the Canaries this year so far — with half of that figure in November alone.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The route from Africa to the Canaries is one of the deadliest in the world for migrants.

Boats taking off for the islands sometimes drift out to open sea, with the migrants onboard facing starvation, dehydration and lack of medical care. Human smugglers frequently organize the trips and abuse the migrants.

The spike in migration to the Canaries comes as Spain will hold a snap election on Sunday.

Migration, along with housing, will be one of the topics at the forefront of voters' minds when they cast their ballots.

Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez, who belongs to the center-left Socialist Party, has faced backlash from the opposition regarding his tolerant attitude towards migration. The Spanish government earlier this year enacted a plan to legalize up to 500,000 undocumented migrants.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In late July, over 80,000 migrants crossed from Morocco into the Spanish North African enclave of Ceuta, triggering a crisis for the Sanchez government. Countries such as Germany and Italy tightened or extended border controls due to the influx of migrants into Ceuta.

The far-right Vox party, which could receive its best-ever result in upcoming election, has called for Sanchez and his ministers to be prosecuted for treason over the Ceuta crisis. The conservative Popular Party could form a coalition government with Vox after the election.

#121. Spanish elections: What you need to know and what's at stake

The People's Party of Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o is narrowly ahead in most opinion polls

After eight years of Socialist-led government under Pedro SÃ¡nchez, Spain is at a crossroads.

Snap elections are taking place on Sunday 29 November, and voters face a choice of continued left-wing rule or a conservative-led government, with the likely support of far-right Vox.

Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o's conservative People's Party is narrowly ahead of the Socialist PSOE in most polls.

He has spoken of Spain being probably at "the most significant moment in our country for the past 50 years", and he aims to "repair the state, regenerate democracy, reunited the nation and recover Spain's ambition and self-esteem". But it is unlikely he could do anything without the backing of Vox, who are third in the opinion polls.

For more on FeijÃ³o and who else to watch in this election - read here.

Spain has a population of almost 50 million, and almost 39 million people have the right to vote for the Congress and the upper house, the Senate. The polls open at 09:00 (08:00 GMT) on Sunday and close at 20:00 (19:00 GMT), when the first exit polls come out.

SÃ¡nchez called this vote eight months early after MPs rejected planned changes to Spain's housing laws.

He was responding to nationwide protests after an 87-year-old woman called Maricarmen Abascal was evicted from her rented flat in Madrid and later died.

But his government lost the support of a centre-right party called Junts from the north-eastern region of Catalonia which sought to protect the rights of property owners.

Housing remains one of the hot-button issues for voters, but they are also focused on the high cost of living, corruption and immigration. And the election takes place within weeks of a devastating rail disaster that has shattered confidence in Spainâs high-speed rail network.

Spain saw its first general strike for 14 years this month over a shortage of housing and high rental costs

Spain's property prices and rents have soared by more than 80% from 2015 to 2025, and wages have not kept pace.

Spain has a combination of high demand and scarce supply: the Bank of Spain estimates 700,000 homes are needed to ease the pressure.

The result is that millions of Spaniards have little chance to buy a house, while they live in fear of eviction if their rent is increased. On 11 November, four million Spaniards were galvanised into joining the first general strike since 2012, according to the unions.

Pedro SÃ¡nchez has embraced housing reform as a big campaign issue and left-wing parties have promised rent controls and protection against eviction, while the right is focusing on building more homes, tax breaks for homebuyers and planning deregulation.

SÃ¡nchez's wife BegoÃ±a GÃ³mez has played an important role in the election campaign

Support for the Socialists is down from 2023, but even then he came second in the race and relied on the backing of other parties, mainly on the left, to stay in power. This time those other left-wing parties, in particular the Broad Front (Frente Amplio) have fallen back and the big questions now are more about FeijÃ³o's conservatives.

"It is a demobilisation of the left. Sanchez's party is not doing well," says Jacobo Ramos Folch, Policy Analyst and visiting professor at the University of Navarra.

"That lack of majority is really a reality â and the rise of Vox. What is changing that shift of majorities is the rise of Vox."

Vox have been around since 2013 but polls suggest this time they could almost double their 33 seats in Spain's lower house, the Congress.

Their big themes are "national priority" â putting Spanish citizens first for subsidies and welfare, along with repatriating migrants "in an illegal situation" and "remigration" of foreigners who do not contribute to Spain.

Pedro SÃ¡nchez (left), Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o (centre) and Santiago Abascal (right)

Migration has become a key challenge for Spain, and Alberto NuÃ±ez Feijoo has accused his rival of being a "problem for Europe when it comes to immigration" after the influx of tens of thousands of migrants into the exclave of Ceuta from neighbouring Morocco in July.

This month too, the Canary Island of El Hierro has seen record arrivals, with 2,000 people landing there on 24 November. Abascal has spoken of "another invasion".

And yet a recent CIS survey put it far behind housing, cost of living, healthcare and working conditions.

The PP and Vox have bitterly criticised the Socialist-led government for a drive to legalise unauthorised migrants this year that attracted 1.2 million applications.

FeijÃ³o's party wants to see more controlled immigration. Vox has called for mass deportations.

Clashing political visions reflect a divided Spain ahead of election

FeijÃ³o has accused the Socialists of being "the most corrupt party" that Spain has ever seen, and a number of cases have involved SÃ¡nchez's inner circle and family.

People should distinguish between real and "fictitious" corruption allegations, says el momento mÃ¡s trascendental.

His former transport minister JosÃ© Luis Ãbalos has been given 24 years in prison over multiple corruption offences.

Leading party figure Santos CerdÃ¡n awaits trial for alleged bribery and denies wrongdoing.

SÃ¡nchez's wife BegoÃ±a GÃ³mez faces trial for alleged misuse of public funds and using her marriage to further her academic career. She denies wrongdoing. And his brother David SÃ¡nchez has been banned from public office for nine years for administrative misconduct.

FeijÃ³o has made clear his objective is to form a majority and make "parliamentary support agreements" with Vox. As far as a coalition is concerned, he says he will respect the will of Spanish voters, but maintains Abascal has never talked to him about running any ministries.

The PP leader is clear that Vox's national priority policy would run counter to Spain's constitution and European Union treaties: "No-one can sign illegal agreements."

Even if they were to go into coalition, Camino Mortera-Martinez of the Centre for European Reform, and a former European affairs adviser to the Spanish prime minister is not convinced Vox would wield much influence.

"They're not going to get any of the core ministries. They're not going to get defence or home affairs or foreign policy or economy," she told the BBC. Even if they take over the education ministry, Mortera-Martinez argues education policy is largely devolved to Spain's regional governments.

The leader of Vox, Santiago Abascal, is hoping to double his party's seats in parliament

Since 2022, Vox has worked with the PP in a series of regional coalition governments, holding ministries such as culture and tourism. At the time FeijÃ³o had reservations about Vox joining the conservatives in government in Castilla y LeÃ³n.

The following year they went into coalition with the conservatives in Valencia, AragÃ³n, Murcia, and Extremadura, but pulled out of all five governments in 2024 when they refused to accept unaccompanied migrant children being moved to their regions.

They have since signed new coalition pacts with the PP in Extremadura, AragÃ³n, Castilla y LeÃ³n and Andalusia.

Unlike many European countries Spain has 17 autonomous communities (and two cities) that have their own parliament and their own powers. Catalonia in the north-east and the Basque Country in the north have their own police forces.

The regions matter to Sunday's result. Catalonia alone has 48 seats out of the 350 in Spain's parliament, and the Socialists have recently come to rely on regional parties for support.

SÃ¡nchez's government relied on the support of Basque separatists Bildu and two Catalan parties, ERC on the left and Junts on the right. But when Junts refused to back his housing reform, he decided to dissolve parliament.

Spain is made up of 50 constituencies which each have a minimum of two seats.

The exclaves of Ceuta and Melilla both have one, and the other 248 seats are divided proportionally according to voter numbers.

Madrid province will have 38 seats, Barcelona 32 and Valencia 16.

To win a seat, a party has to win 3% of the vote in a province, but in smaller provinces with fewer seats parties generally need more than that.

#119. X’s latest update for creators focuses on vertical video

X announced new creation tools Wednesday, many of them focused on short-form vertical video. The updates are part of the platform’s effort to compete with social networks such as YouTube and Instagram as the format continues to grow in popularity.

The tools are designed to help creators make professional-quality vertical videos, share original content more easily, and earn more. They also give creators more ways to record, edit, and publish content directly on the platform without relying on third-party apps.

A key addition is an upgraded media editor that lets creators produce polished vertical videos without leaving X. Recording features include the ability to capture video in segments and use green-screen effects, the company said.

The editor also offers a range of postproduction tools. Creators can convert horizontal videos to vertical format, automatically generate captions, trim footage, add text overlays, choose custom thumbnails, and adjust photos before publishing.

Thanks to a recently announced update, users can now add up to 20 photos and videos to a single post, which audiences can swipe through horizontally. X says the feature is intended for sharing event recaps, behind-the-scenes moments, and product launches.

Availability of the new features varies by region, X said.

Earlier this month, the company introduced a clipping feature that lets users take snippets from longer videos, add their own commentary, and post the clips without leaving X. Clips credit and link to the original video, plus the person who creates a clip and the original poster can earn from its views.

Another feature, “React with Video,” launched earlier this year. Similar to TikTok’s “stitch” feature, this lets creators respond to posts with video commentary while displaying the original post in the background.

X has also introduced new earning opportunities and updates to its creator monetization programs in recent months. These include the “Video Kickstart Bonus,” an invitation-only pilot that rewards selected creators for publishing original vertical videos under three minutes long and without watermarks.

#116. The US pastor who wants to see women's right to vote overturned

Watch: Doug Wilson talks about DC outpost of church where Hegseth, other members of Trump administration worship

"Patriarchy is in accordance with the way God made the world." That is the view of Pastor Doug Wilson, a conservative minister who the US Secretary of Defence Pete Hegseth calls his mentor.

Speaking to the BBC from his base in Moscow, Idaho, Wilson explained his view that a society led by men is "a very great blessing". It's a perspective Christians who disagree with him say is based on a fundamentalist understanding of the Bible, which promotes the subjugation of women.

A self-described Christian nationalist, Wilson believes the US should be a theocracy, with gay sex criminalised, abortion outlawed, and people of other faiths unable to hold public office.

In the past he has delivered a sermon on "The Lost Virtue of Sexism," arguing that the Bible says women should be excluded from leadership of the church, and certain roles in civic affairs. And as part of his views on the division between the sexes, he would like the 19th Amendment of the US constitution, which granted women the right to vote, to be repealed, something his detractors say is anti-democratic.

The vast majority of Christians support women's right to vote, and dismiss his message as misogynistic and extreme.

Rev. Canon Dana Colley Corsello, the vicar at the Washington National Cathedral, rejects Wilson's understanding of what the Bible says about women's roles.

"In my mind, there is no biblical precedent or standing for what [Wilson has] proposed," she told the BBC.

"I'm married, I have a husband, I have children. My husband and I are complete equals in everything we do."

Have the American Pope and the American administration fallen out?

Pete Hegseth brings combative style as face of Trump's war in Iran

But while Wilson still represents a fringe perspective amongst Christians in the US, some of his views have been gaining support.

The denomination he co-founded, the Communion of Reformed Evangelical Churches (CREC), has around 150 churches in the US. It's a drop in the ocean compared to the 350,000 or so other Christian congregations the US. But Wilson says membership continues to rise.

His influence has grown in part because of his connection to Pete Hegseth. The Secretary of Defence is a "proud member" of a CREC affiliated church, a Pentagon spokesperson told the BBC, adding that the "secretary very much appreciates many of Mr. Wilson's writings and teachings".

Wilson says the two have "a warm relationship", although he stresses it is "largely a pastoral one, not a political one". Wilson has led prayer services at the Pentagon, including one in mid-November.

Joining Wilson on stage, Pete Hegseth said "thank you for your leadership, for your mentorship, for the things you've started, the truth you've told, your willingness to be bold."

Hegseth worships at the Washington, DC branch of Wilson's network of churches. And there are other members of the current administration that worship there too, according to Wilson.

Pete Hegseth has thanked Pastor Doug Wilson for his "mentorship"

Of all his views, it is his statements on women's suffrage that have garnered the most headlines.

Wilson favours "household voting", where the head of the home â usually a man â votes on behalf of their family. It's the system used for elections within his church, although the women in Wilson's family do vote in civic elections in order to counter votes of liberals, he says.

He says only in "odd" circumstances, such as divorce or bereavement or "where the husband was a quadriplegic," should women assume the role of head of household. Just 7% of votes in Wilson's church polity are cast by women.

When challenged by the BBC, he admitted it was a system that gave men "outsized influence".

Wilson says that repealing the 19th Amendment is "low" on the list of priorities for him and his congregation. But, he adds, "we do think it".

Julie Ingersoll, a professor of religious studies at the University of North Florida, says Wilson's beliefs are misogynistic and extreme - part of a world view that says women should be in complete submission to men in every part of their lives.

It is extremely unlikely that the 19th Amendment would be repealed - it would require either a proposal from two-thirds of the House and Senate or two-thirds of the state legislatures. Three quarters of states would then need to ratify it.

Still, says Ingersoll, the threat to women's rights should be taken seriously.

"It's not unlike the battle that they waged against Roe versus Wade," she says, referencing the Supreme Court decision that granted women the right to an abortion. It was overturned in 2022, after years of legal battles that restricted women's access to abortion.

"Thirty years ago, people would have said, oh, they're not gonna undo abortion rights.

"But they chipped away and they kept chipping away until they were able to overturn it. And I see this as directly parallel."

While Wilson says overturning the 19th Amendment could take a century or more, there are conservative Christians who are pushing for women to have their right to vote removed more immediately.

Last February, pastor Dale Partridge posted on Instagram saying "We will repeal the 19th Amendment within ten years".

Partridge, who has around 900k followers across social media, credits Wilson as being someone who "broke the ice" for "strong politics and theology".

Partridge has said that "the majority of women are not capable of responsible voting" because too many of them are liberal.

He also frequently collaborates on podcasts with another conservative pastor named Joel Webbon.

"Those guys have overshot by a long way," says Wilson of the two younger pastors. "They are disruptors and basically rage baiting."

So what might be driving those who support the effort?

Kelly Marino, an associate professor at Sacred Heart University who studies women's suffrage, says that the last decade has seen big wins for the women's rights movement, and "liberal political ideology" in general.

But, she argues, "any time where there was sort of this peak of feminism, there've been people who respond aggressively and try to push back".

Added to that are the high levels of uncertainty and conflict that have characterised the post-Covid period.

"I think there's been this craving for tradition and things that felt safe," Marino says.

That, she says, can be seen in the popularity of notions such as being a "tradwife" or "soft girl", who reject career goals in favour of traditionally female roles of wife and mother.

Marino says that while she doesn't think the constitution could be changed in the immediate future, the conversation around repealing the 19th Amendment is shaping the way people think about women's roles in general.

Canon Corsello, the Vicar at Washington Cathedral, sees the midterm election results as a repudiation of the push against women's rights.

"Cultural issues did not translate in this election", she says. "When people are preaching love, empathy, inclusion... that actually resonated with people".

Wilson's views have, however, been gaining ground beyond the US. The CREC has more than 30 churches internationally, including in Poland, Japan and Brazil.

The latter country has seen its own movement of right-wing influencers pressing for the removal of women's suffrage, with some leaning on religious language to make their point.

Wilson wants to continue expanding his brand of Christianity around the world, and has recently accepted a church in England into his network.

While CREC churches in each country have their own distinct characteristics, Wilson says their core beliefs are the same.

When it comes to the US, he says his ideal scenario would be for his congregations to "to practice what we believe in the church and for outsiders to look at that and envy it and say, man, that seems like a good system".
