#115. Character.AI’s plan to auction off users’ private chats challenged by state AGs

What happens to your private conversations when an AI company goes bankrupt? If it’s Character.ai, apparently, the plan is to auction them off to the highest bidder.

Now, a coalition of 42 state attorneys general is intervening in Character.ai’s bankruptcy proceedings in an effort to stop the chatbot maker from selling off sensitive user data, which includes the private conversations of millions of users.

The attorneys general involved in the matter have put out individual press releases, claiming that Character.ai’s dataset includes chat histories from more than 21 million U.S. accounts, more than 2.1 million of which belong to minors. In addition, the sale would involve other personal data, like IP addresses, the statements said.

Only one anonymous bidder for the chat data has been disclosed so far: Smirks and Pangs Acquisition LLC (Smirks), which was set up as a Delaware company on November 13, 2026. The following day, Character.ai picked it as the base bid for the auction, scheduled for December 1, California Attorney General Rob Bonta noted. It is unclear what the buyer’s plans are for the chats.

In addition, some states, including California, Colorado, New York, and Connecticut, have enacted chatbot safety legislation that will go into effect on January 1, 2027, which could trigger further violations if the sale goes through.

“Character.AI promised consumers that their data would not be sold. This kind of behavior is illegal under federal and California law and bankruptcy should not be used to sidestep these consumer protections,” Bonta said in a statement.

Connecticut Attorney General William Tong also criticized Character.ai’s business model.

“Their chatbots drove vulnerable youth down a rabbit hole of emotionally volatile and sexually inappropriate chats,” Tong said. “Their reckless misconduct and broken business model drove their company into the ground. Now they want to abuse the bankruptcy process to do exactly what they said they wouldn’t do — auction off those private chat records and personal details to a shady buyer.”

Tong added: “We objected to the Disney sale, and we’re calling on the bankruptcy court to reject this sale too as yet another guise to eviscerate legal liability and consumer protection.”

The opposition to the Disney sale was led by a similar but not identical coalition of state AGs. They fought against a proposed $20 million licensing deal that would give Disney access to Character.ai’s intellectual property, but not to users’ chats. The deal has not yet received final court approval. (Disney also agreed to hire most of Character.ai’s remaining staff.)

Character.ai suffered after its co-founders, Noam Shazeer and Daniel De Freitas, returned to Google, when the tech giant signed a non-exclusive license to use the AI startup’s tech. The company also faced lawsuits related to child safety — one involved the suicide of a 14-year-old Florida boy whose mother claimed he had become obsessed with a chatbot on the platform.

California AG Bonta is joined in the effort to oppose the sale of Character.ai user data by attorneys general from Alaska, Arkansas, Colorado, Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Utah, Virginia, Vermont, Washington, Wisconsin, and West Virginia.

#114. Peter Magyar meets Volodymyr Zelenskyy: Hungary and Ukraine make a fresh start after the icy relations of the Viktor Orban era

After years of frosty and hostile relations under Hungary's Kremlin-friendly former PM Viktor Orban, his successor, Peter Magyar, and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy seek to reset their countries' ties.

The location and the gesture were carefully chosen: Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Magyar and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy stood side by side, laid flowers and bowed their heads at the military cemetery in the center of the Ukrainian city of Uzhhorod.

One of those buried there is an ethnic Hungarian who was one of the first soldiers killed in Russia's full-scale war of aggression against Ukraine.

Magyar intentionally chose not to begin his first visit to Ukraine as prime minister at a government building. Instead, his Tuesday afternoon visit began at a place where he could demonstrate his solidarity with Ukraine more clearly than anywhere else.

Uzhhorod, known as Ungvar in Hungarian, is the capital of Ukraine's Transcarpathia region, which is home to around 80,000 members of Ukraine's Hungarian minority. Thousands of ethnic Hungarians are fighting Russian occupying forces on the front lines.

For Hungary, where ethnic Hungarian minorities in neighboring countries traditionally play an important role, the war is therefore closer to home than it is for other European countries.

Magyar described his visit to Ukraine as a "historic day" in a Facebook post. Before the ceremony at the cemetery in Uzhhorod, he said: "I bring with me the solidarity of the Hungarian people. We stand with Ukraine against the Russian aggressor."

Zelenskyy responded on X, saying "We appreciate this gesture of respect toward those who sacrificed their lives for Ukraine's independence."

The meeting between Magyar and Zelenskyy marked a turning point in Hungary's approach to Ukraine.

The two leaders repeatedly shook hands throughout the day. Such scenes would have been unthinkable just a few months ago. The propaganda of Magyar's pro-Russian predecessor, Viktor Orban, portrayed Ukraine as a "mafia state" and "lawless country" on an almost daily basis.

Under Orban, relations between Hungary and Ukraine reached a historic low.

Now the two countries are seeking a political reset, which is important not only for Hungary and Ukraine but for Europe as a whole. After all, Orban's repeated obstruction of support for Ukraine drove the EU to the brink of despair.

During talks in Uzhhorod, the two leaders also agreed to closer economic cooperation.

The talks centered on Hungarian diesel supplies to Ukraine, greater use of Ukrainian gas storage facilities by Hungarian companies, and joint infrastructure projects, including the opening of new border crossings and a direct rail link between Budapest and Kyiv.

Nevertheless, no formal agreements were signed on Tuesday.

At a subsequent news conference, journalists asked questions about sensitive issues between the two countries: minority rights, Ukraine's EU integration and a new EU loan for Ukraine.

Throughout the day, the two men were visibly keen to avoid any discord.

Magyar, for example, described his angry outburst over Ukraine's "Carpathian Eight" initiative in parliament in late September as a "misunderstanding" and suggested that Hungary might, after all, take part.

Zelenskyy's cautious replies to journalists suggest that he knows his country's path to EU membership also runs through Budapest.

One of Magyar's statements on Tuesday was key: "We've finally put a stop to this completely unnecessary and inexplicable political escalation."

Even before taking office in early May 2026, Magyar had promised to reset relations with Ukraine. But the process has not been entirely smooth.

In many respects — but certainly not all — Magyar is distancing himself from his predecessor. He has made no bones of the fact that he sees Russia as the aggressor, but he rejects both arms deliveries to Ukraine and even the transit of weapons across Hungarian territory.

Magyar previously accused Ukraine of treating ethnic Hungarians as second-class citizens — a claim that is not borne out by the facts. He pressed for greater minority rights and for a time blocked the opening of negotiating chapters in Ukraine's EU accession talks.

Magyar also departed from diplomatic convention by choosing the location of his meeting with Zelenskyy in Ukraine: Indeed, it was Magyar who invited Zelenskyy to Transcarpathia.

Magyar's sometimes confrontational tone toward Ukraine can also be explained by the need to avoid appearing too pro-Ukrainian to the Hungarian public.

According to a November 2026 poll conducted by the think tank Globsec, 33% of Hungarians would now support their country providing military aid to Ukraine. Although this is much more than during Orban's time in office, many Hungarians remain skeptical of Ukraine.

That's why Olena Halushka, a Ukrainian political scientist at the International Centre for Ukrainian Victory (ICUV), sees some aspects of Magyar's visit as a concession to public opinion in Hungary.

"Magyar has taken over a deeply polarized country," she told DW's Ukrainian Service. "A visit to Transcarpathia is more of a step for the Hungarian population than for Ukraine."

Ukraine accommodated not only Magyar's choice of location but also Hungary's demands for better protection of minority rights, which were set out in a 10-point plan in June. That's because EU integration is one of the country's top political priorities.

After planned restrictions on the use of minority languages were withdrawn in late 2023, Ukraine's parliament in early October passed a law on minority language rights that in some respects goes even further than European standards.

Hungary subsequently dropped its obstruction of the opening of negotiating clusters for Ukraine's EU accession.

Laszlo Zubanics, chairman of the Hungarian Democratic Federation in Ukraine (UMDSZ, one of two Hungarian political parties in Transcarpathia), told DW that the Magyar-Zelenskyy meeting was "historic."

He particularly welcomed the fact that Ukraine will probably implement practical improvements in the protection of minority rights "not only by the end of 2027, but much sooner, within just a few months."

A Hungarian diplomat who spoke to DW on condition of anonymity also called the meeting "historic," adding that after the Orban government's anti-Ukrainian policies, trust now needs to be rebuilt, and that personal meetings between the two leaders play an important role in that process.

The diplomat went on to say that Hungary not only wants to ensure that the rights of the Hungarian minority are protected, it also has a strong interest in being involved in Ukraine's reconstruction.

Overall, the diplomat concluded, both sides are very keen to expand economic cooperation.

Hungarian political scientist Daniel Hegedus of the Institute for European Politics (IEP) in Berlin told DW that while the meeting was a "symbolic milestone," it will be important to see "how the two political leaders capitalize on the symbolic political capital generated by this meeting."

No observers have suggested that this is the grand finale of reconciliation between Hungary and Ukraine.

Nevertheless, Tuesday's reset sends an important signal to Europe, namely that if the political will is there, a deep gulf that has existed for years can be transformed into a set of diverging interests that can be addressed through negotiations.

Or, as Zelenskyy put it, now there are "all the opportunities to build hopefully constructive relations."

This article was originally published in German.

#112. As foreign troops withdraw from Somalia, could al-Shabab take over?

Some analysts fear Somali security forces may not be able to handle the nation's security without external support

Somalia is facing a fresh test of its stability as foreign troops begin withdrawing, funding for its security forces comes under pressure and a bitter political dispute threatens to derail plans for the country's first direct democratic elections in decades.

The combination of challenges has raised questions about whether the government can defend itself against Islamist militant group al-Shabab while managing growing divisions among political factions.

Some analysts fear the situation could embolden al-Shabab, which is affiliated with al-Qaeda, and potentially allow it to seize control of the capital, Mogadishu.

The group already controls large parts of the country despite the presence of about 12,000 African Union (AU) troops supporting the government.

The withdrawal of foreign forces, alongside the activities of clan-based militias aligned with rival political factions, has heightened concerns about the government's ability to maintain control.

Ugandan troops, who make up the largest contingent of the AU force, began withdrawing last month, with the country expected to complete its withdrawal by next year.

Ethiopia, another major contributor to the AU mission with over 7,000 troops, is also reportedly planning to withdraw about 3,000 troops.

The US, which had earlier announced cuts to funding for the UN Support Office in Somalia (UNSOS) has since said it is extending it for a year. Washington contributes about a quarter of $500m UNSOS's annual budget, which supports Somali security forces, AU missions and individual troop-contributing nations.

With military support uncertain and the government facing mounting political challenges, analysts question whether Somalia's security forces are ready to stand on their own.

The concerns were heightened recently when al-Shabab claimed to have killed hundreds of elite Somali soldiers in an attack. The government acknowledged the attack but did not disclose the number of casualties.

Abdisalam Guleid, a former deputy director of Somalia's National Security Agency, told the BBC the security situation was increasingly precarious.

He warned that the withdrawal of foreign troops could leave Mogadishu vulnerable to a takeover similar to that of Afghanistan by the Taliban in 2021, following the withdrawal of US and Nato forces.

"I don't think Mogadishu will be able to defend itself. The Taliban style [takeover]â¦ that's the most likely scenario I can envision now," he said.

The Taliban took over control of Afghanistan between 6-15 August 2021 in a rapid offensive, following the withdrawal of US and NATO forces.

Such a scenario has played out in Somalia in the past, Guleid explains.

Why is Uganda fighting in 'hellish' Somalia?

Somalia has experienced the collapse of central authority before.

In the early 1990s, clan-based rebel groups advanced on Mogadishu from several fronts, forcing then-President Mohamed Siad Barre from power.

His overthrow triggered a devastating civil war and a prolonged humanitarian crisis. The collapse of state institutions subsequently created conditions in which Islamist militant groups, including al-Shabab, were able to expand their influence.

Guleid says Ugandan troops have been central to preventing a similar collapse since their first deployment to Somalia in 2007.

They played a key role in driving al-Shabab out of Mogadishu and other major towns, while protecting strategic installations, including the airport, seaport, government institutions and diplomatic missions.

Troops from Burundi, Djibouti, Kenya, Sierra Leone and Ethiopia later joined the AU mission, while countries including Turkey and the US maintained separate military deployments and provided training and equipment to Somali forces.

But Guleid argues that years of international assistance have not translated into sufficient military independence for the Somali government.

He says the authorities have become so reliant on foreign protection that they have neglected to adequately prepare their own forces to take over.

Somalia's Information Minister Abdifatah Qasim Mohamud rejected suggestions that the country would be unable to cope with the withdrawals.

He told the BBC that Somali troops had already taken over some positions vacated by Ugandan forces and were prepared to assume greater responsibility for the country's security.

He says Somalia could not depend indefinitely on foreign troops for its protection.

"I believe there is no reason for concern over this issue. There will be no change in the country's security situation or in the areas they have vacated," he said.

President Hassan Sheikh Mohamud has also been seeking continued international backing.

During a visit to Uganda on 23 November, he and President Yoweri Museveni called on the international community to continue supporting Somalia to consolidate security gains.

Uganda troops were first deployed to Somalia in 2007

Not everyone believes the withdrawal will necessarily result in a security collapse.

Ugandan security analyst Freddie David Egesa argues that years of international military support have helped Somalia establish the institutions needed to take greater responsibility for its security.

He says the withdrawal will provide an opportunity to assess how much progress has been made.

It will also force Somalia's political leaders to put aside their differences and take greater responsibility for the country's future, he adds.

But other analysts warn that the timing could hardly be worse.

Omar Mahmood, an analyst at the International Crisis Group, says Somali forces are under considerable pressure as they fight al-Shabab while attempting to take over positions previously held by foreign troops.

This is a major undertaking for an army that is still developing, he says.

The military is also facing pressure in the Bay region of the South-West state, where forces loyal to ousted regional President Abdiaziz Hassan Mohamed have been fighting the federal government.

The withdrawal of Ugandan troops from the Lower Shabelle region could create further security challenges, Mahmood adds.

Mahmood further points to the long-delayed elections, which need to be secured, as another issue compounding the security situation.

Somalia has been attempting to move away from an indirect electoral system in which clan elders select lawmakers, who then elect the president, towards a system of direct voting.

Beyond the military situation, Mahmood says, the government must also resolve a growing political dispute over the timing and format of the country's elections.

But the proposed changes have divided the government and opposition, with disagreements over how the elections should be conducted.

The dispute escalated this year after the president pushed through constitutional amendments extending the presidential term from four to five years, effectively delaying the next elections until 2027.

Al-Shabab militants still pose a big threat against the government

This month, the Supreme Court ruled against the extension, ordering political leaders to agree on an electoral roadmap within 30 days and to hold elections within three months.

A day before the ruling, the president dismissed Chief Justice Baashe Yusuf Ahmed. They have since challenged the dismissals at the East African regional court in Tanzania.

Mahmood says the political and security crises are closely connected, with the absence of consensus over the elections making it harder for the government to focus on the fight against al-Shabab.

Guleid also warns that the threat to Somalia's stability comes not only from the militant group but from clan-based militias increasingly challenging the federal government.

"Today, we have clan loyalists fighting alongside al-Shabab against the federal government," he says.

He added the country's leadership appears unable to anticipate the scale of the challenges ahead.

Who will fill the gap left by foreign partners?

Somalia's security challenges are compounded by its heavy dependence on international financial support.

Latest data from its finance ministry shows that donor grants amounting to $878.8m accounts for two-thirds of its planned $1.36bn budget in 2026.

Despite that, the ministry said it had only received about $314m of grants in the first nine months of the year, equivalent to 35% the expected annual aid.

Mahmood believes that although some international military support is declining, other partners could help bridge the gap through bilateral agreements and continued assistance.

But Guleid is sceptical that countries such as Turkey, Qatar and Egypt could quickly replace the military and financial contributions of Uganda and the US.

He argues that Somalia's dependence on external support makes it particularly vulnerable when international partners change their priorities.

That dependence has also contributed to increasingly strained relations with some foreign governments.

Somalia's challenges are compounded by its heavy reliance on foreign support

Somalia accuses the United Arab Emirates of funding armed groups and undermining its sovereignty, in the latest dispute involving an external power with significant security and economic interests in the country.

The UAE has rejected the criticism and hit back at the Somali leader, describing him as an "outgoing president" in reference to the dispute over his presidential mandate.

The two countries formally severed ties earlier this year, although the UAE has long been an important security and economic partner in Somalia.

For Egesa, the withdrawal of foreign troops could ultimately provide an opportunity for Somalia to demonstrate how much progress it has made in rebuilding its security institutions.

He believes the staggered departure of troops will allow Somali forces to assume responsibility gradually, while also forcing rival political factions to work together.

He argues that unless Somalia addresses its military weaknesses, political divisions and growing influence of armed groups, it risks losing the security gains made.

For now, it faces the difficult task of fighting an entrenched insurgency, managing rival political interests and preparing for a contentious electoral process, all while some of military partners are preparing to leave.

Whether Somalia can withstand these pressures without renewed instability remains an open question.

#111. DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers

The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.

According to federal prosecutors, Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”

But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.

Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.

The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.

404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.

Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.

Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCrunch that they took credit for the theft of FBI data in September, which they said they would not publicly release, a departure from its earlier extortion-based cyberattacks.

The federal charges against the Canadian cybersecurity executive relate to conspiracy to commit extortion, though much of the court docket — and his alleged links to the hacking group — remains under seal.

#108. Want to buy your kid an AI toy this Christmas? Not in New York

Santa won’t be delivering any AI chatbot toys to New York kids this Christmas.

Gov. Kathy Hochul this week signed a bill banning the manufacture, distribution, and sale of such toys in New York for the next five years. The bill explicitly targets toys with embedded or integrated AI chatbots, like talking stuffed animals, dolls, and robots that rely on the internet to keep kids yapping.

The bill makes New York the second state after California to institute a ban on AI toys.

Technically, the new law takes effect 90 days after signing, so some parents could try to fulfill their kids’ wish lists for a creepy toy like a jumped-up Furby in time for the holidays, but the governor urges them not to, given the risks. Apparently, about a quarter of American parents with kids under eight either own an AI toy or are considering giving their child one this holiday season, according to a new study released this week by Common Sense Media.

When asked how New York will enforce the ban given the pervasiveness of e-commerce platforms, a spokesperson directed TechCrunch to a statement from Albert Pulido, deputy secretary for finance and technology: “We’re partnering closely with the attorney general on this and the fees are very steep — they’re $15,000 a day. So working closely with the attorney general will make sure these toys are banned in New York State.”

When asked how Amazon intends to help enforce the ban, including for third-party sellers, a spokesperson from the company said it will take steps to restrict the sale of AI-enabled toys in prohibited jurisdictions, without going into specifics.

The Common Sense Media study found that AI toys can undermine children’s human relationships and expose them to inappropriate content. The researchers tested five popular AI toys marketed for learning and companionship, including Grem, Lovi, Meemo Bear, BubblePal Panda, and Tavlshero‘s AI Puppy.

The study found that the toys failed to meet numerous safety practices, undermining human relationships in part by claiming they loved their users, promising to keep secrets, suggesting they were more fun than real relationships or school, and helping children break parents’ bedtime rules.

While most of the toys in the study refused direct requests involving sex and violence, BubblePal Panda role-played a mass shooting in detail when asked. Most were willing to explain how to use or help locate dangerous items like matches, knives, and pills, according to the study.

Common Sense Media also found that the toys didn’t provide parents with reliable ways to monitor their young children’s interactions with the chatbots. Either they didn’t produce transcripts of chats at all, or the transcripts they did produce were riddled with errors.

It’s worth noting that Common Sense Media conducted a similar test of AI toys in January, finding that they pose an unacceptable risk for young children. Grem was included in that study, which suggests the industry hasn’t adequately addressed previously documented concerns.

While a November study conducted by the group found that parents are wising up to the potential dangers of AI toys and starting to steer clear, parents from lower-income households and those experiencing symptoms of burnout are more willing to consider them.

“These toys are promising relief to burned-out parents but simply miss the mark when it comes to basic safety measures,” Dr. Jenny Radesky, associate professor of pediatrics at the University of Michigan Medical School, said in a statement. “Caregivers should steer clear of these products that do not support the development of imagination or early human relationships.”

TechCrunch has reached out to the toy manufacturers for comment.

#105. Nigeria detains 5 Mexicans, confiscates over $220 million of meth

Nigeria's anti-drug agency said Mexican experts were allegedly recruited by a Nigerian national to run a meth laboratory in the southeastern state of Enugu.

Nigeria's National Drug Law Enforcement Agency (NDLA) said Wednesday it had arrested five Mexicans and confiscated over $220 million (around €200 million) in methamphetamine as part of a significant drug bust.

The agency said the Mexican experts were allegedly recruited by a Nigerian national to run a meth laboratory in the southeastern Nigerian state of Enugu.

"A total of 1,512 kilograms of methamphetamine, finished and in production, with an estimated value of $226.8 million was recovered from the clandestine factory," NDLA spokesperson Femi Babafemi said.

Nigeria first rejected the entry of the Mexicans into the country in August. They later entered Nigeria again in September via neighboring Benin using forged passports, with authorities then putting them under surveillance.

On November 11, the Mexicans were taken into custody at the Enugu lab after their movements were tracked by the Nigerian government. A Nigerian was also arrested in a nearby hotel.

The latest drug bust comes after Nigerian authorities in May also detained 10 people in a anti-meth operation in the southwest, including three Mexicans.

The arrests raise concern that Mexican cartels are getting a foothold in Africa for international drug trafficking. In addition to Nigeria, South Africa is another country where Mexicans have been arrested for being involved in a meth lab.

According to Vanda Felbab-Brown, an expert at the Washington DC-based Brookings Institution, Mexican cartels such as the Sinaloa Cartel and the Cartel de Jalisco Nueva Generation (CJNG) have expanded their operations into other continents such as Africa.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In a June testimony to the US Senate Caucus on International Narcotics Control, Felbab-Brown said the cartels find "desirable partners" for production in countries such as South Africa and Kenya due to factors such as "limited law enforcement capacities and entrenched criminal and corrupt networks."

According to the UN Office on Drugs and Crime,criminal networks often take advantage of porous borders in the West and Central Africa region to carry out activities such as illicit drug trafficking.

"Many countries in the region have vast territory and borders are located either in very remote or densely populated areas, thus making law enforcement activities particularly difficult to conduct in an efficient manner," the office said. "The porous nature of the borders in West and Central Africa is a major factor of insecurity: the borders are neither demarcated or sufficiently controlled."

#104. 1Password said hackers broke into its internal systems, but customer passwords are safe

Password manager maker 1Password has confirmed that some customer information was taken in a recent intrusion into its internal systems, but that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

The Toronto-based tech company said in a blog post published Wednesday that it detected an intrusion on November 19, in which an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate account with GitHub, which the company uses to share and store its source code.

The attacker used that employee’s access to push malicious code to the company’s internal servers, which allowed the hacker to download some data about 1Password customers, including their names and the email address that they use for logging into their account. The company said that the attacker also took details about when a 1Password account was created and when a user last logged in.

1Password said that customer vaults, which store their passwords, were “at no point” available to the attacker. The company also said that it doesn’t have access to customer account passwords or their secret keys, which are used to scramble their passwords so that 1Password cannot see them.

In addition, the company said that its apps and extensions are unaffected by the incident.

It’s unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s questions about the incident.

1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. We’ve also asked if the company has received any communication or demands from the hackers. We’ll update if we hear back from 1Password.

The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.

#103. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#99. Anthropic’s Dario Amodei calls accusations of surrendering human control to AI ‘absurd’

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei is pushing back against claims that Anthropic believes we should “surrender human free will” to AI systems. In a long X post on Wednesday, Amodei called the suggestion “absurd.”

“We should never sacrifice legitimacy or self-determination at the altar of efficiency or security,” he wrote. This argument against autocracy applies word for word against governance by AI, and I feel equally strongly that turning over governance to AI is a bad idea…No one who had carefully read my writing or my interviews could reasonably conclude that I believe anything different.”

The statement comes after a growing chorus from Anthropic rivals suggesting that human agency could be at risk if AI is developed irresponsibly. Earlier this month, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said he wanted to ensure people continue to have “agency over the future.”

Meta’s chief AI officer Alexandr Wang also wrote in an essay on X that we “should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI.”

“It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement,” Wang wrote. “Now that we are at a point where the scientific and technical problems on capability progress are likely solved, we must not only make progress in the extremely urgent areas of safety, alignment, and security, but we have to start thinking through how we progress thoughtfully.”

But in published essays, Amodei claims he has repeatedly advocated for democratic oversight of AI, saying the technology should strengthen democratic institutions and that humans should retain ultimate decision-making power.

Amodei noted that there’s a difference between surrendering human free will broadly and the idea that “as AI systems advance, they become capable of doing specific tasks in a more automated way.” As examples, he gave AI driving a car, designing a cancer drug, or writing end-to-end software.

Amodei suggested drawing a line at recursive self-improvement (RSI), or AI that can improve itself without human assistance. Many safety experts see RSI as inevitably leading to a loss of human control, a point Amodei implicitly acknowledged, writing, “we may want limits on this process.”

Still, Anthropic is laying the groundwork for RSI by incorporating AI into an increasing share of its own research and development. The company said in June that Claude authored more than 80% of the code merged into Anthropic’s codebase. An independent assessment published this month by AI safety research nonprofit METR found that Anthropic’s models are improving at about twice their historical rate, primarily driven by AI-assisted research.

In its own IPO filings, Anthropic describes how each successive generation of Claude accelerates the development of the next, a process it refers to as “closing the loop.”

“As we said publicly in early November, we don’t expect it to be safe for Claude to build successive frontier models fully autonomously, without meaningful human supervision or understanding,” Amodei wrote.

The problem with “meaningful human supervision or understanding” is that AI is already advancing faster than engineers and safety experts can fully audit it, leaving them increasingly reliant on other AI systems to act as judge and jury. The issue compounds when AI models engage in deceptive behavior, particularly when they recognize they’re being evaluated, making them harder to trust and control.

Still, Anthropic is by no means the only company that is pursuing RSI or building AI systems to automate more and more human tasks. This week, OpenAI claimed to have solved one of the biggest open problems in computer science — the exact speed limit of matrix multiplication, which is the mathematical engine behind modern computing, including deep learning and physics simulations.

In his post, Amodei accused competitors of engaging in a “vibes-based commercial and political positioning,” disingenuously throwing around phrases like “human control and agency” or “AI should just be a tool” without approaching the AI race any differently.

“Certainly the products that they offer don’t differ materially in their orientation towards automation of tasks; every company seems to be releasing models more and more capable of task automation,” Amodei wrote. “Every company claims to understand there are risks and downsides of such automation and to offer safeguards.”

#96. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#94. The canceled Tesla Cybertruck is the fastest-selling used truck in America

Tesla Cybertruck sales never reached the lofty heights CEO Elon Musk predicted when it launched. But the futuristic, steel-clad EV is finding new life in the used car market, according to analysis of sales figures by iSeeCars.com.

The automotive search engine and research platform analyzed more than 1.1 million sales of one- to five-year-old used vehicles in October 2026 and measured the average days on market by drivetrain, vehicle type, and individual model. The upshot: The Tesla Cybertruck was the fastest-selling used truck, averaging just 39.1 days on the market compared with 65.5 days for trucks overall. Used Cybertrucks sold faster than any other truck, including the Ford Maverick Hybrid, which held the No. 2 spot, and the Rivian R1T, in third place.

According to iSeeCars.com, the Cybertruck’s sales performance lines up with a broader pattern in the used vehicle market: Used battery electric vehicles are hot sellers right now, with 16 of the 25 fastest-selling used cars fully electric. EVs are even edging out hybrids in terms of average days on market. EVs average 45.6 days on market compared to 54.5 days for hybrids and 61 days for gasoline-powered vehicles. Not every EV is selling well, however. For instance, iSeeCars.com found that the Cadillac Escalade IQ was the slowest-selling vehicle in the study, averaging 134.3 days on the market, more than twice the overall industry average.

Karl Brauer, executive analyst at iSeeCars.com, told TechCrunch that high gasoline prices have changed consumer-buying habits, prompting more people to opt for an EV or hybrid over an internal combustion engine vehicle. If high prices persist, as they have since March, that trend could continue.

Keep in mind that these were October sales, the month before Musk announced Tesla would stop making the Cybertruck in 2027. Fuel prices and the relative rarity of Cybertrucks in the used car market were driving demand even before the cancellation announcement, Brauer said.

“The Cybertruck has only been out for a few years, so you have a lot of used ones coming off lease and entering the used car market for the first time,” he said, adding that used Cybertrucks are cheap relative to their MSRP.

The impending cancellation will likely fuel used Cybertruck sales, although the pace may ebb and flow over the next year.

“The canceling of the new Cybertruck is making people kind of aware — it’s like, oh wow, these things aren’t going to be coming anymore,” Brauer said. “Whatever demand is for them, supply will not be continuing to grow.”

Brauer said that once production of the Cybertruck ends — which is expected in summer 2027 — sales of used versions may pop once again.

“When the last one is being produced, that’ll probably reignite things again, and for a few months after that,” he said.

#92. Egypt: At least 31 dead after fuel truck overturns on highway

The accident occurred on the packed Ring Road in Giza near Cairo. Dozens of ambulances responded to the crash, and Egyptian health authorities say the death toll could rise.

A fuel truck overturned on a packed highway in Giza, Egypt, on Wednesday, killing 31 people and injuring 21 more, according to Egyptian authorities.

The fuel tanker crash ignited a massive blaze on Ring Road in Giza's Waraq district. The Ring Road encircles the greater Cairo metropolitan area.

Egyptian Health Minister Khaled Abdel Ghaffar said the injured were transported to nearby hospitals and added that the death toll could rise further. He said 39 ambulances are responding to the accident.

A massive fire broke out after the accident, with rescue workers pulling people from their vehicles. A large crowd gathered in the densely populated Waraq neighborhood amid the rescue efforts.

The blaze left at least 27 vehicles, including cars and motorcycles, charred.

An investigation has been launched into the crash, according to Egypt's public prosecutor. DNA tests are being carried out to identify badly burned victims of the blaze.

Egyptian President Abdel-Fattah el-Sissi expressed his deepest condolences and sympathy to the victims and directed relevant authorities to provide necessary support to the families of the victims and the injured.

Other countries also reacted to the crash in Giza.

Italian Foreign Affairs Minister Antonio Tajani said he extended his "deepest solidarity to the victims' families," Egyptian Foreign Minister Badr Abdelatty "and to the Egyptian people, whom I count as friends, at this tragic time."

Algerian Ambassador to Egypt Mohamed Sofiane Berrah also said the Embassy of Algeria in Cairo extended "its deepest condolences and heartfelt sympathy to the sisterly Arab Republic of Egypt."

Car accidents are frequent in Egypt due to poor road conditions, reckless driving and lax enforcement of safety rules.

#90. Jensen Huang broke Nvidia’s code of conduct, and his employees may not forget it

When Jensen Huang gave his first all-hands address since returning as CEO, there was an odd emphasis on Nvidia’s code of conduct. He admitted to violating the code, then emphasized that no one was exempt from it. But that “does not mean, the moment you violate a code of conduct, we walk you out the door,” Huang said, according to Wired. “Facts, circumstances, a lot of assessments” still matter. In other words, the code applies to everyone, but breaking it doesn’t automatically cost anyone their job, including, apparently, the CEO.

It’s an awkward stance for a company whose CEO has admitted to breaking its rules. A central part of modern human resources, a code of conduct can seem like a quasi-legal rulebook that lays out unacceptable behavior. But experts say most codes are better understood as a reflection of corporate culture. When an infraction goes unpunished, the main risk isn’t legal but cultural — that employees will feel embarrassed or even angry about the company they work for. That’s the risk Huang now faces at Nvidia.

Matt Plass, chief market strategy officer at LRN, an ethics and compliance training outfit, recently surveyed more than 2,000 workers across different industries for a study on workplace codes of conduct. As Plass sees it, the biggest risk is that employees will become ashamed of their workplace.

“People coming into the workforce now, ethics is incredibly important to them,” he told TechCrunch. “They’re really mindful of the ethical standards of their company. They’re really embarrassed and angry when senior leadership lets them down.”

The consequences of violating the rules aren’t always tangible, but they’re definitely real. Plass gave one example of a company that banned corporate travel to cut costs — only to have its CEO turn up in a VIP box at the Olympics.

“For over a decade afterwards, ‘the Olympics’ was used within that company as an example of the worst thing an employee could do. People would say, ‘Yeah, I shouldn’t have done that, but it’s not like I went to the Olympics.’”

Plass says companies that remove an executive typically do so after an investigation, then try to win back employees. “In most organizations they make a change at the top based on what’s happened,” he said, “and then they will have a hearts-and-minds initiative, to go out to their employee base and their customers and their investors and everybody else, and say, we get it. We’re sorry. We’ve changed. That’s essentially what they do.”

Of course, that playbook is harder to follow at a company like Nvidia, where Huang left without a clear succession plan, and there are serious doubts about whether the company could continue to thrive without him. In cases like that, Plass acknowledges there’s no easy answer.

“I would draw a distinction there between the kind of companies where the CEO is a kind of godhead figure … where the person and the company are almost impossible to separate,” he said. “There it’s much, much harder to deal with.”

In that context, Huang’s stutter-step over the code of conduct is easier to understand. Judging by the employee campaign for his reinstatement, Huang’s return hasn’t created a morale problem so far. But as the coming months put more pressure on Huang and his colleagues, sidestepping the code could end up costing Nvidia something harder to measure — its employees’ pride in the company.

#84. Colombian police storm supermarket to free 76 held hostage for 17 hours

Relatives of those being held hostage spent anxious hours as the situation unfolded

Colombian security forces have freed around 76 shoppers and employees who had been held hostage for about 17 hours at a supermarket in the city of Cartagena.

Gunmen had stormed into the building at 08:00 local time (13:00 GMT) on Tuesday after what Colombian President Abelardo de la Espriella described as a failed attempt at robbing an armoured car carrying money.

They held those inside at gunpoint while trying to negotiate their getaway.

With the situation becoming ever more intense as the day progressed, de la Espriella gave the green light for special forces and police to launch a daring operation to free the hostages, killing one gunman.

Residents living near the supermarket reported hearing explosions as the security forces moved into the building.

Police later confirmed that officers had used stun grenades to overpower the hostage-takers. Four have been arrested.

Around 20 people were still being held hostage when the security forces moved in

De la Espriella described it as "an extraordinary joint operation", which he said had been carried out "flawlessly".

He flew into the northern city to oversee the operation personally. He could be seen flanked by the commanders of the operation with a handgun strapped to his belt, in footage released following its conclusion.

De la Espriella said the gunmen had been given the chance to hand themselves in but had refused.

Earlier in the day, the assailants had freed 10 of the hostages, among them elderly women.

But they continued to hold around 20, even as a police helicopter hovered above and special forces teams prepared to enter the building, located in a popular shopping centre.

Local media report that, as well as asking for safe passage out of the supermarket, the gunmen also demanded nearly $2m (Â£1.5m) and armoured vehicles.

De la Espriella said they had no political affiliations but had taken the hostages once their plan to rob a cash-laden armoured car had been foiled.

"It's not that a terrorist group carried out a kidnapping - no, these are criminals who were caught off guard, had no way out and then kidnapped a group of people," he said.

Johan Correa, one of the rescued hostages, described to local media how the gunmen, who he said had been disguised, had entered the supermarket, where he worked.

"We had just started our shift when some guys dressed as police officers came in and started robbing the place," he said. "They were robbers, but we thought they were police."

He added that he and other hostages had been locked in a cold storage room, face down with their hands tied.

"We were there for more than four hours, lying on the floor with our hands tied, and it was a really tough experience."

Another employee, Dayro Rafael VelÃ¡zquez Castrillo, told Caracol radio that the assailants wore masks and had been armed with guns and knives.

VelÃ¡zquez said the gunmen repeatedly threatened the hostages, telling them that they had rigged the exits with explosives.

Relatives and bystanders congregated outside the entrance to the supermarket

He said that "thank God" they had been rescued, describing moments of tension as they heard explosions and a helicopter flying above.

Photos taken during the rescue operation show armed soldiers inside the building as people kneel with their hands behind their heads.

It is unclear from the photos whether those kneeling are hostages or the hostage-takers.

Local media report that the authorities feared that some of the gunmen may have tried to pass themselves off as hostages as the rescue mission unfolded.

The identities of the four arrested have not yet been released.

The hostages are being reunited with their families after being checked over by medical teams, according to the mayor of Cartagena.

Many anxious relatives had spent hours waiting for news outside the perimeter place around the supermarket by security forces.

A woman called Sandra told Caracol that her 76-year-old mother was one of those being held hostage.

Sandra's mother - among a group of elderly people released after six hours - was reunited with her daughters and grandchildren.

Footage recorded by local media showed bystanders cheering as some of those freed were escorted to ambulances waiting nearby.

But with around 20 people still inside the building, tension outside rose and police said they had to stop some of the relatives from breaching the perimeter.

At one point, flights into the city were halted, with planes diverted to Barranquilla.

Special forces, including units specialised in dealing with kidnappings, were deployed to Cartagena, a popular destination among foreign and Colombian tourists.

Cartagena's Mayor Dumek Turbay called for calm but the granddaughter of one of the hostages told Colombian newspaper El Tiempo that "this shows we're not safe anywhere".

De la Espriella was elected earlier this year on a promise to crack down on Colombia's criminal groups.

He had travelled to the central city of Villavicencio to inspect the damage caused by a car bomb that detonated outside the regional government's offices on Tuesday.

While de la Espriella blamed the leader of a dissident rebel group for that attack, he was adamant that the hostage takers were "simple bandits" without political affiliations.

#83. Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor dies at 65

Andy Taylor first revealed he was battling cancer in 2022 when Duran Duran was inducted into the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame. The band had numerous hits in the 1980s, including "Girls on Film" and "Hungry Like The Wolf."

Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor died after "a long and courageous battle with prostate cancer," his family announced on Wednesday. He was 65.

Taylor was responsible for 1980s new wave hits like "Girls on Film," "Hungry Like The Wolf" and "Rio."

The band's pioneering music videos helped drive early success, including through airtime on MTV.

"From the moment he arrived in Birmingham from the North East with a guitar and an unshakeable belief in what we could become, he brought an indelible fire and swagger to our music," the band said in a statement.

"His playing drove the songs that took us around the world, and with a dry wit that was matched only by his wonderfully unsentimental, no-nonsense outlook on life he kept us grounded and gave the band its edge through the extraordinary years that followed."

Duran Duran accumulated a slew of accolades including two Grammys, six platinum records, two Ivor Novello Awards and the MTV Video Visionary Award.

Taylor parted with the band in 1986 to pursue other projects.

The band was due to reunite when they were inducted into Rock & Roll Hall of Fame in 2022.

However, Taylor was unable to be present. He revealed that he had been battling stage 4 metastatic prostate cancer for several years.

"I would like to thank each of my brothers in this great band," he wrote at the time. "You can dream about what happened to us but to experience it, on one's own terms, as mates, was beyond incredible."

#82. FCC approves SpaceX plan to put AI data centers in space

SpaceX was given conditional approval by the Federal Communications Commission on Wednesday to deploy and operate a constellation of up to 1 million satellites for its orbital data center business, a regulatory victory for Elon Musk and his bid to move AI data centers into space.

The FCC authorization was granted despite objections from Amazon, Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society, which flagged concerns over orbital debris and congestion, radiofrequency interference, and environmental consequences. Amazon, which is building a competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink service, argued in its petition that the application lacked basic details and described it as a “lofty ambition rather than a real plan.” Despite that lack of detail, Amazon also said the proposal “risks real harm.”

The FCC dismissed some of the key concerns raised by opponents, including potential threats to scientific research, because the agency determined they fell outside its regulatory authority.

FCC Chair Brendan Carr said in a statement that the action “opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America’s leadership and growth.”

Musk thanked Carr in a post on X, writing “Much appreciate. This will be a great asset to America and the world.”

This initial regulatory approval doesn’t mean orbital data centers are imminent.

SpaceX faces a number of technical and economic challenges, including how to dramatically reduce the cost of manufacturing and launching satellites, cool processors in space, and even how to secure financing for chips that are destined for orbit.

The company must also clear other regulatory hurdles. The FCC has not yet approved SpaceX’s plans to handle orbital debris for satellites that would operate above 600 kilometers, for instance. SpaceX requested a waiver of existing FCC rules, which requires satellites ending missions below 2,000 kilometers reenter Earth’s atmosphere within five years of completing those missions. The company is interested in moving some of these retired satellites into other orbits around Earth or even around the sun, according to the FCC authorization document.

SpaceX has said it plans to deploy the satellites, known as Starmind, at altitudes ranging from 500 to 1,002 kilometers. The FCC’s approval gives SpaceX the authority to use radiofrequency communications to maintain contact with and control its orbital data centers from Earth if their primary communications systems fail. The Starmind satellites would primarily communicate using lasers to connect with other satellites, including SpaceX’s Starlink.

The FCC has given SpaceX time to sort out these issues. Under the approval, SpaceX has seven years to get its first operational satellite into orbit and 14 years to deploy the entire constellation.

#80. S&P considers Nvidia credit downgrade over governance and ‘key man’ risks

S&P Global Ratings, one of the three major credit rating agencies, is considering downgrading Nvidia’s rating, citing concerns about the company’s management and governance practices.

Despite Nvidia’s strong financial performance, the ouster and reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang has raised concerns about “weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning” and “key man risk,” meaning heavy reliance on Huang for its future success, S&P said in a statement.

The agency expects to finish its review within 90 days and could cut Nvidia’s rating from AA to AA-, the fourth-highest on its scale.

Although Nvidia is certain to keep its investment-grade rating, a downgrade could raise its borrowing costs. Because Nvidia backstops neoclouds and other infrastructure companies that lack strong credit on their own, a lower rating could make their debt costlier or force them to post more collateral.

In August, Nvidia announced plans to arrange $500 billion in financing for AI infrastructure with six financial institutions, including Goldman Sachs, Apollo, and Blackstone. Under the original terms, Nvidia would guarantee 25% of the loans. But even before Huang’s ouster and return, lenders had been demanding that Nvidia guarantee more, Bloomberg reported.

A downgrade would likely prompt lenders to demand even larger guarantees.

#79. Protesters call on Israel to free Palestinian student held without charge

Mahdi Tunsi appears by videolink in front of his attorney Nasser Odeh during his hearing at the Jerusalem District Court on Tuesday

Israeli students, activists and academics are calling for the release of a Palestinian undergraduate who was taken from his university dormitory by Israeli police earlier this month and has since been held without charge.

Mahdi Tunsi, a 26-year-old sociology and criminology student at the Hebrew University of Jerusalem, appeared in an Israeli court in the city on Tuesday - a hearing that was held behind closed doors.

Israeli police say he was arrested "concerning ties to an act of terrorism".

Authorities have requested what is known as "administrative detention", which allows the Israeli state to detain people indefinitely without charge on the basis of secret evidence that they cannot see.

"We definitely think this is a political arrest," said Dr Yael Berda, one of Tunsi's university professors.

Tunsi, a Palestinian resident of occupied East Jerusalem who works with well-known Jewish-Palestinian campaign groups, was arrested a month after Israel held an election that saw Arab-led parties win a record 19 seats in parliament, the Knesset, and Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu's right-wing coalition lose its majority.

Since then, Berda said she had seen an intensifying campaign against Palestinian rights.

Knesset Member Dr Ofer Cassif was one of the those gathered outside the court

Outside the Jerusalem District Court on Tuesday, demonstrators waved placards with a black-and-white image of Tunsi, while crying out "shame" in unison.

"There's no due process - [administrative detention is] a sugar-coated term for being kidnapped and held as a hostage," said Dr Ofer Cassif, a Member of the Knesset representing the Arab-led Joint List alliance.

"We must all stand firm against such crimes. It's unacceptable."

Since the Hamas-led attack on Israel on 7 October 2023, which triggered the Gaza war, the number of people in administrative detention has more than doubled.

In October, there were more than 3,100 in administrative detention, according to Israel Prison Service figures published by Israeli rights group HaMoked. Most of them are Palestinians from the occupied West Bank.

Israel says that its use of the policy is compliant with international law and a necessary preventative measure to combat terrorism.

Protesters held signs calling for Tunsi to be freed in Arabic and Hebrew

In a statement, Israeli police said they would "not elaborate further regarding the investigation" into Tunsi.

He appeared via video link from prison - his hair and beard shaved off. His friends also said that he was without medication as well as his glasses, despite a judge requesting them to be brought to him in detention.

The Israel Prison Service said it acted in accordance with the court's rulings.

"When the process was complete and all the necessary approvals had been received, the detainee's lawyer was told he could pass the eyeglasses to the detainee immediately, subject to security procedures," it added.

Berda asked to enter the courtroom on Tuesday but was denied entry. According to Berda, not even Tunsi has been told of the evidence against him.

The judge has said more time is needed to consider the state's request for administrative detention. A decision is expected in the coming days.

The judge has said more time is needed to consider the state's request for administrative detention

"If they have evidence, have them bring it or release him," said Berda.

"That's why we're demanding his immediate release or moving it to a criminal track where he could defend himself."

Berda said that while Tunsi could not vote in Israel's general election because he is not an Israeli citizen, he was "engaged" and very supportive of Arab parties' involvement.

"Mahdi is one of the best human beings I know," said Noga Kaplan, who studies with him at the Hebrew University.

"He has a really pure heart and is very passionate about his studies."

Protesters demand the release of Mahdi Tunsi outside the Jerusalem Central Court during his hearing.

After Tunsi's arrest, the Hebrew University issued a statement saying it was notified a few minutes before it took place.

"The university is not a party to the legal proceedings involving the student, and we do not have information that would allow us to comment on the circumstances leading to the arrest or on the ongoing legal proceedings," it said.

It added that it was concerned for Tunsi's well-being and hoped "that the matter will be resolved promptly and that his rights will be fully respected throughout the process".

Tunsi is a resident of Silwan, a neighbourhood in East Jerusalem where, in recent years, Palestinians have been evicted by Israeli authorities, who say their properties were built illegally. Israeli settlers have since moved into some of those properties.

"Political organisation stopped after October 7 because people were getting super scared about the consequences," explained Kaplan.

"Mahdi saw studying as one of the ways to speak about it in class."

"He didn't do a lot of public stuff because what's happening now is his biggest fear."

#76. Five Russian military jets hit in airfield strike, says Ukraine

A Russian MiG-31 military jet (file photo)

Ukraine has said it damaged five Russian military aircraft in an attack on Russia's Savasleyka airfield, 300km (185 miles) east of Moscow.

Three MiG-31 and two Su-34s aircraft were hit, Kyiv's General Staff said, adding that the extent of the damage was still being assessed but that "there may be even better news to come". It did not elaborate on what weapons it had used to hit the airfield.

The airfield at Savasleyka, in Russia's Nizhny Novgorod region, has been a target for Ukraine for several years. It hosts aircraft used by Russia to launch hypersonic missiles and glide bombs.

The take-off of MiG-31s in particular frequently triggers nationwide alerts across Ukraine, as the Kinzhal missiles they carry have been known to hit large infrastructure and military targets and are difficult to shoot down.

Overnight, Kyiv also said it hit two oil refineries in Russia's Samara region some 800km (500 miles) from the front line â the latest in a series of long-range strikes which have dented Moscow's oil processing capacity.

"Did you repair them? We'll hit them again," the defence ministry in Kyiv said.

Samara region governor Vyacheslav Fedorishchev said the night-time attacks had been "a true ordeal", with more than 145 drones shot down. One person was killed and several towns experienced power outages, Fedorishchev said.

Kyiv also said it had hit a radar system as well as fuel and logistics depots across Ukrainian territories currently occupied by Russia.

"Without fuel, military vehicles cannot move; without ammunition, artillery falls silent," the defence ministry said, adding that every successful hit meant Moscow could carry out fewer strikes.

Kyiv has developed a thriving weapons industry and Ukrainian strikes on Russian targets well beyond the front line have become commonplace in the years since Moscow launched its full-scale invasion.

On Tuesday, President Vladimir Putin called for a tightening of security measures across the country's energy facilities.

But he also argued that Russia had managed to stabilise its domestic fuel market without suffering "critical damage" and added that Moscow would "inevitably" retaliate against Kyiv.

Ukraine continues to come under daily attack. Ten people were killed across several regions between 24 and 25 November, while thousands are without power after Russian strikes on energy infrastructure.
