#73. Israeli strike kills senior Hamas security official and family in Gaza City

The strike caused extensive damage to the Abu al-Ouf family's apartment

An Israeli air strike in the Gaza Strip has killed a senior Hamas security official and three members of his family, according to local doctors and the Hamas-run interior ministry.

Mohammed Abu al-Ouf, 47, the head of Hamas's internal security service in northern Gaza, his wife and their two teenage daughters died when their apartment in Shati refugee camp in Gaza City was bombed at dawn, the ministry said.

The Israeli military said Abu al-Ouf was a "terrorist" and that he was eliminated "to remove the threat he posed".

On Tuesday, the overall commander of Hamas's internal security service was among 10 Palestinians reportedly killed in Israeli strikes.

Israeli air strike kills Hamas internal security chief in Gaza

Israeli attacks have turned Gaza into 'toxic wasteland', Oxfam says

Wednesday's strike in Shati caused extensive damage to the Abu al-Ouf family's apartment.

Photos showed the exterior wall and several interior walls were destroyed, with rubble and broken furniture left scattered across the floor.

An interior ministry statement said that Abu al-Ouf was killed along with his wife Noura, 41, and their daughters Yusra, 15, and Jana, 13.

It condemned the attack as a "heinous crime" and accused Israel of targeting security personnel in Gaza "in an effort to spread chaos within Palestinian society".

Hamas's internal security service is responsible for enforcing discipline, protecting the group's infrastructure and rooting out informants.

Israel's military said in a statement that Abu al-Ouf was a "significant figure" who had served as "a key source of expertise" in efforts to restrict Israeli counter-terrorism operations in Gaza.

"His elimination is a blow to the apparatus's leadership capabilities and follows yesterday's elimination of his commander," it added.

On Tuesday, an Israeli drone strike in western Gaza City killed Naeem Abu Nahel, the head of Hamas's internal security service.

He had only been in the position since September, when his predecessor was captured in a raid in Gaza City by Israeli special forces.

Near daily Israeli strikes continue in Gaza despite a ceasefire agreed in October last year.

At least 1,679 people have been killed in Gaza over the past 13 months, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

Israel says six of its soldiers have been killed in Gaza during the same period.

The Gaza war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,740 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza, the health ministry says.

#72. Extreme weather threatens Thanksgiving festivities in US

A major storm is forecast to move up the US East Coast and is likely to cause travel delays. Weather warnings were enough to postpone Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade for the first time in its 100 years.

New York's iconic Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade has been postponed for the first time in its 100-year history as extreme weather threatens to disrupt the plans of tens of millions of Americans.

The parade has now been pushed back to 8:30 a.m. EST (1330 GMT) Saturday.

"Our team is now working through the complex operational logistics required to bring the Parade to life on Saturday, and we will share additional details as they are finalized," the company said.

New York City Mayor Zohran Mandani said: "I know that this change of plans will cause inconveniences, yet safety has to come first."

"This will be a heavy storm. We are expecting sustained winds between 30 and 40 miles per hour, with gusts as high as 60 to 65 miles per hour possible," he added.

NBC, which broadcasts the parade, said it will instead show a rerun of the 2025 edition on Thursday.

Despite gale-force winds on the East Coast, coastal flooding in the West and snow in the Midwest, travelers flocked to airports, roads and railways Wednesday for one of America's busiest travel periods of the year.

A major storm developing on the Eastern Seaboard could bring heavy rain, coastal flooding, high winds and power outages to the Carolinas on Wednesday. It is forecast to move to the northern Mid-Atlantic and Northeast on Thanksgiving and is expected to affect air travel at airports in the region.

Already on Wednesday, the security line at New York City's John F. Kennedy International Airport topped one hour.

In the Midwest, winter storm warnings and watches were posted for parts of Missouri and Illinois, with up to 13 inches (33 centimeters) of snow expected in the St. Louis area by Thursday afternoon.

Meanwhile, on the West Coast, king tides were to continue to inundate some coastal areas.

#70. Russia's own figures show it has lost nearly 500,000 people in Ukraine war

Russia does not provide numbers of its fallen soldiers

At least 490,334 Russian soldiers have died since the start of the war in Ukraine in 2022, according to Russian government statistics analysed by the BBC.

The leaked Rosstat data reveal how the full-scale invasion of Ukraine has fundamentally changed mortality patterns in Russia, and show the high cost paid in human life in a war that Vladimir Putin had assumed would last only days but is now almost five years old.

BBC Russian has had access to more than 11 million death records from 2021 to September 2026, after a hack by Belarusian group Cyber Partisans BY and Ukraine's Silent Crow.

Russia's statistics agency admitted to a cyber-attack, but denied any protected information had been leaked.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said on Wednesday that only Russia's defence ministry could officially report data on losses: "I suggest no other data be taken into account." The defence ministry has not released any official casualty figures for several years.

Together with independent Russian outlet Mediazona, BBC Russian has compared the Rosstat data with its own list of confirmed military deaths, pulled from open sources since the start of the invasion, and it has found matches for 90% of the records.

Military experts estimate that the BBC's list of 290,012 deaths, gleaned from official reports, social media, new memorials and graves, may account for 45-55% of the true death toll, which would range from 527,295 to 644,471.

The records show that 65% of 18 to 24 year-olds in Russia who lost their lives in 2025 died in the war. For those aged 25-34 the number was 59%. Only among 45 to 54 year-olds does cardiovascular disease claim more Russian lives than the conflict.

The data also reveal the extent of losses in specific areas of Ukraine. Some 20,000 Russian soldiers died fighting for control of 10 Ukrainian villages whose combined pre-war population was 12,000.

During the fight for one small village called Andriivka, south of the town of Bakhmut in Donetsk region, 1,122 Russians lost their lives. Before the war 74 people lived there.

Russia does not give public figures for losses sustained since the war began in February 2022. And two years ago Rosstat stopped publishing data of deaths from external causes, which was used to gauge the extent of military losses.

For example, only 70% of deaths we have established of soldiers from the occupied regions of Donetsk and Luhansk in eastern Ukraine who fought in the Russian army were accounted for in the Rosstat files.

Overall, the Rosstat dataset confirms the trends that we have observed with our own count.

Ukrainian losses were last put at about 50,000 by President Volodymyr Zelensky in July 2026, but another list based on open sources by the anonymous UA Losses website puts the figure at 115,952.

The Rosstat dataset contains no names but does include ages, places of residence, places of death and also circumstances of death.

We have filtered the records first by location, leaving only Ukraine as well as Russian border regions that saw incursions by the Ukrainian army.

Next, we identified 425,174 entries explicitly indicating a military-related cause of death, such as explosions or shrapnel during combat operations.

As this count could include civilians, we filtered out women as well as men outside the 18-65 age group. And if someone lived and died in the four Ukrainian regions occupied by Russia, we excluded them as a possible civilian â unless he had a record of military service attached to his entry.

Another 14,093 entries were identified by looking at employment, and 51,067 were recorded in the database as deceased on the basis of a court ruling. This often happens when a soldier goes missing in a combat zone for more than six months and their body has not been recovered.

Again, we filtered out civilians by only counting deaths in the war zones in Ukraine and Russia.

According to BBC Russian's analysis of the leaked files, in the first year of the war the average age of those killed was 34. By 2025, as prisoners and contract soldiers entered the ranks, it had risen to 40 (38 according to our own list).

Some of Russia's poorest regions in Eastern Siberia account for a disproportionately large share of the losses. Men from Tyva, Buryatia and Zabaykalsky Krai are 20-25 times more likely to die in the war than residents of the biggest cities of Moscow and St Petersburg.

In most regions, the distribution of deaths based on Rosstat data differs from that in the BBC's list by just a few tenths of a percentage point.

At least 5,017 foreigners are among those who died fighting for Russia, according to our analysis of the Rosstat files, including 1,031 Uzbeks and 720 Tajiks. We have left out Ukrainians from this count to avoid including civilians.

This is a conservative estimate as dual nationals are listed as Russians in the database, and there is no reference to North Koreans who died repelling the Ukrainian incursion into Russia in 2024.

The BBC identified 2,304 North Korean soldiers who died there by analysing satellite imagery and official photographs of a newly built memorial in Pyongyang.

The leaked Russian statistics may not be complete but taken in conjunction with BBC Russian's own work they are significant.

In the context of a war that has lasted four years and 10 months, these are high numbers for Russia, when compared with the Soviet war in Afghanistan that ended in 1989.

In that 10-year war, an estimated 15,000 Red Army troops died, and an estimated one million civilians were killed.

#67. My family were killed in Ukraine. My mum blamed Zelensky's troops - the truth is complicated

Watch: BBC reporter investigates deaths of family members in Russia-Ukraine war

I am sitting on my balcony in Kyiv looking at a photo of a garden in the village I grew up in, in eastern Ukraine. The plot is overgrown with thick grass, and two wooden stakes are planted in the ground. Despite the warm sunshine, I find myself shivering.

The woman who sent me the photo says the stakes mark the graves of my grandparents, killed as the Ukrainian-Russian front line reached our little community. Not far away, in another garden in the village, the body of my father, Serezha, is buried in a greenhouse.

The photo Anastasiya was sent of her grandparents' graves

I cover the Russia-Ukraine war for the BBC World Service, but found myself in the middle of that story in February last year when my mother sent me a devastating text message.

"Granny, grandad and Serezha are gone. Now there is no-one left," it said.

Because not only had I lost three beloved family members, my mother blamed Ukraine for their deaths - the country where I was born and have lived for most of my life.

Mum was born in Siberia. From the moment Moscow launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, she had supported Russia in the conflict.

Anastasiya (bottom left) with her father (top left) and grandparents (right) before the war

Backing Moscow is not that unusual in the Donbas region - the predominantly Russian-speaking part of eastern Ukraine where my village Dachne is located.

After pro-Russian forces captured large parts of the Donbas in 2014, many of those who stayed supported Moscow over Kyiv, a stance cemented by exposure to Russian state TV.

Now the Russians occupy almost 80% of Donetsk - the part of the Donbas where my village is. Over the past nearly five years, since Russia's full-scale invasion, entire towns and cities have been razed to the ground, external.

The remains of Anastasiya's family home are circled in a frame of a video, filmed by Ukraine's 33rd Mechanised Brigade on 27 January 2025

I had already fallen out with my sister Sasha, who had been living in Moscow with her young family when the war broke out. She refused to take sides - I felt hurt and cut her off.

But earlier this year, around the first anniversary of dad's death, Sasha unexpectedly texted me. We started talking over text about our childhood - the way things used to be. I realised I was not angry with her anymore. She too was grieving, missing dad just as much as I was.

Anastasiya's father Serezha - he stayed in Dachne to look after his elderly parents

I told her I wanted to make a documentary about dad and she was very supportive. I knew then that I had an ally on my journey. But we decided not to tell our mum just yet.

I started looking for witnesses to the events in Dachne that took place in January 2025, the month dad is believed to have been killed. It was not easy. It was a village of a few hundred people, but many had left, others had died, and those who remained had limited phone signals.

Russia publicly announced the capture of Dachne on 14 February and it has remained under Moscow's control ever since.

This meant it was impossible for me to travel there in person. So I began trying to contact my family's neighbours.

Anastasiya could not safely travel back to Dachne, so she worked through contacts in village group chats

In a Dachne village messaging chat, I found the number of a man I'll call Misha. I was told he had been with my father on the day he died. Speaking to the BBC could put him in danger with the Russian occupiers, so I did not tell him I worked for the BBC or that I was recording our call.

Misha told me that on 26 January he had looked in on my father and realised he had been injured. Serezha told him he had been "clipped" by a drone, and had asked Misha to bring a first aid kit, he said.

It is impossible to know which side was operating the drone that killed my father, but we do know that Russian drones were constantly targeting the village at that time.

"I asked if the wound was serious. He [Serezha] said it wasn't," said Misha - but then recalling how, when he had returned to check on my father a couple of hours later, he had found him dead.

With the help of two other villagers, Misha said they had buried him in the greenhouse.

"I'm sorry I couldn't save him," Misha told me.

BBC Eye Investigations: Who Killed My Family?

BBC journalist Anastasiya Gribanova investigates who killed her father and grandparents in Ukraine, uncovering painful accounts as she tries to reunite her divided family.

Watch on BBC iPlayer or listen on BBC Sounds now (UK only)

If you are outside of the UK, listen to the Who Killed My Family podcast here

Watch our YouTube documentary here, external

As for my grandparents, my investigations suggested the events surrounding their deaths may have been more complicated.

Their house, on the eastern side of Dachne, had been destroyed. Neighbours told me they had escaped to another home on the same street.

I eventually reached a woman I'll call Lina, who lived nearby and was the last person, as far as I know, who saw my grandparents alive.

She could not recall what this date was, but knew it was in the second half of January.

At this time, she said, "the Russians were fully advancing - the edges of the village were being shelled. Then the Ukrainian soldiers moved from the edge of the village into the centre. They hid in people's yards and used side streets to avoid drones."

She said two soldiers entered her property. They had not been wearing any identifying insignia but had been speaking Ukrainian, she said - a language that Lina described as "Bandera", a derogatory term for Ukrainians.

"You're going to have to leave. Why are you still here?" Lina said they had asked her.

She said one had pointed a machine gun at her as she had stood outside her house, while another had gone to see my grandparents. She told me she had feared for her life and then fled her house for the night.

The following day, she told me she had returned to my grandparents' street to check on them.

She had found the door to the house they had been staying in open, she said, despite the freezing conditions.

With drones buzzing overhead, Lina said she had not dared look inside, but had later heard from neighbours that my grandparents had been found dead. She believed it had been the Ukrainian soldiers who visited her house who had shot them.

Another neighbour, who I will call Maria, described discovering their bodies - my grandmother in bed, covered in a blanket, and my grandfather lying on the floor in a pool of now-dried blood.

Maria also believed Ukrainian soldiers had killed them - because, she said, that she too had felt threatened by Ukrainian troops at the time.

"If they had seen me they would have killed me too."

Her theory was that my grandparents had irritated the troops in some way.

"Your grandparents caught them in a bad mood because your grandfather was always asking to be evacuated. And [the soldiers] were, you know, high. They were taking drugs. So your grandparents got in their way," she told me.

Most of the other people in the village I spoke to also believe Ukrainian soldiers killed them, though their testimony is not based on direct evidence.

The new local mayor, appointed by the Russian occupiers when they finally took control of Dachne in February, said she had been present at my grandparents' burial the following month, and said half of my grandmother's skull was missing.

These accounts of how they might have died in January 2025 were deeply disturbing, though no-one had witnessed their deaths and the information I was given could not be independently verified.

The villagers I spoke to - now living in occupied territory - did say there had been some tensions between themselves and Ukrainian troops at the time, because the soldiers had viewed those who remained in Dachne as supporters of Russia.

The clinical causes of my grandparents' deaths are unclear.

Their bodies were not exhumed for nearly 20 months, when the local authorities sent them to a morgue in the occupied region, where a basic autopsy was carried out by a forensics expert.

He told me that my grandmother had died of an explosives injury to the skull - but could not specify its cause.

As for my grandfather, it said his body had shown no obvious signs of injury.

I compared two satellite images of the house in which their bodies were found - from just before and just after when they are believed to have died - and could detect no significant damage to the building in the second image.

I also spoke to a villager who said he had buried my grandparents.

He said he did not know anything about who killed them.

"I wasn't there. But why would you kill old people?" he asked.

Anastasiya does not know if she will ever be able to give her grandparents (pictured) a dignified burial

I examined military maps from DeepStateMap.Live, an open-source Ukrainian resource, and US think tank Institute for the Study of War. They suggested Ukrainian forces still held the street where my grandparents lived during the week they had died, although the front line had been shifting rapidly.

My colleagues helped me identify the Russian military units that went on to capture Dachne. We found telephone numbers of Russian soldiers. But none of them agreed to speak to me.

A soldier from the 33rd Mechanised Brigade, who gave his name simply as Vlad, said his unit had still been in Dachne at the time my grandparents were believed to have died and that the Russians had been swiftly advancing.

Another Ukrainian soldier I spoke to also confirmed Ukraine's troops had still been in control of some parts of Dachne in late January.

We put our findings on the deaths of my grandparents to the Ukrainian military.

It said it had no verified evidence on how they died, or which implicated its servicemen during the "intense hostilities". It added that investigating allegations of wrongdoing was the role of law enforcement.

We also approached Russia's ministry of defence and Russia's embassy in London for comment - they did not respond.

Sisters Sasha (right) and Anastasiya were reunited at Belgrade Airport

The Donetsk regional prosecutor's office, which is now based outside Russian-controlled territory, has documented alleged Russian war crimes reported by eyewitnesses in villages around Dachne during December 2024 and January 2025 - including the killing of five civilians in a basement in the nearby village of Kostyantynopilske.

However, Ukrainian investigators have no equivalent cases registered in Dachne during that period. They have documented only three deaths there. My father and grandparents are not among them.

Their bodies lie in occupied territory, and I do not know if I will ever be able to say goodbye properly and bury them with dignity.

There is little more I can do for them, except one thing - mend my relationship with my sister and my mother.

Almost three years after I last saw my sister, I fly to Belgrade where she now lives.

Anastasiya (left) and her sister Sasha look through photos of their father

She is waiting for me at the airport, holding lilies, and a plate with a silly nickname on it that she gave me when we were children. We hug, cry and laugh. It feels as if something between us is finally falling back into place

At her flat, we look through family photographs and talk about my investigation and what I was able to find. My grief is still there, but it hurts a little less. I'm no longer carrying it alone.

"Are you ready?" Sasha asks. She rings our mother - who has also moved to Serbia - and puts the phone on speaker.

"Hi, Mum. It's Nastya," I say. First there is silence. Then I hear her crying. "Nastya, my darling, hello," she replies.

#65. North Korea expels Swiss diplomat in sanctions row

Switzerland is one of the few Western nations to maintain dialogue with Pyongyang

North Korea has ordered Switzerland's diplomatic representative to leave the country within 48 hours and called for the office to close, in a row over sanctions.

It followed Switzerland's decision to place sanctions on the Songdowon international children's camp in the eastern city of Wonsan, which is attended by Russian and Chinese children among other nationalities.

The camp has been accused of involvement in transferring children from Russian-occupied areas of Ukraine.

North Korea's state-run news agency KCNA called the sanctions "a blatant hostile act".

Switzerland is one of the few Western nations to maintain dialogue with Pyongyang.

Confirming the expulsion, KCNA said: "The Foreign Ministry of the Democratic People's Republic of Korea (North Korea) declared the head of the Swiss Diplomatic Office in the DPRK persona non grata and urged him to leave the country within 48 hours."

The diplomatic rebuke comes weeks after Swiss foreign minister Ignazio Cassis made a rare visit to North Korea, where he officially reopened the office and discussed normalising relations.

Swiss authorities said relations between the two countries would be maintained through the North Korean embassy in Bern, and the Swiss embassy in Beijing.

Diplomatic links with North Korea were first established in 1974 and Switzerland opened a co-operation office in Pyongyang in 1997. It was closed during the pandemic and remained shut for six years.

Switzerland's Foreign Minister Ignazio Cassis (C) and his wife Paola Cassis (R) were greeted by a North Korean official at Pyongyang Airport on 29 October

Switzerland's department of foreign affairs said the country "distinguishes between its sanctions policy and its commitment to maintaining dialogue with all countries".

It added: "The reopening of the office in Pyongyang did not in any way affect Switzerland's implementation of UN sanctions related to North Korea's nuclear programme, nor its implementation of EU sanctions targeting North Korea in the context of Russia's war against Ukraine."

Thousands of North Korean soldiers have been deployed to help support Russia's war with Ukraine.

In August, Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky accused Moscow of using North Korean missiles.

Russia using North Korean missiles to strike Ukraine, Zelensky says

North Korean weapons are killing Ukrainians. The implications are far bigger

Why a fresh wave of North Korean soldiers may fight in Russia's war in Ukraine

#61. Kim Jong Un tours nuclear material sites, urges wider use of tactical nuclear weapons

North Korea's Kim Jong Un visited sites that produce nuclear material and told his military to focus on tactical weapons, saying his country's nuclear status is irreversible.

North Korean leader Kim Jong Un toured factories that make nuclear material, along with a research institute, and pressed for the wider use of tactical nuclear weapons, state media reported on Thursday.

The visit took place on Wednesday, but the state news agency KCNA did not provide the location of the sites. Photos showed Kim walking between rows of what appeared to be centrifuges for enriching uranium. Analysts also spotted small, standardized tactical warheads such as the Hwasan-31.

Kim said North Korea's production capacity for nuclear material is being steadily expanded. He said tactical weapons should be used in different areas and for different missions, citing the danger from a hostile state on his border, referring to neighbor South Korea.

He also accused the United States of encouraging its allies to seek nuclear-powered submarines and to share nuclear weapons.

"Our possession of nuclear weapons is an absolutely irreversible choice as long as malicious forces exist that use nuclear weapons as a means of achieving hegemony," Kim said, adding that he would treat any attempt to deny North Korea's nuclear status, which was written into its constitution in 2023, as a declaration of war.

Kim said Pyongyang would not seek approval from other countries.

Lim Eul-chul, a professor at South Korea's Kyungnam University, said the visit signals Kim's desire to change the circumstances of any future talks on North Korea's weapons programs.

"This is not so much North Korea permanently closing the door to dialogue with the United States as a strategy of raising the bar: 'denuclearisation talks' are over, and if talks open in future, it will deal only on a 'nuclear state versus nuclear state' basis," he told Reuters news agency.

South Korean news agency Yonhap said this was Kim's fourth visit to a nuclear facility since September 2024. It came about a week after US President Donald Trumpvisited China for the APEC summit, where Kim did not publicly respond to an offer to meet.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Trump told the New York Post that Kim had not ignored it and that the timing was wrong.

Meanwhile, the South Korean Defense Ministry said Pyongyang must stop justifying nuclear development and implement UN Security Council resolutions prohibiting its nuclear weapons and ballistic missile programs.

#60. Angelina Jolie and Brad Pitt settle legal battle over French winery

Brad Pitt and Angelina Jolie have settled a long-running legal battle over her sale of a stake in their French vineyard, which unfolded amid a bitter divorce.

"We have settled out of court in the best interests of our family and will not be commenting further," said the Hollywood stars' representatives in a joint statement.

They bought a controlling interest in ChÃ¢teau Miraval in 2008 and wed there six years later.

In the lawsuit, Pitt, 62, argued that Jolie, 51, acted without his permission in selling her share in the winery to a Russian oligarch. She counter-sued, accusing Pitt of drunkenly attacking her and their children on a flight, prompting her to file for divorce in 2016.

The settlement announced on Wednesday resolves both of their respective legal actions.

"Any comments in media stories attributed to 'sources' are in fact not coming from either of us, or anyone close to us," said the statement from their representatives.

Pitt's lawsuit had accused Jolie of seeking "to inflict harm" on him when she sold her stake in their co-owned vineyard, which he claimed had forced him to partner with "a stranger with poisonous associations and intentions".

The Fight Club star said he and Jolie had agreed not to sell their stakes in the venue without the permission of the other.

Jolie and Pitt wed at ChÃ¢teau Miraval in 2014

It was alleged that Jolie had sold her share to a Luxembourg-based spirits manufacturer controlled by Russian oligarch Yuri Shefler, without Pitt's knowledge.

Jolie hit back with her own lawsuit, accusing Pitt of grabbing her by the head and attacking two of their children during a 2016 private jet flight trip.

Pitt also verbally abused and poured alcohol on his family during the flight from France to Los Angeles, her lawsuit had alleged.

Her lawyers alleged Pitt began shouting, accusing her of being "too deferential" to their six children - aged eight to 15 at the time.

Pitt was not charged following a police investigation into the incident and he denied the allegations.

They reached a divorce settlement late last year after an eight-year legal battle.

It is understood that Pitt's litigation with Stoli Group, a beverage company owned by the Russian oligarch, is ongoing.

Zahara Marley Jolie and Angelina Jolie at a film festival

At the time of their 2014 marriage, Pitt and Jolie were among the most lucrative names in Hollywood.

They met on the set of the 2005 film Mr and Mrs Smith and were soon dubbed Brangelina by the press.

The marriage was Pitt's second after a relationship with Friends star Jennifer Aniston.

It was Jolie's third, after actors Billy Bob Thornton and Jonny Lee Miller.

Jolie adopted her first child, Maddox, from Cambodia in 2002 a few months after he was born. Pitt later adopted him after he and Jolie had begun their relationship.

In 2005, the pair adopted daughter Zahara from Ethiopia when she was a few months old.

The couple's first biological child, Shiloh, was born in Namibia in 2006.

Their son Pax was adopted at the age of three from Vietnam in 2007, and twins Knox and Vivienne were born in 2008.

Since the couple split, four of the six children have legally changed their last name to Jolie rather than Jolie-Pitt, according to court filings citied by Hollywood press.

#59. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#55. US court blocks regulator's early review of ABC's broadcast licenses

The FCC chairman had ordered an early review of ABC's licenses a day after President Donald Trump called for the broadcaster to fire Jimmy Kimmel earlier this year. The licenses were not set to expire until 2028.

A US judge on Wednesday barred the Federal Communications Commission (FCC) ‌from ⁠continuing ⁠to take steps that threaten ABC's ​broadcast license.

In April, FCC Chairman Brendan Carr ordered an early license review for eight ABC stations, even though their licenses were not set to expire until 2028.

ABC sued, arguing that the move was retaliation for broadcasting content that angered President Donald Trump.

On Wednesday, US District Judge Loren AliKhan said the FCC ​chair appeared to have taken "marching orders" from Trump's rhetoric.

"This is not a close case," she wrote in her ruling. "No one doubts that the commission may enforce the law in the manner it sees fit. But in doing so, defendants may not retaliate against or punish plaintiffs and other broadcast license holders for disfavored speech."

Carr's order to review ABC's licenses had come a day after Trump called for ABC to fire late-night host Jimmy Kimmel over a joke he made. His show was taken off the air for six days amid pressure from the White House.

The broadcaster, which is owned by the Walt Disney Company, welcomed the ruling on Wednesday.

"Today's decision is an important victory for the First Amendment," an ABC News spokesperson said in a statement.

Meanwhile, a spokesperson for the FCC said the ruling was "wrong on the facts and wrong on the law — as controlling Supreme Court precedent makes clear."

"We will ​continue to use our lawful authority to hold broadcasters accountable to their public interest obligations," the spokesperson added.

But one dissenting opinion within the FCC came from its sole Democratic commissioner, Anna Gomez.

She said the ruling brought "badly needed accountability to a commission and an administration that have politicized the machinery of government in a sustained campaign of censorship and control."

#54. Macy's postpones Thanksgiving parade due to severe weather

New York City mayor on postponing 100th Macyâs Thanksgiving Day Parade

Macy's has postponed its 100th annual Thanksgiving Day Parade in New York to Saturday because of severe weather.

Preparations to hold the iconic holiday celebration, known for its massive balloons that float among the city's skyscrapers, had already begun when the forecast for heavy rain and strong winds on Thursday led to the delay.

The last time the parade was suspended was during World War Two, but it has never been postponed due to weather. In 1971, Macy's decided to ground the balloons, which often feature beloved characters from pop culture, because of weather.

A powerful coastal storm is set to affect the New York area in coming days.

The balloons will be deflated again and then re-inflated for Saturday

On Wednesday night, New York Governor Kathy Hochul declared a state of emergency for Long Island, New York City and Westchester County

The potent nor'easter is expected to make it too windy and dangerous for the iconic balloons to fly.

Macy's had expected to put 100 inflatables into the sky to mark its 100th parade and feature 10 new balloons, including one depicting the animated dog Bluey wearing a crown and another showing a Minion character riding a banana like a horse.

"While we are heartbroken that our parade was postponed, the safety of our partners, the safety of our participants and spectators is, and always will be, our top priority," said Will Cross, the parade's executive producer, during a news conference on Wednesday with city officials.

Cross also said the giant balloons would be deflated on Wednesday night and stored at a facility in neighbouring New Jersey until they were reinflated for the parade on Saturday.

Macy's confirmed the new parade date and updated a countdown clock on its website to reflect the Saturday schedule. Meanwhile, broadcaster NBC has said it will show the 2025 parade on Thursday.

New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani, who will not attend the parade on Saturday because of a scheduled surgery, said at the news conference that "you have to plan for every possibility" and confirmed that "extreme weather has forced" officials to make the change.

"We know that this is an inconvenience for many who come to visit our city. This decision, however, was made with safety being paramount," he said. "We want to ensure that we're delivering a parade that's not only incredible and spectacular, but one also that doesn't come at the expense of anyone's safety."

Mamdani also said that there would be an expanded police presence along the parade route on Saturday and that there were "no known specific or credible threats to the parade at this time".

Cross added that Macy's is working through what costs it could cover as a result of the delay and said the company is in contact with bands, performers and others who travelled to the city for the parade.

BBC Weather's Elizabeth Rizzini looks at the weather that will impact travel over US Thanksgiving.

During some past parades, strong winds and inclement weather have led to the tearing and deflation of the balloons as well as collisions with lampposts that caused injuries.

The US National Weather Service has issued a high wind warning, valid from 10:00 local time (15:00 GMT) on Thursday until 01:00 (06:00 GMT) on Friday with gusts potentially reaching 60mph (97km/h) - although the very strongest winds are likely to occur after the parade was due to end.

Heavy rain and coastal flooding will pose additional hazards across the eastern US as the storm sweeps through, while some inland parts could also see snow, BBC Weather reports.

The governor of the neighbouring state of New Jersey on Wednesday declared a state of emergency across the state's 21 counties, beginning at 18:00 local time (23:00 GMT).

Governor Mikie Sherrill's office said the powerful coastal storm is expected to bring damaging winds, heavy rainfall, and severe tidal flooding through Friday, with coastal and bayside communities facing the greatest risk. Sherrill urged residents to stay off the roads as much as possible once the storm arrives.

Originally called the Macy's Christmas Parade, the celebration, which also features live performers, debuted in 1924 as a way to boost holiday sales. It was founded by Macy's employees and their families.

From 1942 to 1944 New York went without the parade as the US directed materials like rubber and helium toward the country's war effort.

The 2.5mi (4km) procession route begins at Macy's Herald Square flagship store and concludes in Manhattan's Upper West Side, with performers and spectators lining the streets to greet Santa Claus and ring in the holiday season.

Country artist Megan Moroney and Grammy nominee Teddy Swims were slated to perform at the flagship store, but it is unclear whether they, along with the dozens of celebrities and float performers on the roster, would be returning on Saturday.

#50. Trump's accounts traded Nvidia shares 44 times this year, records show

Donald Trump's investment portfolios have bought or sold shares in chipmaker Nvidia at least 44 times this year, a review of the US president's financial disclosures shows.

Trump's most recent public declarations of his investment trading show that between January and September the portfolios purchased between $5.2m and $17m (Â£4m and Â£13m) in Nvidia stock - and sold between $4.4m and $12m. His portfolios held between $12.5m and $58m of Nvidia shares at the end of 2025.

Financial disclosures to the Office of Government Ethics report a low and high range of transaction values, so a more precise analysis of how much Nvidia stock was traded is not possible.

The period of recent turmoil at Nvidia and volatility in its share price is not covered by the disclosures. In the last two weeks, co-founder and chief executive Jensen Huang resigned and then Trump played a significant part in his reinstatement.

The White House has said that Trump's stock and bond portfolio is independently managed by third parties and neither he nor his family "has any ability to direct, influence or provide input regarding how the portfolio is invested or when investments are bought or sold".

Huang resigned on 12 November after reports of extramarital affairs, including with one woman who has alleged ties to Chinese intelligence. Nvidia's share price dived following news of his resignation.

But on Monday Nvidia's board announced the 63-year-old was being reinstated as CEO.

Trump told US media he had "made the deal" to "convince" the tech giant's board to bring Huang back. The board said it had "received input from senior levels of the United States government" that he posed "no national security risk".

Nvidia's share price surged 31% on Monday following Huang's return. About $1tn (Â£763bn) was added to Nvidia's market value, but the company's shares are still trading below their value before reports of Huang's alleged affairs began to circulate.

However, concerns remain among both Republican and Democratic politicians about Huang's actions and Nvidia's compliance with US controls over the export of advanced computer chips to China, as well as Trump's involvement in getting him reappointed as CEO.

Nvidia's chip technology and investments have been critical to the growth of the artificial intelligence (AI) industry, leading to it becoming one of the world's most valuable companies.

The firm now represents more than 6% of the S&P 500 share index, according to State Street financial services. This makes it the second most valuable company on the index after Apple, whose shares Trump's accounts also frequently trade.

Nvidia also ranks highly in many global indices, meaning that investment accounts held around the world, including pensions, are likely to include its shares.

Our analysis of Trump's Nvidia trades does not include other investment products held by him which may include its shares.

Trump made at least $2.2bn (Â£1.7bn) from a variety of investments and commercial activities, according to the financial disclosure report for the first year of Trump's second term.

Barbara Perry, a presidential historian at the University of Virginia's Miller Center, told the BBC there is "no precedent for this," adding: "It's beyond anything we've ever seen in the presidency."

#46. FedEx plane overshoots Miami runway, with flights briefly suspended at airport

FedEx aircraft FedEx plane sends dirt flying while overshooting Miami runway dirt in the air while overshooting Miami runway

The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) has halted flights bound for Miami International Airport in Florida after a FedEx aircraft overran a runway on Wednesday evening.

Departures heading to Miami were grounded just after 20:15 local time (01:15 GMT) and all but one runway at the airport was closed, according to the FAA advisory. Flights are expected to resume by around 22:00 local time, the FAA told the BBC.

The airliner that ran past the runway arrived from Memphis. No injuries have been reported. The FAA is investigating the incident.

The ground stop comes on the day before Thanksgiving, one of the busiest travel days of the year in the US.

Flight delays in Miami reached one hour but appeared to be declining later Wednesday night.

Passengers scheduled to travel on Wednesday night were encouraged to check directly with their airlines for the latest flight updates before heading to the airport.

"We appreciate our passengers' patience as we work to safely resume normal operations," Miami-Dade Aviation Department spokesman Greg Chin said in a statement to the BBC.

The airport is among the top five domestic destinations for travelers in the country over this holiday week, according to the American Automobile Association.

The Miami-Dade area is under a flood watch until late Wednesday night. Periods of heavy rain throughout the day are expected to cause flooding, according to the US National Weather Service.

A nationwide ground stop earlier this month halted flights across the US for hours - the longest such event since the terror attacks on 11 September 2001.

#43. Can Rassvet, 'Russia's Starlink' change the course of the war in Ukraine?

SpaceX's US Starlink satellite system has played a key role in countering the Russian military in Ukraine, but now a Russian counterpart is under development. What could this mean for Ukraine in future?

Both Russian and international media refer to the satellite communications network Rassvet (Dawn) as the "Russian Starlink," a satellite internet service operated by the US company SpaceX. Rassvet, which was developed by the Russian aerospace company Bureau 1440, operates in low Earth orbit. Interest in the system surged after the Russian military lost access to Starlink terminals, which until then had ensured communications and internet access in the battle zone in Ukraine. On the front, for example, Starlink helps drone pilots and commanders receive images from drones equipped with cameras. Military personnel also communicate together thanks to Starlink.

Though Russia does not fall within Starlink's area of coverage, the Russian military managed to acquire "illegal" terminals through third countries and was thus using the same system as the Ukrainian army.

In early 2026, SpaceX, in coordination with the Ukrainian Defense Ministry, introduced a "whitelist," which registered all devices on Ukrainian territory authorized to operate on the Starlink network. Any terminals being used by Russian forces were subsequently deactivated and reports of serious communication problems on the front then began to appear in Russian social media channels.

In May, the US news channel Bloomberg reported that the Ukrainian army had recaptured about 400 square kilometers (ca. 155 square miles) of Ukrainian territory since Russian forces lost access to Starlink.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Two first launches of serial Rassvet satellites took place in March and July 2026. According to information from Ukraine's military intelligence (HUR) published by the Reuters news agency in early November, Russia has started using Rassvet for military communications over Ukraine and to carry out attacks. The system is reportedly being used to improve strike accuracy, expand capabilities of reconnaissance drones and to strengthen resilience against electronic warfare.

HUR spokesperson Andriy Yusov also told Reuters that "the constellation is currently insufficient to provide uninterrupted coverage of Ukrainian territory."

Georgiy Trishkin, a rocket analyst and operator of Telegram channel Tekhassky Vestnik (The Texas Herald), told DW that Russia aims to have a functioning "sovereign" alternative to Starlink — consisting of 292 satellites in orbit — running by the end of 2027.

According to Serhii Beskrestnov, aka Flash, an advisor to the Ukrainian president, the main danger posed by the Rassvet system is that it could allow the Russian army to control drones over Ukrainian territory. He also pointed out on the platform Facebook that the Ukrainian military cannot use Starlink to control drones over Russian territory, since the US service does not work there.

Inspired by the Starlink model, the Russian military also wants to be able to equip drones with terminals, which will enable connectivity in more remote parts of Ukrainian territory. Bureau 1440 unveiled some initial models last month, but according to Trishkin, the similarities between Rassvet and Starlink stop at the "basic features and concept."

"When it comes to details, these are completely different projects. No one can currently match SpaceX's technological edge — and especially its lead in production technology," he said, pointing out that while the Starlink constellation comprised over 10,000 satellites, Rassvet would not have more than 300 in its first phase.

He added that Bureau 1440 would struggle to make enough of the kits used on the ground. While SpaceX can manufacture about 15,000 per day, this would be a "remarkable achievement even over the course of a year" for the Russian company. He also said that the terminals produced by Bureau 1440 were much more expensive than those by Starlink as they relied "on foreign components."

"Right now, no one can beat SpaceX in terms of price, volume and quality of service," Trishkin said. "The next step for the company is mobile satellite services, where it's making very rapid progress. Will Bureau 1440 be able to respond? Unlikely for the time being."

SpaceX's Starlink was primarily designed as a civilian service, while Starshield is more for government and military sectors. According to DW's sources, Rassvet was likely designed to support battlefield communications and thus does not currently require as many satellites as Starlink. Ukrainian military expert Oleh Starikov said that if Rassvet continues to develop as it has then it could fulfil the mission put to it by the Russian military. He added that SpaceX CEO Elon Musk was developing a system that would work around the world, whereas Bureau 1440's satellites sufficed for Russian military purposes.

But he also underlined the massive expansion of Russia's satellite network generally, which — on January 1, 2022, before the full-scale invasion of Ukraine — comprised 102 satellites according to Dmitry Rogozin, who at the time was general director of the Russian space agency Roscosmos. In September of this year, Rogozin's successor Dmitry Bakanov said that the number almost quadrupled.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Recent restrictions to Starlink services in Ukraine have brought the issue to the fore again. In mid-November, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said that Starshield had been throttled in Ukraine. "Unfortunately, our units have been recording significant disruptions to Starshield's operations over the territory of Ukraine for several days now — along the entire front line and in the temporarily occupied territories of Ukraine," he said, adding that the restrictions occurred at speeds of 161 km/h and higher.

Restrictions on the use of Starlink were first put in place on October 14 and then lifted, according to US President Donald Trump, on October 23.

When asked by DW whether the Ukrainian military could gain access to Rassvet, Trishkin said it was "only a matter of time" before terminals fell "into foreign hands and are subsequently examined."

This article was originally published in Russian.

#40. California warns of flooding as King Tides peak over Thanksgiving

High king tides threaten California coastline, flood communities

Coastal areas of California are bracing for potential flooding as king tides peak in the state during the US Thanksgiving holiday.

Water levels across the US state's vast coastline could reach over 8ft (2.4 metres) in some areas on Wednesday and Thursday, according to the National Weather Service (NWS) - breaking records set just last month.

The NWS has warned parts of California could face major coastal flooding, which is likely to be significant in low-lying areas. There have already been some reports of flooding.

Meanwhile, storms are also forecast for the Pacific Northwest, including the Canadian province of British Columbia, and eastern parts of the US.

Portions of a pier in northern California came crashing down into the ocean on Wednesday as high tides and strong waves battered the structure, video showed.

Beachgoers have been advised to stay away from the water's edge as large waves could suddenly form. Such waves could be damaging to piers and jetties, and could capsize small vessels, the NWS warned.

Flooding has already been reported in the San Francisco Bay Area.

In San Diego, a high tide of 8 ft 11in was recorded on Wednesday morning local time - potentially breaking the record for highest tide measured in the area, which was just set late last month at 7.98ft.

In Los Angeles, the tide on Wednesday was thought to be as high 8.19 - the record for the city that was set only weeks ago 27 October when parts of the California coast experienced flooding.

Recently set high tide records were also broken in Santa Barbara and La Jolla, according to preliminary data.

King tides are the highest predicted tide of the year and are above the highest water level reached at high tide on an average day.

While they typically occur once or twice a year in most coastal areas, they are happening more frequently in some as sea levels rise. These king tides are being strengthened by the strongest El NiÃ±o weather pattern on record.

Storm systems are also expected to impact the US through the Thanksgiving holiday - one affecting the Pacific Northwest and another the eastern part of the country, the US National Weather Service said.

On the east coast, a developing Nor'easter storm is expected to bring heavy rain, strong winds, and coastal flooding.

Major impacts are expected to begin on Wednesday across the southeast, and move northward Wednesday night and on Thursday, the NWS said.

"Those along the eastern seaboard can expect gusty, possibly damaging winds, heavy rain, and major coastal and marine impacts," it added.

The developing storm system could bring about one to four inches rain along America's east coast and a peak of four to six inches in coastal North Carolina.

The US department store chain Macy's has postponed its 100th annual Thanksgiving Day Parade in New York, which was due to take place on Thursday, until Saturday because of severe weather - the first time this has happened.

The stormy weather on both coasts could disrupt travel for millions of people.

The American Automobile Association (AAA) has predicted that 81.5 million Americans will travel at least 50 miles (80km) from home for Thanksgiving between Tuesday 24 November and Monday 30 November.

Officials have also issued a flood warning along Vancouver's coastline in Canada's most western province of British Columbia.

Residents were urged to take "immediate action to protect life and property", a weather warning from Environment and Climate Change Canada said.

King tides were expected to potentially increase already high water levels there too.
