#37. US Army says controlled blast of explosives in Iowa was 'huge success'

Smoke appears after explosion heard in Iowa near Army detonation site

The US military has hailed as a "huge success" the detonation of nearly five tonnes of toxic explosives in Iowa, an operation that sparked fears of health and environmental problems across the region.

Smoke was seen rising into the sky shortly after 09:00 local time (14:00 GMT) following the explosion, as the Army declared the risk "as close to zero as is absolutely humanly possible".

Plans to detonate the lead azide, a volatile compound reportedly at risk of exploding on its own, were paused several times before the blast following public outcry and legal challenges.

On Wednesday evening, the Army said that an "all clear" has been issued around the ammunition plant.

President Donald Trump said shortly after the blast that reports showed mitigation efforts "far exceeded expectations".

"The Army's controlled, mitigated detonation exceeded all expectations and was a HUGE success," Adam Telle, acting secretary of the US Army, wrote on social media.

Despite widespread concern from residents of Middletown, Iowa, ahead of the blast, officials have said that lead readings in the area so far have been promising.

In a Wednesday evening update on X, the Army said that the Federal Emergency Management Agency (Fema) had issued the "all clear" around the plant and that there was no airborne lead detection following the detonation.

"Post-detonation surface sampling is underway, and results will be released once complete," the Army added.

Environmental Protection Agency administrator Lee Zeldin also said on social media that in the hours after the detonation "all EPA air monitors continue to report a ZERO DETECT of airborne lead".

The explosives had been in storage for around 20 years, and degraded as part of a process known as recrystallisation - this made them more responsive to accidental detonation from friction or other movement.

No level of lead exposure is considered safe, with children and pregnant women particularly vulnerable to harmful effects.

Middletown Mayor Rodney Johnson told the BBC he felt "relieved" after the detonation.

He watched the blast from his job at a manufacturing plant eight miles (12km) away from the blast - and monitored a camera at his two-bedroom home just one mile from the detonation site.

"We did not feel anything. We did not see anything," Johnson said.

Also eight miles away from Middletown, Sandy Mabeus closed the windows and the garage at her home, brought their outdoor cat inside and hunkered down.

The family watched three separate live streams on iPads and televisions of the explosion as it happened.

"We did not feel or hear a thing here," she told the BBC, adding that she checked the videos to confirm it had actually happened.

"The waiting and wondering has not been a good thing for anybody in the area," she said. "Hopefully they had a very good result and we can just get on with things."

Iowa farmer David Vandenberg lives about 20 miles from the blast site and said he first learned of the detonation around two weeks ago. The Army Corps of Engineers took samples from his yard and pond on Tuesday.

"I was listening this morning, and I did hear a rumble about nine o'clock," he told the BBC on Wednesday, but didn't see or smell anything.

Vandenberg expects officials to return to take more samples but "was happy to see that the blast was relatively contained. "I think the effects will be minimal"

Illinois Governor JB Pritzker said on X that his state was "seeing little to no impact" from the blast. In a press release, he said he was directing state agencies to remain on standby for any "unanticipated impact".

The Iowa site is only 10 miles (16 km) from the Illinois border.

Iowa's incoming Governor-elect Rob Sand said there was "more testing to do" and called for "immediate transparency" to explain why the lead-up was "so chaotic and unclear".

Sand said that any Iowans who have been impacted in any way "must be taken care of fully, right away".

Cameron Hamilton, the Fema administrator, said there would be additional resources and testing in the coming days and weeks.

In recent weeks, the US Army scheduled and rescheduled their detonation ahead of colder winter temperatures.

It also warned locals of "potential exposure to toxic lead dust", which led to some panic. It instituted measures including an optional evacuation zone and a shelter-in-place advisory ahead of the detonation.

The Army faced pushback from residents and politicians alike and there were legal challenges to prevent the detonation from going ahead on environmental and health grounds.

But on Monday, the appeals court overturned a decision to halt the blast. It said that - while these concerns were valid - the risk from an unplanned explosion was greater.

Sheila Flynn and Kwasi Gyamfi Asiedu contributed to this report

#33. US stocks fall further as fears over AI intensify

Big technology firms connected to AI continued to see share prices fall on Wednesday, with stock in Nvidia and Anthropic dropping sharply in early trading.

The surge in Nvidia shares after boss Jensen Huang was reinstated was temporary, and the general stock sell-off continued, with all three main US stock market indices down by closing time. The tech-dominated Nasdaq closed 0.6% lower.

The declines came amid concerns over the level and sustainability of AI investment, which has rattled investors in recent weeks.

Shares in other major tech firms, including Amazon, Tesla and Microsoft closed the day lower, though Meta and SpaceX were in positive territory by the end of the trading day.

Nvidia's share price was down 8% in early trading on Wednesday, extending Tuesday's slide as the boost from Huang's return eased. At close, it ended up 3.4% lower.

The resignation and subsequent reinstatement of Nvidia's CEO after revelations of his extramarital affairs has seen the market value of the world's premier AI chipmaker fluctuate wildly.

Given Nvidia's position as both supplier and financier of many other firms involved in the AI rollout, its rocky fortnight has had a wider impact.

The US government also announced a raft of new tariffs on countries and trading partners on Wednesday as part of President Donald Trump's flagship trade policy.

The value of big tech companies have sheltered the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs.

But share prices in recent weeks have been volatile in response to the US midterm elections and other events in the AI industry.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

Investors have taken fright at Democrat gains in the US midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms and even a moratorium on the development of new data centres.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will eventually pay off.

"It seems that these companies are trying to one-up each other on the amount of money that they're spending, but you have to show that it's worth it, and that's where Wall Street is saying I don't know," said Todd Schoenberger, chief investment officer at CrossCheck Management.

Despite concerns, investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, which said the technology will remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

Shares in Anthropic continued to trade below the $83 they debuted at last week, while Amazon shares fell by just over 3%.

The declines come ahead of the US stock market closing for Thanksgiving on Thursday before opening for a shortened session on Friday.

#31. How Chinese companies circumvent chips sanctions in Southeast Asia

High-tech AI chips are hard to get in China because of US restrictions. To get around this, Chinese companies often use offshore computing capacity, particularly in Southeast Asia. The US is also trying to stop it.

Some €510 billion ($559 billion) have been invested into AI infrastructure across Malaysia since February 2025. The consulting group Fitch Solutions has opened 125 data centers in Malaysia to serve the global digital economy. The sector is booming in the western Malaysian state of Johor where, according to Malaysia's Digital Ministry, 16 of 27 new data centers are located.

The government in Kuala Lumpur hopes the investment will create numerous new jobs, up to 90% of which are expected to be filled by locals. The advantages of the data center sites include low electricity prices for energy-intensive computing and good links to international submarine cables.

Major US technology companies have also set up along the Strait of Malacca. Amazon and Oracle have each invested $6 billion, while Microsoft and Google have invested $2 billion apiece. In early November, Google's parent company, Alphabet, announced additional investments of $15 billion.

"Malaysia's vision is not only to build an economy that uses artificial intelligence, but also one that uses AI creatively,” explained Digital Minister Gobind Singh Deo in a statement that was tellingly published in Chinese, which is not an official language of Malaysia.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Data center growth in Southeast Asia is part of the race for technological supremacy in artificial intelligence. The US has restricted the export of high-performance chips, such as those produced by US tech giant Nvidia, to China. It has also tried to block the sale of the advanced lithography machines used to manufacture microchips that are made by Dutch manufacturer ASML.

Since Chinese chips have yet to reach the same technological standards as others, some Chinese companies are testing an alternative model in that they legally purchase or lease capacity in other countries. They can thus rent access to US chips that have been installed in systems in Malaysia, for example, and develop their AI models from abroad.

In October, the Institute for Progress (IFP), an independent think tank in Washington, warned that Southeast Asia was becoming an offshore hub for Chinese AI enclaves. It estimated that by the end of the year about 24% of China's total computing power came from remote access to overseas data centers, three-quarters of which were in Southeast Asia. This was predicted to rise to 31% by the end of 2027.

The IFP said that though "US AI chips hosted in overseas data centers are a significant source of compute for China," they were "difficult to size."

It explained that "Chinese entities can rent GPUs [graphics processing units] as a service from cloud providers or lease data center capacity overseas, directly from developers or through intermediaries. Cloud providers and data center developers rarely name their customers or disclose how much capacity each uses, and Chinese companies disclose little themselves."

The Chinese giant Alibaba Cloud currently operates six data centers in Malaysia — three in Johor and three in the greater Kuala Lumpur region, with the most recent one opening on November 19.

The company has invested $53 billion in data centers in total, including in South Korea, Thailand, the Philippines and Mexico, as well as Malaysia. TikTok's parent company also operates data center capacity in Johor.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In early October, Tencent, another multinational technology conglomerate, which is based in Shenzhen, signed a five-year $7 billion contract with Oracle, allowing it to use computing capacity equivalent to 100,000 modern AI chips for training artificial intelligence. The company was supposed to pay 30% upfront.

The deal fell through a few weeks later, however, and both companies have remained silent about the reasons. According to media reports, there was pressure from the governments in both Beijing and Washington. China wants to develop its own chip industry, whereas high-ranking US officials reportedly raised security concerns with Oracle.

In mid-2025, the US threatened to impose an additional 25% tariff on Malaysia imports "to prevent circumvention of export controls on chips," according to the Chinese consulting firm Equal Ocean. "The Malaysian government quickly responded by opposing illegal resale practices and introducing licensing requirements for the transshipment of certain high-end chips."

Washington is now stepping up its efforts. On the same day that US President Donald Trump met with his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping at the APEC meeting in Shenzhen, China last week, the Bureau of Industry and Security, an agency of the US Department of Commerce, issued new rules with regard to remote access to advanced AI chips and servers.

It said that the goal was "to prevent certain foreign actors from leveraging advanced computing power based on US technology to threaten US national security and foreign policy interests." The restrictions pertain to data centers that provide significant computing capacity to companies with ties to countries in "Country Group D:5 or Macau."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

According to US export regulations, the D:5 group includes China, Russia, Iran and North Korea and others. In future, companies will have to conduct more thorough checks to identify the ownership and control structures behind their clients.

Washington has also introduced new licensing requirements for advanced semiconductors destined for Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand and Brazil. Subsidiaries of US companies are exempt.

"The US is not only reviewing which countries will receive AI chips in future but also wants to have a say in where the chips are used, who operates them and which customers they serve," explained Chinese industry expert Xu Sanlang. "This means that data centers, which have been providing remote computing capacity to Chinese clients will have to comply with these restrictions in future."

This article was originally written in German.

#29. New US tariffs: 'Trump lashing out really indiscriminately', expert says

The United States on Wednesday unveiled proposed tariffs on 14 economies including the European Union and China, after a months-long probe into alleged excess production. It's not a step in that direction, we have President Trump lashing out really indiscriminately. It's very hard to see his approach being very productive".

#28. Israeli judges recommend dropping most serious charges against Netanyahu

Benjamin Netanyahu called for his trial to be ended "immediately"

Judges in Israel have suggested prosecutors drop bribery charges against Benjamin Netanyahu, potentially handing the caretaker prime minister a boost as he fights for his political survival.

The judges told the attorney general that there was little chance of conviction in the first of three cases he is facing, in which he is accused of granting government favours in exchange for positive media coverage.

They also questioned whether his actions amounted to criminal conduct in a second corruption case, and suggested prosecutors seek a plea deal in a third.

Netanyahu has consistently denied the allegations against him and called on Wednesday for the trial to be ended "immediately".

"Ten years of terrible suffering for me, my family, my son," he said in a televised statement.

"Ten years of incitement to hatred that wounds the soul."

Netanyahu was first charged in 2019, but court proceedings have been prolonged in part by the recent Israel-Gaza war.

The case now under serious doubt, known as Case 4,000, concerns an alleged "reciprocal arrangement" between Netanyahu and the controlling shareholder in Israel's biggest telecoms company. Both denied any wrongdoing.

In a highly unusual step, a judging panel at the Jerusalem District Court summoned Israeli Attorney General Gali Baharav-Miara on Wednesday to express their concerns.

"The chances of conviction are low â to an extent that warrants reconsidering the matter," Judge Rivka Friedman-Feldman told her, Israeli media reported.

The judges also cast doubt on whether Netanyahu's actions, which are subject to a second case â concerning another alleged quid-pro-quo with another media baron, known as Case 2,000 â met the threshold for criminality.

While they left open the possibility of a conviction in the third case: Case 1,000, which alleges Netanyahu acted for the benefit of an Australian billionaire after being gifted cigars and champagne by him.

Netanyahu: What are the corruption charges?

However, the judges told the attorney general that, in the public interest, there needed to be a "rethink" in how the trial should move forward, Israeli media report.

Baharav-Miara said she would consider the judges' recommendations.

It has been six years since Netanyahu became Israel's first sitting prime minister to face a criminal trial.

He has always claimed to be the victim of a political witch-hunt and said on Wednesday that the "fabricated cases" against him were now falling apart.

The cases have continued to progress in court while Netanyahu remained in power throughout the war in Gaza, though judges agreed to reduce the number of hearings due to security concerns around the conflict.

The accusations against him have rocked Israeli politics, fuelling already deep divisions. His trial has coincided with controversial, far-reaching judiciary reforms by Netanyahu's government.

For his opponents, Netanyahu's fear of prison has distorted his decision-making as prime minister and made him especially dependent on his far-right coalition partners to remain in power.

This latest development will give a boost to Netanyahu, whose political opponent Gadi Eisenkot is currently attempting to form a coalition government after an inconclusive general election last month.

While Netanyahu's parliamentary bloc won more seats than that of his rival, both fell short of an outright majority. Israel's president has offered Eisenkot the first chance to form a new administration.

Netanyahu remains subject to an arrest warrant from the International Criminal Court for alleged war crimes and crimes against humanity in Gaza, which he also denies.

#25. Has global jihadism failed, or is it simply changing shape?

Has global jihadism failed, or is it simply changing shape?

Rice, pasta, sugar, cooking oil and beans are hardly what you'd expect to see in jihadist propaganda. Yet these were among the supplies that al-Qaeda's Somali branch, al-Shabab, said its "charity" had handed out to hundreds of struggling families in August, as it publicised the aid through its social media accounts and radio station.

The group, which controls large parts of rural southern and central Somalia, has been keen to show off its so-called humanitarian work throughout 2026.

"May God reward them," said a local woman in one of al-Shabab's videos.

It's all part of an effort to win over local communities and present itself as more than just a jihadist group.

Al-Shabab has been publicising its humanitarian work

A quarter of a century after al-Qaeda's 9/11 attacks in the US, and 12 years after Islamic State group (IS) declared its so-called caliphate in Iraq and Syria, the jihadist movement looks very different.

Rather than focusing on attacking the West or fighting for a global Islamic state, some - especially those linked to al-Qaeda - are putting more effort into winning power closer to home. And they seem increasingly willing to make compromises along the way.

Because of this, a debate is growing within the movement. While hardliners reject compromise, pragmatists see working with local groups and former enemies as a way to gain power. But do the pragmatists represent a genuine change or simply a shift in tactics to achieve the same ultimate goals: establishing a state that applies their strict interpretation of Islamic Sharia law both at home and in its dealings with the outside world?

Neither al-Qaeda nor IS has achieved its ultimate dream: taking over one or more countries and ruling them according to their strict interpretation of Islam.

Both have suffered major defeats, lost leaders and faced years of international pressure. IS, which once controlled vast areas of Iraq and Syria, lost its last territorial stronghold in 2019. But jihadism hasn't gone away.

Africa is now the main battleground for jihadism, with al-Qaeda's powerful branches, JNIM in the Sahel (Mali, Burkina Faso and Niger) and al-Shabab in Somalia, fighting long-running insurgencies. IS, its bitter rival, has active branches in Nigeria, the Democratic Republic of Congo, the Sahel, Mozambique and Somalia.

One major source of inspiration, and envy, whether jihadists admit it or not, is Syria's new president, Ahmed al-Sharaa. The former IS and then al-Qaeda commander was once wanted by the US but rose to power in Syria in December 2024 and was welcomed by Western leaders.

Once known by his militant nom de guerre Abu Mohammed al-Jolani, he led the jihadist group Nusra Front - initially linked to IS and then al-Qaeda - and later the group Hayat Tahrir al-Sham (HTS).

He gradually broke ties with both IS and al-Qaeda and distanced himself from their calls for global jihad.

Instead, he focused on Syria, building a local power base in the north-western Idlib province.

What surprised everyone was his growing willingness to work with non-Islamist groups and engage with foreign powers. As HTS advanced on Damascus, its leadership sought to reassure the international community, reaching out even to Russia, a key ally of then-president Bashar al-Assad, to allay fears of an Islamist takeover.

That pragmatism, combined with favourable geopolitical conditions - including a weaker Russia and Iran - helped him take power in Syria.

But the sight of him being welcomed by world leaders, including US President Donald Trump, was even more remarkable.

Trump joked with Sharaa when he visited the White House

During Sharaa's visit to the White House in November 2025, Trump sprayed him with his own branded cologne, jokingly asked how many wives he had and praised him as a "very strong leader".

For some jihadists, Sharaa's success has been inspiring. Others, however, see his actions as a betrayal of everything they believe in.

IS has declared jihad against Syria's new Islamist government, branding Sharaa and his officials "apostates", and has carried out attacks against government forces on that basis. Other hardline clerics have taken a similar stance, but have stopped short of openly calling for jihad against Sharaa.

The divide between hardline jihadists and their more pragmatic counterparts has fuelled a bitter online debate since 2025, with rival voices trading accusations of betrayal and even publishing competing books to defend their positions.

Sharaa, centre, in 2016, was once a jihadist leader wanted by the FBI

For hardliners, going too far can mean abandoning their core beliefs. This might include holding back from imposing their strict version of Islamic law, accepting "man-made" laws or a constitution, taking part in elections, working with non-Islamist groups, or building ties with governments they consider "infidel".

Another source of inspiration for jihadists was the Taliban's return to power in Afghanistan in 2021, as it showed that an Islamist movement could eventually take control of a country after years of fighting Western-backed forces. It engaged local groups, slowly secured power across the country, used international diplomacy, and bided its time before taking the capital, Kabul, in a lightning offensive.

Two groups are especially worth watching: al-Qaeda's branches in Mali and Somalia.

In late April, al-Qaeda's Sahel branch, known as JNIM, showed considerable pragmatism by joining forces with nationalist Tuareg separatists - whom it had occasionally fought - to launch a major offensive that threatened Mali's military rulers. It even went as far as to call the Tuaregs "brothers".

But it's not just JNIM's growing military strength that's attracting attention. It's also how the group is presenting itself.

Its propaganda once prominently featured al-Qaeda's leaders, slogans and calls for global jihad. Since late 2024, however, its videos have dropped the familiar al-Qaeda branding.

And instead of talking about global Muslim causes, JNIM has increasingly focused on local problems, portraying itself as a defender of ordinary people against corrupt and abusive rulers.

It has repeatedly accused Mali's military government and its Russian allies of killing civilians and making people's lives worse.

In April, it appealed to "all sincere patriots" to help build a "new Mali", promising security, justice and prosperity alongside Islamic law.

It has even suggested it could have "balanced relations" with Russia, despite the fact they're fighting Russian forces backing Mali's government. Its wording echoed that of HTS to Russia as it advanced on Damascus.

There are parallels with Sharaa's approach in Syria: build local alliances, reassure potential enemies and avoid giving outside powers a reason to intervene.

Africa security specialist Aman Bezreh sees similarities between the two groups, but says JNIM's shift may take time.

"JNIM has indeed shown signs of pragmatism similar to HTS", she says. "However, its rebranding has progressed more slowly because the group is simultaneously focused on expanding into new territories and recruiting from diverse pools."

The debate over which path to follow is also taking place within JNIM's leadership, according to Wassim Nasr, a France 24 journalist and jihadism expert.

He says one camp favours the Taliban's approach of taking power through force with minimal concessions. The other, which he says currently has the upper hand, favours an approach closer to that of Syria's HTS - making compromises and building wider alliances to gain power more quickly.

Nasr believes JNIM may borrow from both models, but that a break with al-Qaeda is inevitable as a result of its political evolution.

Like JNIM, al-Shabab in Somalia appears willing to make deals with former enemies.

According to Abdullahi Guji, BBC Monitoring's Somalia specialist, al-Shabab reached a truce in October with a powerful Somali tribe, the Ma'awisley, whose fighters had been at the forefront of the fight against the group.

The tribe has since withdrawn its fighters from the front line, dealing a serious blow to the government's campaign against jihadists, says Guji.

Guji believes the group may soon have a chance to test whether it can seize power and establish itself as an alternative government across wider parts of the country.

African Union forces are withdrawing from Somalia amid funding cuts and the approaching end of their mandate in December. Meanwhile, the Somali government is facing political crises and rebellions, creating vulnerabilities al-Shabab could exploit, he adds.

There's another reason some jihadists may see an opportunity to expand: they believe Western governments have bigger fish to fry.

With the war in Ukraine, US-Iran conflict in the Middle East and growing rivalries with China and Russia, fighting jihadists is no longer the priority it was after 9/11.

The US withdrawal from Afghanistan in 2021 and the Taliban's return to power, followed by Sharaa's rise in Syria, have reinforced that belief.

Colin Clarke, executive director of the Soufan Center, a non-profit research group that focuses on security, says some jihadist groups have learnt that avoiding confrontation with the US can help them escape Western military action and "live to fight another day".

That doesn't mean the West has stopped fighting terrorism. But if jihadists believe it is distracted or less willing to intervene, they may feel emboldened to pursue power.

Unlike its fiercely uncompromising rival IS, al-Qaeda has long shown some flexibility in pursuing its goals. But history offers a warning.

After the 2011 Arab uprisings, which largely sidelined al-Qaeda, affiliated jihadist groups in Yemen, Libya and Tunisia began using the name Ansar al-Sharia (Supporters of Sharia) to win local support without carrying the al-Qaeda brand.

In Mali in 2012, al-Qaeda leaders advised jihadists who had seized the north alongside Tuareg separatists not to rush into imposing strict Islamic rules. Instead, they urged them to work with local allies, avoid alienating communities and play down their al-Qaeda links.

The message wasn't to give up their ambitions. It was to show strategic patience until the time was right. That same thinking could be at work today.

Democrats have an identity crisis - it won't be an easy fix

Russia's mega-rich are already imagining a future after Putin

Europe's youth is on the march - but does anything actually unite them?

So are these groups really changing their ambitions, or just their tactics? Even those advocating compromise aren't necessarily abandoning their dream of establishing a state ruled by their strict interpretation of Islamic law.

Clarke wonders whether this more pragmatic approach could be a "Trojan horse strategy" â a way for jihadist groups to gain power without giving up their longer-term goals.

"The big question is whether being in power actually moderates these groups, or whether they're simply changing their tactics to survive and strengthen their position," he says.

IS remains firmly committed to its global ambitions and rejects compromise. Al-Qaeda's Yemen branch, AQAP, continues to call for attacks against the US and Israel, and urges "lone wolf" attacks against Western and Jewish targets.

And it's too early to say whether groups like JNIM and al-Shabab will follow the Taliban or Syrian examples, and whether they're willing to make compromises.

Some jihadist groups may become more focused on local politics as they face the challenges of governing. Others may simply be hiding their wider ambitions until they're strong enough to pursue them.

The global jihadist dream may have failed, but jihadism is far from finished.

#22. India news: Resolve tariff concerns in trade talks — India tells US

India has denied US claims that New Delhi's policies promote excess manufacturing capacity. Further headlines from India include Constitution Day celebrations. DW has more.

Here are the latest developments from India on Thursday, November 26:

In its statement on planned new US tariffs, India's commerce ministry urged the US to resolve trade disputes and complete negotiations over a new trade deal.

The ministry said that it was "committed to working closely" with the US towards an "early conclusion" of the trade deal.

The US proposed a tariff rate of 25% on goods from India, reduced to 17% if a trade deal is reached.

US President Donald Trump and Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi first announced talks for the US-India Bilateral Trade Agreement (BTA) last year.

But negotiations were delayed over strained ties and global conflict, reestablishing headway only this year.

The two nations released the framework for an interim trade agreement in February. As part of that deal, India would "eliminate or reduce tariffs" on all US industrial products and a range of US food and agricultural goods.

However, the negotiations for a final deal were once again stalled after the US Supreme Court invalidated much of Trump's sweeping global tariffs.

In October, India's Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman described the talks as having reached "a plateau, beyond which giving or taking might be very, very difficult."

But US Secretary of State Marco Rubio said a few days later that New Delhi and Washington were "about asclose as you can be without having an executed agreement."

Facing a new United States tariff threat, India has stressed that any trade concerns be addressed within the framework of the ongoing trade deal talks between the two countries.

New Delhi's statement comes after Washington on Wednesday unveiled a new wave of tariffs on 14 of its major trade partners, including India, claiming that the economies were promoting excess industrial capacity.

"India and the United States are engaged in negotiations for a Bilateral Trade Agreement. It has been India's position that any trade-related concerns should be addressed within the framework of these negotiations," a statement from New Delhi's Ministry of Commerce and Industry said late Wednesday.

The Office of the US Trade Representative (USTR) announced new import duties of 20% to 40%  to counter the alleged excessive manufacturing abroad as it completed a monthslong probe into alleged unfair trade practices involving 16 trading partners.

The new tariffs will be subject to a period of public comment before taking effect.

"Today, after lengthy investigations, Ambassador Greer has determined that the acts, policies, and practices of various economies designed to artificially support manufacturing exports are unreasonable and burden or restrict US commerce and has proposed responsive action," the USTR said on Wednesday.

The USTR said that it will reduce the tariff rate for Indian imports to 17% if a trade deal is sealed between New Delhi and Washington in the long-drawn-out talks trade negotiations between the two sides.

India said that it had "engaged constructively" throughout USTR's investigation and said that it did not have "structural excess capacity in any sector."

This is Dharvi Vaid Dhulia from DW's bureau in New Delhi, back with our daily India news blog — where we break down all the biggest headlines that have grabbed the attention of the world's most populous democracy.

This Thursday marks 77 years since India officially adopted its Constitution. Prime Minister Narendra Modi has extended his greetings on the occasion and political pressure group Cockroach Janta Party has planned a "Save Democracy, Save Constitution" rally.

After the US proposed raising levies on 14 of its trading partners, including in India, over alleged excess industrial capacity, New Delhi has called for the trade concerns to be addressed through the ongoing talks of a trade deal with Washington. ​India has also rejected having structural overcapacity in any sector.

#20. Thirty-one killed in massive fire as fuel tanker crashes on Cairo highway

Huge fire erupts near Cairo killing at least 30 people

Thirty-one people have been killed and 21 others injured in a large fire on a busy highway in Cairo after a fuel tanker crashed, Egypt's health ministry says.

Hospitals nearby treated patients suffering burns and smoke inhalation after the incident, which occurred on a section of a ring road in the Warraq neighbourhood, to the north of the old city centre, on Wednesday evening.

Footage circulating on social media showed billowing flames and black smoke towering high into the night sky, with multiple nearby vehicles torched.

Officials have not given an account of how the fire broke out, but state-owned media reported the tanker had overturned, dousing nearby vehicles with petrol.

The regional public prosecutor has launched an investigation into the incident and asked that DNA samples be taken from bodies of the dead.

The tanker, which belonged to a private contractor, had been travelling along the highway when a high-voltage cable fell in front of it, prompting the driver to lose control, Al-Ahram reports, citing sources with knowledge of the investigation.

It then crossed the central reservation, overturned and caught fire, the state-owned newspaper says.

Egypt has suffered several deadly fires in recent years, with informal residential areas and often run-down infrastructure making incidents more common, the response to which is often complicated by slow emergency response times and locally improvised rescue efforts, even in the sprawling Egyptian capital.

Images from the scene show a large crowd gathered on the highway after the fire had been extinguished, as emergency services blocked off entry routes.

Images from the scene show a large crowd gathered on the highway after the fire had been extinguished

The tanker crossed the highway's central reservation before overturning and bursting into flames, local media report

The health ministry said 39 ambulances had been dispatched to respond to the fire, while it posted images of Egypt's health minister overseeing the treatment of the injured at one of Cairo's hospitals.

Road collisions are also common in Egypt, often attributed to lax maintenance and the widespread flouting of traffic laws.

Nearly 6,000 people died on Egypt's roads last year, official figures show, a rise of several hundred on the previous year's total.

Emergency responders have since cleared the highway and allowed it to reopen, state broadcaster NMA reports.

A bulldozer could earlier be seen at the scene clearing the burnt-out wrecks of cars.

#17. Images of asteroid targeted to test Earth's defences reveal stadium-sized crater

The asteroid before (left) and after, showing the crater left behind by a spacecraft's impact

The first images of an asteroid that Nasa deliberately crashed into have revealed a stadium-sized crater on its surface.

The extensive damage was caused when a Nasa spacecraft hit the asteroid Dimorphos in 2022. The asteroid posed no threat, but this was a test of our planetary defences to see if it was possible to knock a space rock off course.

Four years on, a European Space Agency (ESA) spacecraft called Hera is scouring the scene to fully assess the aftermath of the collision.

"The crater is almost as large as the body itself, which is really, really spectacular," said Dr Sabina Raducan, a planetary scientist, who is on the Hera science team.

"It's hard to describe in words all the excitement to actually finally see what happened. And as a crater person, I can also say it's beautiful."

The original 2022 crash test was carried out by a Nasa spacecraft called Dart which hurtled into Dimorphos at 14,000mph (22,000km/h).

It was such a big smash-up that the trail of debris, measuring about 6,200 miles long (10,000km), was spotted by telescopes on Earth and in space.

Dimorphos is about 150m (500ft) wide and was partly chosen as the target because it is one of an asteroid pair - it orbits around a larger space rock called Didymos, which is about 730m (2,400ft) wide.

The crash showed it was possible to change the path of the smaller asteroid.

Before the collision, it took Dimorphos 11 hours and 55 minutes to orbit around Didymos. After the crash, the duration of its orbit had shrunk by about 33 minutes.

Watch: Moment Nasa spacecraft leaves stadium-sized crater in asteroid

A key objective of the Hera mission is to measure the mass of Dimorphos, in order to work out how effective the spacecraft's shove was.

This information would be vital if we ever spotted a space rock that was on a collision course with Earth.

"It would help us to predict what would happen if we had to hit another asteroid," said Prof Simon Green, a planetary scientist who is on Hera's science team.

"We [could] measure its properties and say: 'Well, we need this mass of spacecraft, travelling at this speed, this early, in order to deflect it enough so that it misses the Earth'."

Hera's arrival on the scene is helping scientists answer many more questions too.

Not only did the collision alter the shape of Dimorphos, it also changed the way it moves.

"Before the Dart impact, Dimorphos was more like our own Moon - so it was always showing the same face to Didymos, and it was behaving quite nicely," explained Raducan.

"Now it's very, very chaotic - it's tumbling. Imagine a barrel that you throw down the stairs, and the barrel is rolling and it's moving, and basically that's what Dimorphos does right now around Didymos."

Hera has also taken detailed images of Didymos, revealing its surface in detail.

"The gravity at the surface of Didymos is about 40,000 times less than the gravity on Earth," said Naomi Murdoch, who is on the Hera mission team.

"We can already see some fascinating and complex geology. We can see regions of boulders.

"The smallest details that we can make out in these images are about 3m in size. So that means that all the boulders that you can see with your eyes are at least 3m big, and some as big as 10m. So what we're seeing here is boulders the size of cars and buses sitting on a world with almost no gravity."

The mission has sent back new pictures of the smaller Dimorphos orbiting around Didymos

The Hera mission will spend the next six months studying Dimorphos and Didymos, sending back more images taken from closer up in higher resolution.

"Hera is a sort of Inspector Columbo going back to the crash scene to investigate what is the outcome of this impact," said Patrick Michel, principal investigator of ESA's Hera mission.

The spacecraft is packed with instruments and also has two shoebox-sized satellites on board, which can study the impact site up close and later land on the asteroid.

But the first images taken by Hera have also revealed a new challenge. Dimorphos and Didymos are travelling through space surrounded by hundreds of boulders ranging in size from tens of centimetres to metre-size rocks.

This will make it especially challenging to operate the spacecraft and cube satellites through this boulder field.

A long trail of debris from the collision was spotted by the Hubble Space Telescope

This was the last image from Dart before it crashed into Dimorphos in 2022

Asteroids pose a significant threat to our planet.

The biggest, which measures more than a kilometre across, could cause global devastation. But medium-sized asteroids around the same dimensions as Dimorphos could wipe out a city.

"We all know what happened to the dinosaurs, right? And we don't want that to happen to us," said Carolyn Ernst at Johns Hopkins Applied Physics Laboratory, who has worked on both the Dart and Hera missions.

"The good news for us is that there are far fewer potential threats out there in the Solar System than there were millions of years ago. But there are objects out there."

She said the biggest asteroids have been spotted, and none are heading to Earth.

"The smallest stuff isn't too much of a threat because our atmosphere protects us," she added.

"But there's a size range in the middle where we don't know where all of them are, and it could, if impacted, cause problems for humanity. And if we can find out about that and know about that, well, we sure as heck would want to try to stop that."

Artist's impression showing Hera. It has two small satellites on board to study the asteroid up close

Scientists want to gather as much intelligence as they can about space rocks to make us as well equipped as possible to deal with whatever the Universe throws at us.

A new mission is launching in 2027 to track potentially hazardous asteroids - a space telescope called the Near-Earth Object (NEO) Surveyor.

There are also more visits planned to asteroids, including to one called Apophis, which is about 450m (1,480ft) long.

Apophis was discovered in 2004 and it was initially thought it might hit the Earth as early as 13 April 2029.

But now scientists understand its orbit better, and say it won't hit us for at least the next 100 years. Instead of a crash in 2029, it will fly just 32,000km (20,000 miles) above our surface.

This will give scientists the rare chance to study a space rock up-close.

Two spacecraft will head to Apophis, including Nasa's Osiris-Apex (which previously grabbed a sample from another asteroid called Bennu) and Ramses, a joint mission between ESA and the Japanese Space Agency (JAXA).

Nasa's Dart spacecraft 'changed path of asteroid'

Spacecraft launches towards asteroid knocked off course by Nasa

#11. US announces new tariffs on host of countries including China

Watch: What we know about Trump's Thanksgiving tariffs

The US has announced a fresh raft of tariffs as President Donald Trump pushes ahead with his signature trade policy.

The new taxes charged on imports to America are set to hit many of the country's biggest trading partners - China, the European Union and 12 other jurisdictions, including Japan, South Korea and Taiwan.

The White House began investigating the trade practices of other countries in March after the US Supreme Court ruled Trump's previously announced Liberation Day tariffs were illegal.

China is facing the highest tariff rate of up to 40%, followed by Vietnam at up to 35%, with others facing either 20% or 25%.

The US Trade Representative (USTR) announced the proposed tariffs together with extensive reports of how each jurisdiction's trade practices purportedly impact American commerce.

The new duties are not expected to take effect until next year and have to go through rounds of public consultation and, potentially, hearings.

Tariffs are taxes levied on imported goods. The amount is typically calculated as a percentage of a good's value and paid to the government by businesses importing products.

Some of these rates will be effectively reduced because of caps on tariffs Trump has agreed to. Individual countries also face different levies.

The proposed tariffs will affect goods from China, the European Union, Singapore, Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia, Cambodia, Thailand, Korea, Vietnam, Taiwan, Bangladesh, Japan, and India.

The USTR's Jamieson Greer said the latest tariffs issued would "level the playing field for American workers and chart a new, more sustainable course for the world economy".

He said that many of America's trading partners had "for decades" adopted industrial strategies which included "targeting manufacturing, wage and labor suppression".

They also employ "other market distorting practices that tilt the trade balance in their favour while eroding America's manufacturing base", Greer said.

But economists have warned tariffs can fuel inflation and push prices up for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs, as well as dampen global trade.

Trump has repeatedly threatened or imposed tariffs during his second term, arguing they will boost US manufacturing and create jobs.

Despite a poor result for the Republican party in the midterm elections, the president has continued to press on with his flagship trade policy.

Trump is also seeking to cut the US trade deficit - the gap between the value of goods it buys from other countries and those it sells to them.

Trump's so-called Liberation Day tariffs were struck down by the Supreme Court earlier this year

These new levies are build on a "stronger legal foundation" under US law compared with Trump's April 2025 Liberation Day tariffs, Singapore Management University economics lecturer Chang Pao-Li said.

Trump previously invoked emergency presidential powers to impose tariffs that have since been struck down, while his latest approach relies on the USTR's authority to impose tariffs to address certain foreign trade practices, she said.

The levies are still likely to face legal challenges, but there has been a "historical precedent" for such tariffs remaining in force, Chang said.

Many tariffs imposed under Section 301 - a law that allows the US to probe and retaliate against foreign trade practices - during Trump's first term remained in effect during his successor Joe Biden's administration.

However, Stephen Brown, chief North America economist for Capital Economics, said the latest tariff announcement was "not as alarming as it looks", due to the taxes "essentially replacing" previously announced tariffs made in the summer.

"The increase in the effective tariff rate looks set to be a touch lower than we've been assuming for our forecasts, although the new tariffs will still provide a marginal boost to prices," he said in a note.

The additional tariff burden is likely to be small because a limited number of goods are subject to the new duties, Brown added.

Jurisdictions affected by the new wave of tariffs have voiced their disapproval over the Trump administration's move.

Washington has also proposed a 10% tariff on goods from Brazil, as part of a separate investigation over what it says are "harmful trade practices" undercutting American workers.

Brazilian authorities expressed their "outrage" at the announcement, saying that the move is aimed at pressuring the government to change regulations around issues like digital trade.

The South American nation faces duties on finished aircraft and sugar sold to the US.

"Brazil will not yield to such threats," President Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva's office said.

The European Commission rejected the new US tariffs, saying it would investigate the reasons for bringing them in. It pointed out that tariffs are limited to the 15% previously agreed.

The latest tariff rate is "not consistent" with the reality of bilateral economic-trade cooperation between the US and Vietnam, Vietnamese Deputy Prime Minister Bui Thanh Son said.

The Japanese government said the US tariffs were "regrettable", while the Singapore government said it disagrees with the White House's findings and that it is engaging the USTR to explore ways to reduce the tariff.

South Korea's trade minister said the proposed 15% tariff will be "more than a decent outcome" as it would fall in line with a previously agreed limit with Washington.

Last year, Seoul pledged $350bn (Â£267bn) in US investments in exchange for lower tariffs as part of a trade deal.

Trump tariffs on Asia: Which economies were hit hardest?

China's growth accelerates as AI boom helps boost exports

US trading partners have been "waiting for this shoe to drop" and have argued that they are being wrongly accused of producing too much, said the Asia Society Policy Institute's Wendy Cutler, a former senior US trade negotiator.

"Many believe that China, not them, should be the target for US action," she added.

Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun described the new tariffs as a form of "political manipulation", describing as "false" the US claim that China produces more than it can consume.

The tariff announcement is likely to increase tensions between the US and China, the world's two biggest economies, who remain locked in a trade dispute and the AI race.

The two countries have also been embroiled in a tit-for-tat tariffs war, which is currently on hold.

Despite last week's meeting between Trump and China's President Xi Jinping, the two sides made no announcement on the trade war truce, which is set to expire on 10 January.

Beijing has remained vague on how it will respond but for now seems "prepared to live with the new rates provided they don't exceed 20%", Cutler said.

Additional reporting by Leehyun Choi in Seoul

#5. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.

The Manhattan district attorney’s office has been investigating Trump’s alleged role in a hush money payment scheme and cover-up involving adult film star Stormy Daniels that dates to the 2016 presidential campaign.

Trump’s attorney said he plans to file “substantial” legal challenges. The former president has repeatedly denied wrongdoing in the matter and accused Democrats of targeting him politically.

See more photos of the days leading up to Trump’s indictment:

A small group of supporters chanted as former President Donald Trump arrived at his namesake tower on Monday afternoon, with a handful of people waving banners with messages like “Trump won” and “Arrest Biden.”

While Trump couldn’t see his admirers as he entered a side door on 56th Street, his arrival came with more fanfare and security than at any other time in his post presidency.

Now that he is inside the building — where he launched his presidential bid nearly eight years ago — Trump is expected to meet with lawyers and political advisers on the eve of his arraignment, an aide told CNN.

Trump’s mood was described by an adviser as “defiant and focused,” as he watched expansive television coverage while flying back to his native New York City from Florida.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Witnesses are being questioned about what they saw in Trump’s private residential and work areas within the club, some of the sources said.

Former President Donald Trump has arrived at his midtown Manhattan property Trump Tower.

He will stay at Trump Tower tonight and is expected to depart New York immediately after Tuesday’s arraignment to head back to Florida — where he plans to deliver remarks at his Mar-a-lago home.

President Joe Biden told CNN’s Betsy Klein he isn’t worried about unrest due to former President Donald Trump’s criminal indictment in New York.

“No, I have faith in the New York Police Department,” Biden said on Monday during a tour of a power generation facility in Fridley, Minnesota.

Asked if he had faith in the legal system, Biden replied: “Yes.”

As former President Donald Trump walked down from his plane at La Guardia Airport on the eve of his arraignment, CNN’s Gloria Borger noted the unique situation he’s in.

Trump is “one of the most famous people in the world, and he is a former president, presidential candidate, and now he’s a defendant. We haven’t seen that. It’s hard to kind of process that,” Borger said to CNN’s Dana Bash.

Borger also highlighted how Trump’s day in court is something he’s avoided for decades and that it will be a grievance he uses to rile up his supporters.

“Donald Trump is somebody who’s probably tried to avoid his day in court for a very long time — decades. And now, you know, you have this man who is saying to people who support him, ‘You know I am your retribution.’ And he is going to make his grievance their grievance. And I think we’re going to see that play out over and over again, and we’ll see it play out in court with his attorney,” she said.

CNN’s Audie Cornish noted how Trump ending up in court seemed unavoidable given the “sheer number of investigations against him.”

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan said he’ll likely issue a decision tonight on whether media outlets can broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

CNN is part of the media coalition that asked the judge for permission to broadcast the hearing.

More on this: Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

The Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request and said that it was deferring to the judge to make a decision on how best to manage his courtroom.

Donald Trump — the first former president in history to face criminal charges — has arrived in New York for an expected arraignment on Tuesday after being indicted last week by a Manhattan grand jury.

The former president will stay at Trump Tower tonight, according to a source familiar with his plans. But even before Trump’s appearance, his presence will be felt in the Manhattan courthouse Tuesday, as all trials and most other court activity is being halted before he is slated to arrive.

CNN’s John Miller, Jeremy Herb, Katelyn Polantz, Tierney Sneed, Sydney Kashiwagi, Kristen Holmes, Holmes Lybrand, Hannah Rabinowitz, Paula Reid, Alayna Treene, Gregory Clary and Devan Cole contributed reporting to this post.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

The former president touched down shortly after 3:25 p.m. ET.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, CNN has reported, but the indictment remains under seal. He is the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history.

He will spend Monday night at the Trump Tower, according to a source familiar with his plans.

The Secret Service is scheduled to accompany Trump in the early afternoon Tuesday to the district attorney’s office, which is in the same building as the courthouse, where he is expected to be arraigned.

The former president is expected to return to Florida shortly after his court appearance and has scheduled an event that evening to speak publicly.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

Former President Donald Trump has hired a new attorney to serve as lead counsel in his defense against charges brought by the Manhattan district attorney’s office, multiple sources tell CNN.

Trump has hired Todd Blanche, who was most recently a partner at law firm Cadwalader, Wickersham & Taft. Blanche has previously represented Trump campaign manager Paul Manafort and Igor Fruman, a former associate of Rudy Giuliani who was also a key figure in Trump’s first impeachment trial. Politico was first to report the news of Blanche’s hiring.

Trump attorney’s Joe Tacopina and Susan Necheles will remain on Trump’s legal team.

Blanche’s hiring has been seen by some inside Trump’s orbit as a sidelining of Tacopina, who up until this point has been one of the most forward-facing attorney’s dealing with Trump’s defense. However, a senior adviser to Trump pushed back on that characterization and said the addition of Blanche was intended to add more legal firepower to Trump’s defense.

When pressed by CNN’s Kaitlan Collins on Friday if Tacopina was the right lawyer in New York to take Trump to trial, another lawyer representing the former president, Tim Parlatore, pointed to a potential conflict of interest.

“I know that Joe has certain potential conflict issues, given his prior contacts, with Stormy Daniels. So, who’s the right attorney, to take it to trial, is something that the client will have to decide. Ultimately, the decision of who to stand next to, before a jury, is a decision that only the client can make,” Parlatore said.

Asked by CNN’s Paula Reid if he is still on the case and whether he will be in court tomorrow, Tacopina said: “Yes of course ! And I will be the lead trial lawyer when the bell rings!”

CNN’s Paula Reid contributed reporting to this post.

Senate Homeland Security Chairman Gary Peters said he has not seen any indication that protests in Washington, DC, over former President Donald Trump’s indictment will become dangerous.

“I think you’re always going to be vigilant, so folks will be vigilant. Security and local law enforcement are certainly monitoring it,” he told reporters Monday.

Peters, who is also a member of Senate Democrat leadership, said it is unclear what effect the indictment will have on the Senate. “We don’t know what’s gonna transpire tomorrow, let alone what’s going to happen in the few weeks and months. I think we’d have to take a look at what the indictment is all about.”

Former President Donald Trump has departed Florida and is en route to New York to face criminal charges.

His personalized Boeing 757 took off from the Palm Beach International Airport this afternoon at about 12:59 p.m. ET ahead of his Tuesday appearance in a Manhattan courtroom to be arraigned.

Trump, the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history, is expected to land at New York’s LaGuardia airport around 3 p.m. ET, according to a source familiar with his plans. He will stay at Trump Tower tonight, the source told CNN.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, according to two sources familiar with the case. A New York grand jury handed down the sealed indictment late last week. The charges have yet to be made public.

The former president is not expected to stay in New York City for very long. Immediately after his Tuesday court appearance, Trump plans to return to Florida, the source said, where he has scheduled an event to speak publicly.
